And a sixth refusal arrives on paper in Harrow's own hand: a board may not maintain a register, has no fund, no date and one seat standing empty. **He works out in the empty yard that the register can only be a room people come to, paid for out of a levy agreed by a cooperative that counted hands in a shed and a ward that cannot be assessed a rate at all, by a person neither of them can reach** — and that the only body which has already done it correctly is the yard that has just refused him.

### Chapter 82 — The Twentieth of the Rains
The **ninth day of the harvest month**, and a sheet is on the Gate tank with the yard's four rules in type and **nothing above them and nothing at the foot of it but a place and a day: *Tallow Ward. Gate yard. The twentieth day of the rains month.*** Under the four rules the empty box is printed — *an independent witness signs, not paid out of the district account, not living in the yard* — and printing the box is the loudest thing anybody has done in Tallow Ward in four months. **Nobody can be traced who printed it, and the yard decides not to try, on purpose**: a hundred and forty people in the ward have lifted a working sheet off that nail in four months, and the day a yard starts naming the hands on its own sheets is the day the sheets are a thing a yard keeps, and a thing a yard keeps can be produced at it. **Aldis Kern says where it came from, once:** the twentieth of the rains month, this tank, four lines on the back of a complaint sheet off a trestle table, and the yard will not say who held the pen. He refuses Marek gratitude in advance — *you did not write it, you are not on it, and if any office prints a rule with your name at the foot of it we will nail the twentieth of the rains month up beside it the same day* — and he admits he thought of it on the sixth and did not do it, because he is frightened of a printer. The yard grants one thing on three conditions: the four lines are copied in its words with its date and nothing else; **the empty witness box stays empty, and Droll gives a new and worse reason for it** — a levy is agreed by two bodies and an independent witness has to be neither of them, so the old reason was humiliating and true and the new one is decent and is a rate, and she voted for the worse reason; and **the yard's second person at the register is a person of the yard and is to be asked the same four questions like anybody else.** That person is Sanne Droll, and she says the cost in her own words: she does not sell rope on a Tuesday and has not since the rains, four days of a wage went into the second round and no total is ever written down, and she is going to be asked about her own household and there is nothing she can say in that yard that changes it. The yard votes at the sixth bell, writes nothing, and hangs the conditions on the nail under the new sheet. **THEN THE STANDING IS GRANTED, AND THIS IS THE REPAIR.** Kern says the thing that is not a condition, from the step, in about a hundred and forty people's hearing, and Lask reads it out twice, and **the yard does not vote on it either time**: *there is a man standing at the end of this yard, he has been in it five weeks and he has not written one word of any of it down and he has not asked one of you a single question* — **the register is read out by Marek Vale, four hours a day on whatever days it opens, in the words the person gave it in, and he stops when somebody says *that is not what I said*, and if he does not stop he is a man in a good coat and not a reader, and anybody in the yard is to say so to his face and in front of other people; and he cannot enter one word of it, and that is the form and not a punishment we have done to him.** It is not a condition, not a favour, and **there is no paper of it anywhere**; one man says *you cannot give a man a standing* and Kern says the yard can give him the four hours and the road and the words and nobody in this city can take one of the three off him. **Marek says thank you and Sanne Droll says *do not*, and nobody thanks anybody, which the chapter calls the fourth condition and which is not a condition.** **The volume's hinge used to happen off the page: Ch 84's posting clerk spoke conditionally (*if the yard has given you a standing*), Ch 88's Harrow asserted it, Ch 90 asserted it, and every state file dated it to this chapter — and the chapter did not grant it. It is now on the page and the whole hinge is standing on the floor.** **Nobody is thanked, and Marek is not missed.** The chapter ends in the lane: *there is a register now.* *Is it going to be a room like Merrow's.* *How would he know.* *Because Merrow's had a licence over the door and this one had a tank.*

### Chapter 83 — One Price
The **twelfth of the harvest month**, from the second bell on **Oren Pike's counting floor** — four numbers, nine seconds, four seconds of silence, ninety people not shouting — and a man with no office watches a price set four times and could not have done any of it. Pike has waited for this visit since the fourth. **The price for reading is a market decision and he has counted it**: two a reading on his floor for eleven years, sixty to ninety a week, and the split has not moved in four years — two thirds houses that can afford eight and one third people who are not houses and have never paid, some of whom put a coin in a drawer he would not show anybody. **A market in which nobody can read the paper is a market with one price**, and that is arithmetic: a claim that cannot be read is a claim that cannot be told apart from any other, so the low ground's flood quarters, a district's dead rate and a woman's eleven trays of sand are the same object to a buyer, and the price is set by the people who can least afford to be wrong. He then does the thing the chapter exists for: **his house will be the first entry in the register**, all of it, buys and holds and every line against the low ground, nobody asked — and the reason is stated without shame and recorded in his own words: once his paper can be told apart, risk gets different prices and the difference is worth more to him than anything offered this season, **he is going to make money out of the register, and his margin is the number he will not invent in front of eleven men.** He prices the levy instead: a market pays two, a levy carries about four a week, and **the hundred and forty is not got out of the four** — *four a week and a hundred and forty a season do not agree with each other and I did not get the second one out of the first* — **so he gets it the way a factor gets anything, from what a household on the low ground can carry for a room it does not use without being asked a second question, a little over two pounds, which he has off a floor for nineteen years, put against sixty-one lines**, and **he will stand behind one hundred and forty and not a penny more either way.** This is the corrected derivation: the old text had *a season comes to between one hundred and forty and one hundred and sixty*, which is four a week over thirty-five weeks and cannot be squared with the twelve-week season of Ch 77 — a little over two pounds a head, a day of a carter at his gate and the rope Droll did not sell on a Tuesday twice over, out of households that have already had a rate put on their ground out of a piece of sack. He has not got a number for what a room is worth, and says so. **Neris Kade**, in the room and not of it, cannot vote and is not thanked, says the register should be necessary, that a register only houses can read is a schedule, and that being right is worth nothing and the levy will be collected by somebody in a yard. **Pike refuses her the satisfaction in one sentence: *do not put that in your book as the Fund's woman being right. I have not got a number.*** Then **Josip Malnar**, who came for barrow boards and did not come for any of this, says what his four hundred and ten pounds is — a daughter's wedding money, for a piece of a thing, at a discount, off an honest man with a table and a stool in a yard — and asks that when they say the number of people who will bear the failure, somebody say **the district is not me**, and Marek promises to say it in a room, in Malnar's words, and be thanked by nobody.

### Chapter 84 — Who May Write In It
Two days. **The fourteenth** at the Gate tank: four hours a day, nothing charged, and nobody asked to sign anything to come in are agreed in forty minutes, and then **Marta Brel**, forty-six, cannot read and cannot write and has been at a water office counter twice this year with her husband, who stands at the counter and says the figure out loud for her in about four seconds, and who works the north road and is away for weeks at a time, **and there is nobody else**, says what the register must do about a person who cannot write her own answer down. **The husband is alive and this is the corrected reading: the sentence used to have him at the counter in the present tense and dead since the flood year in the same breath, and Ch 76 has the form filled in his own hand with eleven days of his labour as the settlement, so he cannot have been dead. He is alive, and he is an insufficient answer, which is the chapter's point.** **Kern** gives the yard's own rule back: a second person may come into the room, is named at the counter, does not get a hand on anything, and if that person is lying the reader cannot find out and never could — and she does it the way she did it at the trestle, saying it while somebody holds the charcoal. **Sedge** is in the yard with a blank sheet and a carpenter's pencil, cutting as people talk, and his four rulings are deliberate: **lines, not boxes**, because a box is a thing a person can be put in and every bad instrument anybody put in front of Marek this summer had a box with a word at the top chosen by whoever cut the form; the heading is to be **the tank and the date**, because the yard told him in writing that no name may go at the foot, and because he has copied the yard's four lines and will not pretend to a person in a room in town that he thought of them, and he wants that in his own hand. And then the question that stops the yard, asked in four seconds: the second line is *what the person behind it said*, and he chose the word **person** himself in nine seconds and is not sure of it. **The fifteenth** in the seed house, thirty people, sixty-one trays along the wall: anybody may enter a line about their own paper and **no person may enter a line about anybody else's** — not a factor, a house, an office, a man with no office, not Sedge, and he says he wants to and cannot. **Bessok Tan** finds the word the Share has been not-saying for four years: a member is a **household**, and Wenna Drel is on the roll because her mother is. **Fisk** puts *the person* in and leaves it empty where it is empty, because the yard's minute says the sign of a wrong answer is the same in both kinds of record. Two more lines are cut: **hours**, and **the cost, which is not money** — the days a person loses by standing in a room, which Sedge writes as *days* on the sixth line and cannot enter, and he predicts it will be the biggest hole in the thing. He puts the sheet on the bench in front of a keeper, says a form has to be cut by a man who is not sure it is right or there has never been one, and goes up the hill. **Neris Kade refuses to hold a sheet** in front of about a hundred and forty people, for the same reason as the yard's rounds — a column with an examiner's name over it becomes a thing an office says the examiner agreed to — and nobody thanks her, and Marek does not thank her either, and understands why. Then a short scene at the top of the causeway with **the posting desk's duty clerk**, sixty and twenty-two years in the copy room: *reading is not entering, and the register's page is not the Trust's page, and a man with no office may enter nothing, and none of that has changed because a yard printed four lines on a sheet* — and the second half, which nobody had thought of: if Marek reads it out, in a year somebody will ask him whether he checked the figures, and he will have to say no, and they will be right not to like it, **and there is nobody at that counter who checks except a yard's second person who is not a reader and not a keeper, whom she has never met and would like somebody to tell about.**

### Chapter 85 — A Mark in a Calendar
**The seventeenth day of the harvest month** at the second bell, in **two rooms over a sailmaker's shop on the Bell Weir road** with a lease charged in Crown March money. **Halvard Sunn**, forty-four, four years in this city, has been refusing this since the fourth and says so before anybody sits down. He explains what a Tide Reckoning mark is — a month in another calendar, a tide phase, a harbor seal, a translator — and that **it is not a warranty about a figure**, so he will not stand behind the sums in a register, and a mark in the middle of a list that keeps adding to itself is a sentence that cannot be true. **The cost, which he gives in his own rooms because Marek came up a stair instead of sending a boy:** the register cannot leave the basin and cannot leave the next street, and in about a month a house at home will ask whether Morrowfen paper is being read outside Morrowfen, and he will have to write *I do not know*, and a notary who writes that is a notary they will not need in a year, and he has two rooms on a lease and a press that is not his and no second office in this city. He says the man who could have made the register travelable has told him **in the fourth and last year of a four-year post** that he will not — **which is the corrected figure and agrees with the four years in this city and with the four years a house at home has been paying him; the chapter previously said second year and could not be reconciled with either** — and asks that it be recorded that he did not enjoy it and is not going to be grateful to Marek for understanding it in nine seconds. **Then Petar Duss**, the drayman whose bed and gear were taken off him at the sixth bell in the growth month under the fifth box of a factor's form and who has never asked about the four bells, comes up the outside stair with a Red Uplands grain contract worth four shillings to counterseal.** Sunn does it, and Marek sees the hand — the same hand, on a third instrument, on a haulier's paper for barley off the second terrace that has nothing whatever to do with the Share, the low ground, the flood quarters or the fourth clause. **He writes nothing down and tells nobody, on purpose, for the second time, because two people in this city would have carried it into a room inside the hour and neither could have said which of the three it was about.** Sunn notices the not-writing and asks for one thing before the hand: a notary's standing is a list at a harbour, and the list would say only *yes* — and then he says, in a different voice, **that he does not know what the marks are, and wants it said in that room that day by him before either of them asks the other, and that he has not known for nine years, and that the reason he has stopped asking is that he makes a very good living out of a mark nobody in this basin can check.** Nobody thanks him and he does not ask to be thanked. **A harbor notary is not the Saltglass Harbor Council and the Council is not named.**

### Chapter 86 — The One They Cannot Enter
**The nineteenth day of the harvest month.** The yard is told the evening before; **Milan Vray** objects that a single thing sent once to a building is the same action as a schedule with a nicer coat on, asks that the yard minute that it *knew and did it anyway*, and Droll puts it in and gives the words and not her hand. The yard then votes six minutes to enter **its own refusal** in the register — it is the only document in the basin nobody can be shown to be the seller of, and a register that cannot hold it has a hole the shape of the best thing here — and carries it without a count being written anywhere. They do it properly, in the open, on a board on two upturned buckets, and it fails in ninety seconds. **Sedge** has to ask who the person behind the refusal is, and the person behind it is a yard. **A yard does not answer, not out of rudeness:** the yard's own first rule, *ask the household, not the yard*, means a yard cannot be asked; a name is required and a ward is not one; a keeper's signature would put a man's name on the only untraceable paper in the basin and **Droll refuses that in four words and the yard believes her**; and an entry with nothing at the head of it is the piece of sack in Fisk's house, which **Marta Brel** — who has come down off the causeway a second time to find out about her own line and is sent away for a rule about a yard — names as worse in a shed than in a house. **Neris Kade declines in four words to be the one who explains it to him**, because that is exactly why she can and because she has already said the same shape of sentence in a Crown March record this year and cannot take it out, and she will not put it in a yard as well; nobody thanks her, and Marek does not, and the two of them do not refer to it again. The entry is abandoned at the fifth bell, a line is ruled through the first of six lines which is not a cancellation and means *asked once, and the answer stands*, and **Kern says out loud, from the step, in front of about eighty people, that the rule that stopped them is their own, written at this tank on the twentieth of the rains month, and that it has cost them the only document in the basin that cannot be traced, and asks for it on the nail next to the refusal, read out twice — **and claims the sentence and not the hand, which is the corrected reading and the only one consistent with Ch 82: *I have said it out loud at this tank twice this summer and I am not going to stand in front of eighty people and be found out having found it. I do not know who held the pen, this yard does not know who held the pen, and we are not going to find out, and that is the only reason the sentence is any good at all.*** On the causeway at the sixth bell, in the dark, Kade tells Marek not to put it in a room: the rule did not stop him, the room stopped him, and the room had a form in it with one word in the second line that a clerk cut in nine seconds and said he was not sure of. **That is the batch's fourth and smallest Tuesday, and nothing warmer happened in it, and neither of them refers to the mill.** The register is going to live with a hole in it, and the hole is the shape of the yard's own best paper.

### Chapter 87 — In Parts
**The twenty-first day of the harvest month**, from the second bell, **the Gate shed, eleven feet by nine and built for a seed bin**, a counter of two trestles and a board, sixty people in the lane, and the whole contents of the register is one entry. **Nobody is paid**: the levy will not be agreed for three more days. **Oren Pike's house goes in first** and he insists on it for a market reason and gives it without embarrassment: *I go first because I am the one who can afford to be first* — anybody can be second in a room where the first has made a fool of himself. It goes in as its parts, read in the voice of a man reading a price list, and the second person asks the three questions and he answers that the words are his clerk's and he has told the clerk to stand there while he reads it and the clerk is a man of twenty-six who can afford to be a person behind a line and he would not. **Ondra Kelb** counts it twice, once down the columns and once from the back in a different order, with his left hand, and finds that it balances and that nobody has done anything wrong, and says the sentence he said in the seed month and has been ashamed of ever since — **and says that the relief is worse the second time than the first, and why: it is not mine, he is not a party to a thing in a shed in Tallow Ward** — and asks for a column to put that in and Sedge says there is no column and he is not cutting a seventh, because a seventh is a column and a column gets a name and a name gets a heading. Then **a man of about fifty with a bad cough comes in because a rope-maker in his lane said there was a room where you could find out, and is told his own name has been read to him in his own doorway, and when the second person asks him the second question he says: that is my name, I did not sign anything, my wife signed it, she is at the water tank twice a day and is fifty-one and cannot get up the causeway in this cold.** Sedge cannot fill the form: an empty second line is the piece of sack, and a line with a man's name and the man's words is a line the man's wife has never been asked about. **Marta Brel supplies the answer from the step of the shed** — the old rule, a second person may come and is named at the door and does not get a hand on anything, the second line takes his words, the fourth line is for anything anybody else has to say, and the two are printed one above the other and are not joined — and Sedge records that the form is not the whole of the thing and that the man who cut it does not know all of what it is. **Then the yard's second person is asked her own four questions**, because the yard said in a yard that she would be, and she answers in her own words, and the fourth answer is about the days and there is no box for them, and she adds that she has been careful about the hands on this and that there is not one man from a hall in the room and she has not been careful about her own household's days, which are eleven, and her daughter is fifteen and is on no roll. Nobody thanked her. **Pike stands in the lane afterwards, notices nine people did not look in, and says plainly that being asked her own four questions at a counter in front of forty people is the best thing anybody has done in the basin this summer and will cost the room about a fifth of its people inside a month, and that he has no idea which nine and neither does the room.** The register ends the day with one line in it.

### Chapter 88 — A Dissent, Written Out
**The twenty-fourth day of the harvest month**, the second-floor hearing room built for thirty, nine in it, the same table of five with the Trust's seat empty for the fourth time and *in the ordinary course* still in quotation marks, Harrow serving the minutes in his own hand with no clerk, and **Neris Kade in the second seat with a sheet whose only function is to hold four figures in the order they are called out, and the sheet is blank because nothing is called out** — the matter before the table is not a figure but a word. The four terms are confirmed in eleven minutes. Harrow states what the room has: a shed open four hours a day, one entry, a factor's own book, a boy of nineteen and a rope-maker unpaid, and **a man with no office who can read out and cannot enter a line**, and that no office will hold it and the Trust will not fill a chair. Pike's figures come in on an unsigned sheet: a market pays two a reading, **counted on his counting floor on the twelfth of the month in front of about ninety people and not in a lane**; a levy carries about four a week, which is Signe Alve's wage and which Pike said he would not pay his own daughter less than; **and the room is told that a season of that is *not* a hundred and forty** — the four a week and the hundred and forty do not agree and he did not get one out of the other — **so the hundred and forty comes the other way round, off what sixty-one households can carry without being asked a second question, and one hundred and forty on sixty-one households is a little over two pounds each, and the two pounds is where it started** — and Kern, who can weigh it at the tank, says two pounds is what a household of four eats in a week at the Gate, and says it should be said by somebody who has stood at that tank and not by a man in a good coat. **Kern's objection, in the first person, in four sentences:** a ward cannot be assessed a rate at all, so the Share would carry the whole of a levy for a thing the ward uses and cannot pay for and is not a party to, and take the names off that and it is the offer that came to his tank in the seed month. Harrow gives two of the three ways it differs — the levy is capped and a member who cannot pay is not charged and not entered, and it is paid into a room that is in the open and has no office behind it and cannot be produced at anybody — and says the third he has not got and would rather do without than invent at a table. **Then Zorka Vendl takes the dissent at the end, on purpose, so the room will have been in the middle of something else, and asks for it to be taken under term four and used about money for the first time.** Her grounds, in her own words, are a definition and not a complaint: **a levy is a figure a body puts on itself, in a room, on a day, in the words of the body, without an office, without a seal and without anybody able to produce it afterwards against anybody — and the first rule of it is that a member who cannot pay in money may pay in kind, and the second is that a member who has nothing pays nothing and is not entered, and both of those are caps, and a cap is the only part of a levy anybody in this basin can recognise as one, because a rate has never had one.** It is read back, she corrects one word, Harrow writes it in full with her name at the foot, and the finding goes on a nail in a yard — **the first definition of a levy anybody in this city can produce, and nothing in the room paid for it.** Harrow then does the last thing: he records Marek's standing to read the register out, in his own hand, in a minute of a board of five, and says why he will do it without standing — a duty given by a yard, no office granting it, no office able to take it away, not in the finding, because a Crown March representative has no business granting standing to a man who has none, **and it is the only thing in the building today that nobody can be told they got from them.** Kade speaks twice, both times from a seat that is not a membership, neither time a motion: the levy will be collected by two people of the Share at a trestle in a yard and nobody in the room has standing to stop it and being right about that is worth nothing — and **the cap will not be enough, because a member who pays in kind pays in seed, and a member who has nothing pays nothing and is not entered, and a member who is not entered is a member who has not been asked, and the room has just written down a rule that produces a person who does not exist, and somebody in a month should look for her, and she cannot do it and has already said the same thing in a Crown March record this year and it cannot be taken out.** Marek says nothing and does not look at her. **The Trust's seat is minuted empty for the fourth time and there is still no date for anything**, and the paragraph about the levy is the second of five with a name at the foot of it that is not an office, and the paragraph about a man with no office reading a register out in Tallow Ward is the last of five and has no name at the foot of it at all.

### Chapter 89 — How Many People
**The twenty-sixth day of the harvest month**, from the fourth bell, **the shed on the low-ground road lane**, **open since the twenty-second of the seed month — no day count is given and none may be, because the lengths of the months between the seed month and the harvest month are fixed nowhere in the manuscript** — Signe Alve in the chair at four a week, **fifty-two lines and then eleven more**, the second hand, the sixth column up for the fifty-two letters since the nineteenth of the growth month, and the seventh still bare at the head of it. A third of the queue is now people who have heard about a shed at the Tallow Gate. **Merrow stands outside his own door for four minutes before going in, which is not a thing a man does who is about to be helpful.** **A boy of about nine carries a four-word message up to the Gate at the second bell, paid a penny, in a factor's hand: *come down and stand there*; Marek reads it once, writes nothing on the back of it, comes down the low-ground road on foot in the rain at the fifth bell and tells nobody why, which is a thing a man with no office can do and one of about four things he can still do.** **Tam Rell, fourteen, a queue-holder for a shilling a morning out of a tin, four sisters and a father who has not been there since the flood year, comes in on his own account and tells Merrow he was asked and asks three things.** *How much do you get out of it* — three hundred against a seed cost of eleven hundred and a plough at forty, about nine hundred of a dead district's rate cleared this month, and **a year's figure he will not give because the year is not finished and a man who says a year's figure in the middle of a year is doing a thing he would not do in a room with a boy in it.** *Did you know what it was* — no, and the price of not knowing is about **a hundred and sixty a year** for a Crown March clerk who reads a schedule and tells him what a thing is, whose name he does not know, **and he has never said it out loud in a yard because a sentence is free and a figure is not.** *How many people signed it* — **there is not a number, and there has not been for nine years, and he has two he will stand behind and neither is it: fifty-two lines, and thirty-eight answered and fourteen did not out of fifty-two** — and then the thing that is the chapter, said to the bench and not to the boy, with about nine people at the door: **a line is a household, not a person; fifty-two households is not fifty-two people and never has been; the roll is households and the instrument was carried on forty hands up and one down out of forty-one in a shed, and forty of forty-one is not a majority and nobody has ever said it was; and he has been selling lines for nine years and calling them claims, and has never once been able to tell you how many people are inside a claim of his.** Then *is yours better than that one* — **no, without difficulty:** the Gate shed has been open five days — **it opened on the twenty-first, and the chapter previously said two days and one line, which the chapter's own eighth-day arithmetic in Ch 90 contradicts** — and has a line for every person through its door and a longer queue, and in about a month there will be nobody in this basin who does not know where to go, and **the price of an explanation in this city is going to be nothing and there is no form on which anybody may be charged for asking, and he is entitled to be annoyed and entitled to say so in a lane and is not going to be a martyr about it, because he built the first one** — and the thing that beats him is **not a register: it is a yard's method with a name on it**, and a yard is not a person, and it could not be bought off a bench in front of him. **Then the card.** He holds out his hand and says *the card*, and **Marek takes it out of the inside pocket of the coat it has been in since the fifth of the seed month — Ch 70 and Ch 80 both put it there and Ch 89 used to have Merrow produce it from his own pocket with no handover, which the batch 0004 review caught — and puts it on the bench**, and Merrow turns it round so the figure faces the room. He enters it: a figure of his, in a factor's handwriting, given to a man with no office on the fifth of the seed month, more than the hall pays, **not a bribe**, not for a post, not for a vote, for one document and one reading of it, refused on a causeway in four seconds, and **each of them has known every day where it was since.** What it bought: he had to go to a counter-desk clerk who had never been to Rainmarket and did not know what a counterweight was, and that clerk read the fourth clause out loud in a shed, **badly, on purpose, and told them he was doing it badly on purpose and why** — and that is the whole of the risk in the basin this year, and a room at the Tallow Gate exists because a man with no office could not be paid, **and he is not going to be thanked for any part of it and would like it minuted that he offered it and it was refused.** Nobody thanks him. **Marek says the one line he says in the chapter: the room at the Tallow Gate is a yard's doing and not a factor's, a yard voted it in six minutes on the nineteenth and a rope-maker lost a day of selling for it, and he stands in it because a yard said so out loud at a tank on the ninth and refused to put it in ink, and he would rather Merrow wrote that down than the other one; Merrow writes nothing down and gives the card back.** Marek reads his own card on a bench, puts his hand flat on the edge of the trestle, says nothing, is asked nothing, and **does not get a post, a wage, an office or a vote, and has never in this city been owed anything by anybody.** The boy goes out into the rain and asks a fourth thing — his father is not here, and nobody will say it to him, and he has worked out that Signe Alve gives the plate away because she does not know who he is — and Merrow, who has had an answer for everything else, has nothing at all, and says so, out loud, to a boy of fourteen, and does not improve it.

### Chapter 90 — The Fourth Morning
**The twenty-ninth day of the harvest month**, fourth bell, **the fourth and last morning the register is open to look** before it goes live in the ninth month. **Forty-one lines in forty-one hands**: one on the twenty-first, Pike's, and forty people in eight days since, the fortieth with a page torn out of a book read out from a doorway. The reader, nineteen, says it out loud to the lane about six times in an hour because it is the only true thing anybody has. **What a queue does to a building is that it takes the road**: sixty people in the lane, **one of the two who carry the yard's water** standing in it for four minutes with two pails and then carrying them back up and not coming down that day, and nobody asked to move — **the register's first real cost, unpaid and unpaid.** A rumour that the register is going to make credit safe goes forty feet along the lane and is stopped by somebody who was in the seed house, and what he said is not in the account because he said it to a woman on a causeway. **Marek reads the first line out four times, which is the fourth time since the twenty-first** — **the earlier *in five weeks* collided with four mornings inside eight days and could not be supported; the span is the twenty-first to the twenty-ninth**, and tells a woman of about sixty with a counterfoil dated the fourteenth of the second month, precisely, in the words the water office will use, that a copy of a rate is a return and there is no form in this kingdom on which a person may carry one out of a room that is not an office, **and that if he put a paper in her hand it would be a second document and nobody in any room could produce it**; she says that is the second time she has been told and the first time she has understood it, and stays at the front of the queue for two hours, and comes back on a fifth morning there is not supposed to be. **The levy trestle is in the yard, not the shed, because a levy is not a secret and a shed is eleven feet by nine:** Bessok Tan and another collector, coin and seed in the bottom of sacks, and **no total**, and the yard's reason given out loud about nine times, and a man asking what the day has come to and being told *I can tell you what you paid and I cannot tell you what the day has come to*, and why. **Wenna Drel comes at the fifth bell alone**, asks for it to be written down that she said it and that nobody asked her whether she minded, and **nobody asks her** — the cap is a member's cap, the roll is households, there is no form on which anybody asks her, the levy is on a cooperative's members, and she is not a member, she is a person. **Bessok Tan puts the board down on the trestle**, the only time in four years, refuses to write her name, gives the cap to her mother, and sends her to the shed to read her mother's ground, which is the second entry in the register. She stands four feet off the counter in a room of strangers for a quarter of an hour, asks nothing, reads nothing, and goes home; nobody follows her and nobody writes anything down, and the reader says the whole morning went out of the room twice while she stood in it and came back in as strong. **The second entry: the Share's own return-in-kind rule, entered on the twenty-second by Mirren Fisk in her own hand, in four lines, read out and not improved, with *a seed does not fail because of a schedule* asked for on the fourth line, and about nine people leaving before she got to it.** **Ilma Tresch** has her own line read out twice, the second time slower, which is a thing the room does because of a girl of sixteen and a keeper on an island: her ground is the sixth of the eleven, the figure is the figure, the keeper's words are in the second line and hers in the fourth, and there is still no roll in this city with her figure on it and no office that holds it and no way to produce a copy of a thing nobody has. She came to find out whether her own line was right; **it is**, and she has not got the faintest idea what to do about it, and she works out in ten minutes that there is nobody in the room she could say that to — the man at the counter cannot enter anything, the collector is a member of the Share and not of her household, the boy goes home to his mother — and says it to the room, and asks nobody to stop her and nobody to thank her. **Marek puts the flat board down on the trestle under the form**, where anybody can lift it off, and nobody ever does, and he has never been able to say why and has stopped trying. **The register keeps no total, the yard said out loud in the seed month why, and nobody in Tallow Ward has ever been able to find out how many people are standing in a lane outside a shed, and this is the first thing the room is good at and the only one anybody could pay for.** The ninth month is almost on top of them, a cut is not a date, the lamp has not come off the ground, nobody can serve a notice on a yard, and the lane fills up again at the seventh bell and the man with no office reads the first line out four times, **the fourth time since the twenty-first**. **The queue that sorts itself by nothing at all is now explained by the yard's own Ch 84 rule that the register has no list, and the invented callback to a grain factor in a shed a fortnight before is gone: Pike's floor is Ch 83 and his lane outside this same shed is Ch 87, eight days back, and neither said it.** The form's **fifth line is the hours and is empty because the hours are nobody's paper** — **not, as an earlier pass of these files had it, a line nobody could say what it was for; Ch 84:107 names it on the page and the false version had propagated into Ch 87, Ch 90 and four state artefacts.** The piece of sack was written **on the third day of the harvest month**, not on the twenty-second of the seed month.

## Volume 02, Movement V — What the Paper Is Worth (Chapters 91–100)

### Chapter 91 — Advise
**The ninth month is named and the carry's demand arrives in the levy office.** A young man of about twenty-six walks it up Bell Stair on foot in a leather tube and apologises for the tube, and Grale makes him read the word aloud: the second month's letter said *kindly exhibit the line and advise the district* and this one says the district named is **to be charged** and the charge entered, and the office will wait until the close of the month. Both words are correct and Grale wrote neither, and the distance between *advise* and *is to be charged* is the whole of what has happened to four hundred and six people since the second month. **The chapter states what a district account is** — a page showing what is *owed to* a district, so a claim dealt with comes off it and a claim nobody can deal with stays on it for ever — and the recited carry is a line entered once a year since before the flood years and never applied. **The demand cannot be answered by anybody in that room, because applying an unapplied sum requires holding the district account to its presented figure, which is a cut, and a cut is an order in a frame on that office's wall with no copy anywhere, and the officer who enters it is Grale**, who has cut four in nineteen years and has wanted to stop since he was thirty-five and will not do a fifth because a sheet came up in a tube. **Marek is in the room with a column of the Tallow account and nine names that are not on the Gate tank's own count, and writes two words on the back of his list — *a kind?* — and does not ask Grale about it**, because a man who has kept a condition for twenty-three years is not a man you ask a question in a copy room. **Nineteen years and four bells, and the trestle is out in Tallow Ward for the second week of the levy, with no total on the slate, and the cap is already producing the person it was always going to produce.**

### Chapter 92 — Three and a Half
**Grale's referral is answered at last: three dates of four, and a half.** Ivo Sarn's minute of the fourteenth of the second month came up the hill in a Crown March envelope with no name on the outside, and it carries three finished dates on which Hesper Rann entered something in that desk's account that was not a levy, a schedule, a posting or a return, and a half-written fourth — a day, a month, and then the word *the* and nothing, with the minute-taker's own note that the witness stopped him there and that he read the unfinished line back and the witness said it was correct. **Ostrowsky, fifty-eight, nineteen years, nine hundred settlement countersignatures and never once in his life asked where a fourth clause went, is asked one by Grale for the first time in nineteen years**: what goes at the top of a page. *The day. Then the ruling, then the kind. You do not put a party at the top of a page because a page is not a claim, a page is a page, and the day is the only thing on it that finds it again.* **The consequence is the chapter: the three dates a man gave in a cell in Crown March are not dates of entries at all, they are the days at the heads of three pages, in Grale's own hand, in the office's practice — and not one of the eleven older pages in the fifth box has a date of its own. The fourth page was begun by the officer of this office and the day on it is his.** He could finish it in four hours and does not, because the day he proves the line he has to stop, and stopping now would leave the ninth month's demand unanswerable — **and he writes it in his own hand in the record and calls it duty, and understands before he goes home that he has just found a rule that lets him stay and call it duty.** The referral and the frame are in the same book and are not the same thing, and he lets them be in his head at the same time on purpose, once, and gets nothing for it.

### Chapter 93 — Twice
**The chain arrives in Tallow Ward in the same week as the carry, and the yard is charged twice.** Grale walks up the low-ground road on foot to read the last line of the first cut order to the only party that cannot write beside its own line, and the lamp goes out eleven minutes after he arrives, and the office supplies a replacement in the third month and not before. **Mirren Fisk says the part he had not read, once, and asks never to have it repeated:** the field does not want a lamp, the *bench* does; the bench is the germination bench; a lot that does not come up goes through the Share's own rule into eleven days and a third of another member's kept-back and then a rate on a household's ground, **and there is no form in this kingdom in which a rate on a member's ground goes.** In about two years a woman on that road will be standing where he is standing with a number she cannot pay in money and in seed she does not have, and nobody will be able to say who decided and when. *I am not asking you for anything. There is nothing in this room for a man from an office to do.* Meanwhile **Bartel Reisz, fifty-eight, thirty-one years in the third shed of the carriers' row, comes down the lane having been allowed to read his own rate, and finds forty-three and some months at a discount with a house at the end of it that nobody in the ward has ever heard of** — and works out, in front of about forty people, that he cannot pay it: on that plate what is owed is to the yard, so paying it stops the plate owing and pays the house nothing, and next season it is on there again; *you cannot pay your own debt, you can only pay somebody else's, and this is my plate, and a shed is not a household, and a shed cannot be asked.* His own written offer to pay it is in a book with no letter beside his name and no form exists on which he may be given it. **Kern puts the two charges in the open from the step of the tank in front of about ninety people, refuses to work out which the yard can afford, and says he has asked nobody about the third one, which is bigger, on a sheet on a hill, and would like it minuted that he asked nobody.**

### Chapter 94 — Cannot Choose
**The board is asked to choose between the recited carry and the district claim and cannot, and the Chair writes a ward captain's objection in longhand in a yard, again.** The notice goes up at the Gate tank because **nobody can serve a notice on a yard**, under a grandmother's cup, above three boards that cannot be carried into a room; about four hundred people are in a corridor and a stair in the rain outside a hearing room built for thirty, and **Ilya Senn puts the rope on the stair himself** and tells them the stair is narrow. Harrow presides, is ineligible to be chair, has no clerk and serves the minutes himself at four minutes a page; Sedge, Kern, Vendl, and the Trust's seat empty for the fifth time. **Kade's sheet holds four figures in the order they were called and is the only document in the room with all four on it — 40,900 · 43 and some months · 140 a season · 406 — with no total at the foot and no sign between the first and the second**, and she says it is a record and not an argument, from a seat that is not a membership, and that if she held a view the view would have her name over it. **The thing that stops the table is not a disagreement: it is that the two are not the same kind of thing.** Sedge finds it in four minutes. Kade says once, from a seat that is not a membership, that the room is being asked for a comparison and that a declining will be read in nine months as a refusal to decide, and asks that it be recorded where it came from. **Term four does the work: Kern's grounds, five sentences, first person, written out by the Chair in the finding, and then taken out of the room** — because a Crown March record is a building four hundred and six people have never been inside — and rewritten at the Gate tank in the rain, in longhand, on the back of a complaint sheet off a trestle with a carpenter's pencil, and read out twice, and put on the nail under the refusal and beside the sentence from the nineteenth of the harvest month, with a bit of string. **Marek is asked and says one sentence — that there is no office in this city that can produce the steps between that sum and this account, and that it is a form, not a mystery — and Harrow writes it with no name at the foot of the paragraph and does not say why.** Nothing paid, nothing refused.

### Chapter 95 — It Was Fair
**The register goes live, the Morrowfen assignment licence lapses, and Ovid Merrow says the loss was fair.** The Share carries the money, the yard gives the shed, and the register is declared live **for the eighteenth of the ninth month — a date, and a date is a day somebody else picks, and Bessok Tan wants that in the bench book in that order** — with the levy at one hundred and forty a season, the cap standing, the yard's three conditions read out again, and the hole named out loud from the step: the yard's own refusal cannot be entered, and in six months a record of what the yard did will say the yard's method is not in it, and that will be true. **At the posting desk, Sedge reads the third condition of a licence that has been on the board longer than anybody in the room has been alive — the claim shall be sold with its counterweight named upon the instrument sold and the number of persons who will bear it stated upon the same — beside a bundle of nine sheets and fifty-two lines with a stated rate and a discount and a receiving party and no counterweight at all, and a counterseal nobody on that hill can read, which nobody at the desk mentions, because an audit in this city goes as far as the seal and stops.** Ferrow is right and is not a defence: a bundle cannot be made to comply, because the answer is in it and the answer is a district, and a licensed house that will not sell a bundle can still sell a part, and this basin is about to have four prices instead of one and four prices are worse for the people at the bottom of them. **Nothing has been found against anybody: no fraud, no misstatement, a figure that balances and a clerk who counts twice, no finding and nothing to appeal, and the licence can be restored in a minute on an instrument that complies.** Merrow asks for two things in the entry and gets them: that no finding of fraud was made, and **that the loss was fair — said in nine seconds at a counter, and then why: the condition has required him to state the number of persons who will bear a counterweight for nine years, he could never do it, he never counted them, and counting them is the work and the work is the part that does not get to be interesting.** Then he comes down to the Gate shed and asks to enter a line about his own paper and is refused on the register's own first rule, because a counterweight named as a district has nobody to name it to — *that is the rule you came to beat, and it is my own paper, and I cannot enter it* — and says two things and no more: that the price of an explanation in this city is going to be nothing and that is the only thing he has lost, and **that he is not going to count the people in his own book now, and the reason is that there is nothing left to sell them into, and if he is honest in a shed in Tallow Ward that is the entire reason.** Ferrow says *no* in two words and will not give a reason. Nobody thanked anybody.

### Chapter 96 — Held by Nobody
**Grale proves the line in the account desk at about the second bell on the twelfth of the ninth month, in about four hours, and stops at noon, and the district account is then held by nobody.** The line: every one of the eleven pages in the fifth box older than the eight-year practice has a day at the head of it in this office's hand and **not one of the eleven entries has a date of its own**, so none of them has a day on which it could be applied; the receiving party at the end of them is not a party but a word, a kind, the kind of rate that offsets a reserve and is never charged to anybody; **the account and the box have been saying opposite things about the same figure for three hundred years because one of them is a book entry and the other is a book entry, and there is no fourth box on the form and there never has been.** The fourth of the four pages was begun by Grale and the day on it is his. He tells Ostrowsky, and then says the other thing, which costs more: **that what he has believed for twenty-three years — that the order in the frame gets cheaper as it goes down and that somebody arranged the order of the lines about sixty years ago — is not this, is not connected, and will now never be proved by him or by anybody after him, and that is the price of a condition he set himself in a yard in the rains and would set again.** His notice is dated the twelfth and takes effect on the day. He refuses to let Marek put the finding in the register: a levy officer of nineteen years putting a sentence in the first person into a shed four hundred and six people are affected by would make a ward's method into something a man in a good coat can produce at anybody. **Marek's answer — that this is the correct answer and the fourth time this year somebody has said it to his face and every one of the four was somebody who was not going to have to carry it — is the fourth and last of their professional exchanges, and it is not warm.** The water office writes, and writes again, to the address of an office and not a person: **a district account is held by a person, not by a book, a room, a wall or a shelf, and there is no form in this kingdom for a district account without an officer, and a building is not a holder** — which is the same shape as a counterweight named as a district, and everybody in the basin would recognise it on sight. The recited carry stays unapplied, forty-three and some months stays on the plate, and a cut gets cheaper as it goes down is *not* resolved and is now unprovable.

### Chapter 97 — Under Her Own Name
**Neris Kade's own district's paper goes into the register, in her own hand, under her own name, on the fourth line, and it is the forty-second line in forty-two hands.** She goes up the upland road on the twelfth with an empty satchel — a season examiner may not carry a lot she has examined, and the paper is in her own district — into Hartlow, four terraces up, where there is a room off the seed room with a fire in it and a notice nailed to the outside of the door: *ANYBODY MAY COME IN AND SIT AND LOOK. NOTHING IS CHARGED. NOTHING IS SIGNED.* It has been on the door fourteen months and four people in this basin have read it, and the notice on the Gate shed door has been read about nine hundred times, and they are the same three rules. **She tells Ruta Vrabec, fifty-one, who is on the cooperative's roll of six women and has never in her life been asked a question by anybody, that there is a room in Tallow Ward where anybody may enter a line about their own paper, and that she is not going to ask her to be in it.** Ruta goes down the road on the cart, and Janko Vrabec, fifty-eight, the last of the four terraces, comes too, under a board, and nobody is carrying anything for anybody. **The room works out in a minute that the keeper cannot enter the line, because the counterweight is not the keeper: the counterweight is *and the seed-keepers of this village after us bear the keeping*, and the people who will bear it are the mother of the boy of nineteen and the boy, and the number is two, and one of them did not choose it and the other is saying so in a shed in a ward she has never been inside.** Ruta writes the second line herself in her own hand and gets one word corrected. **Kade's sentence is on the fourth line, which is the only line the form gives her: the pledge was accepted as a class and not as an individual, a class is not a person, she has known that for two years, it is the reason she wrote the sentence, the sentence is a confession and it is the only honest thing on the paper; the witnesses were real and one is dead and the other is eighty-one; the paper is well made and the mechanism is lawful and every figure balances and nobody is to be punished; she saw what it was going to do, at the time, and wrote a sentence about it and went on certifying; she has had two years to write this in a room and did not; and she is not asking anybody for anything.** She asks for one thing: that it go on the same sheet as the entry with her name at the foot, because a correction on a paper of its own is a thing that can be lost. **Marek reads the entry out, and reads it once and stops, and she asks him to read it again at the ordinary speed and not slower and not to make a thing of it, and he does, and neither of them looks at the other, and it is the smallest thing either of them has ever asked for.** Nobody thanked anybody. What she is angry about, once, is the sixth line: the days, ruled and empty, and a day and a half of walking for three people, and no box in the kingdom for the days. Janko Vrabec closes the chapter by refusing to let it become the story of a woman of fifty-one who came down a hill, and names the three other people carrying it, and will not do the arithmetic out loud, because there is a rule in the shed about totals and the rule is right.

### Chapter 98 — No Officer
**The water office's second cut of MR 413 is read to every party it names and is not entered, and the school is named as a party for the second time.** A man who is not an officer drafts it in the words of the office on a form that has not changed since before the flood years; the four things a cut order says are written out without being asked; **and Ostrowsky asks, at the first bell, the question he has never asked in nineteen years — where a fourth clause goes — and the answer is the fourth of the four things, which is that a cut order carries the name of the officer who enters it, and there is no officer in the building.** The reading is complete: the low ground's night lamp, the clinic's coal, the river gauge, the four weighbridge lamps where the pumps man says *so the sluice is above the gauge* and is told *so the sluice is above the gauge*, the pumps, and the seed house's lamp again because the rule is that it is read. Then the fourteenth line of the office's list, which is the school. **Pella Rusk says the number and the shape of it in a corridor at the sixth bell, in terms: they have named the school twice, once in a finding and once on the last line of an order in a frame in a building she has never been inside, and neither time was anybody asking her anything, and both times the school was the bottom of the list; and a school is not a party, so it cannot decline, so a declining in her own words is a woman in a corridor and a man's face is not a record and it is gone at the seventh bell; and in a year somebody will open a book and find the school was named as a party and was never asked, and she wants that on the sheet.** The ninety-one and the fourteen are the third time she has said them and nobody asked for it. **Marek is at the other end of the corridor because a man with no standing stands at the other end. She does not ask him about page three, and he notices that it is a decision and not a courtesy, and understands that she has traded the answer she is owed for a number in a sheet, and that the trade was hers.** He offers her the register and tells her, before she asks, what it cannot do. **The order is not entered. A cut is not a date: nothing falls on a day, nothing falls at the close of a month, the lines fall when somebody sends somebody, and this year nobody has sent anybody, and there is no form on which anybody may be applied for.** The frame goes back on the wall over the pale rectangle with one name under its last line, four days old, and the fourteenth line of the list is a school off the north lane with about ninety-one children on its roll.

### Chapter 99 — The Sixteenth
**Tomas's Crown March countersignature comes back with a wrong date, and the crew, and the three seasons of eleven days.** Supervised road work on a Crown March road is Crown March money and a Crown March office countersigns the document it releases the money against, and it dates the day the money went out — the sixteenth — and not the day the board of five in Bell Stair found the schedule lawful, which was the seventeenth, and no office in Crown March has heard of a board in Bell Stair and there is no form on which one advises the other. **Both dates are true and the paper carries one of them, on the front.** He tries three ways to get it corrected and refuses all three: a letter, because then a man in a town he has never been to has changed a date because a bridgewright asked nicely; an inquiry, because he is a bridgewright and the answer will be no and the no will be in a record for ever with his name at the bottom of it; **and a clerk, in a record, saying so, which is the only thing he wants and which does not exist.** He says the work is untouched — sixty-six days across three seasons, six men named, residual on the Gate board, and no form in this kingdom that can un-lay stone — and says the twenty-six months of his own recovery and Bohum Ostrek's two weeks of hand are not the date's fault and are not fixed by a date. **Lio, nine, with a slate, asks what a date is for and is told it is for afterwards, and writes on the slate, in a child's hand, *afterwards is us, so tell them the true day*, and nobody stops her.** Marek establishes that there is exactly one person in the city who can put a Crown March date in a Crown March record, that he will not do it as a favour, and that a representative who has to say in his own minute that a thing in his own region is capable of being read two ways is a representative who has said an office in Crown March is careless. **Harrow writes it at the sixth bell on the seventeenth, in his own hand, at a table with a hat on, as the last of five paragraphs with no name at the foot of it, records the discrepancy at the request of a party to the work and not at the request of any office, and nobody countersigned it and nobody thanked him and he did not mention it again.** Marek is not in the room. He is told at a tank at the seventh bell by a rope-maker, and says *that is very good of him*, and Sanne Droll says *do not*, and the man at the counter has never worked out which of the two was the kindness.

### Chapter 100 — Nothing at the Head of It
**The register's first morning as a live register, the eighteenth of the ninth month, and a line with nothing at the head of it which nineteen hands decline to name and the yard votes against filling.** About eighty in the lane at the fourth bell for a shed that holds twenty-two; the trestle out in the yard for the third week; **no total, and the reader saying out loud eleven times in four hours that there is a line in a hand for every person who has come through the door and a bundle is not one line and a hand is the only kind of entry there is, and that there were forty-one lines in forty-one hands on the fourth morning of looking and there are more now and nobody in Tallow Ward will ever be able to say how many people were standing in a lane outside a shed.** **Wenna Drel, sixteen, brings a slate with her name on it and puts it on the trestle in her own hand, and Bessok Tan puts the roll down flat and will not write her on it again, and tells her that nobody is going to ask her and the yard is not going to and he is not going to, and that if anybody ever does it will be a different thing and she can say no to it — and she takes the slate back and stands four feet off the counter in a room of strangers for a quarter of an hour and nobody follows her and nobody writes anything down, and the cap is still the correct cap.** A boy of fifteen from Ropewalk has ruled a column on the wall list for the kind of paper each line is about, and **there is nothing at the head of it, and it is the third such bare place in this basin in a year, after a sheet cut off a seed sack in a keeper's house in the growth month and a column on a board in a shed in Tallow Ward in the seed month, and the fourth is a column on a bench on the low-ground road, and nothing in this city has ever proposed a heading for any of the four.** Somebody proposes *rate* and then *loan*, in the ordinary way, with a reason, and the room goes quiet, because a heading over a column of people's papers is a line, and it is the one kind of line that cannot be entered at all, because there is no person behind it. **Nineteen people decline to name it over half an hour, nobody counts it out loud, nothing is written down, one of the nineteen is a woman of forty-six who cannot read and one is the boy of fifteen who ruled it and said it is not his to name; and the yard then votes in four minutes both ways — not to fill it and not to rule it out, because a heading is the one thing on a sheet that cannot be put in afterwards by anybody who was not there on the day, and a promise not to name one is a heading too.** Kern also names what the room has cost: about nine people who stopped coming and nobody knows which nine, one of the two people who carry the yard's water going up the lane with two pailfuls past a queue of eighty, a fifth line for the hours that is empty because the hours are nobody's paper, a sixth line empty for ever, and a day and a half of walking with nowhere to put it. **And the refusal is still on the nail, and there is a space on the wall inside the shed where it would go, and the space is under a column with nothing at the head of it, so it cannot go in there either.** Then the last image: a man of about forty from the Tanner's yard comes down the lane with a rate arrears on a shed plate and nothing, and a person with a table and a bundle of **Compact forms** is in the lane — a form is a sheet of paper and a sheet of paper is not a house, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a person may be stopped. **Merrow comes out, says four things in fifteen seconds, and the fourth is that a ward is not a person and a shed is not a person and a district is not a person, and those three are the only three things that could have been asked; he refuses to witness it, and says that is not a kindness, it is a rule and it is an old one and it has cost him money before he has sold anything; and the man signs.** Marek is nine feet off with about nine seconds in which he could have said one sentence, and does not go out, and writes nothing down, **because a line may be entered by the person behind it and about their own paper, and the man is the person behind it and he is not in the room and is not going to be, and the register's one rule — a bundle may not be entered as one line, the counterweight named to the people who will bear it — has nothing whatever to say about a shed's rate arrears, which is where the Compact's money came from in the first place.** The reader reads the first line out twice at the ordinary speed. A register is not a seed.

---

# VOLUME 03 — The Borrowed Harvest (Chapters 101–150)

### Chapter 101 — The Weighbridge
**MR 413, the fourth of the tenth month, at night, and the low ground's barley rate has fallen by about a third in nine days.** Nobody in Rainmarket can say why, and the two people who could are four miles up a road with eleven switchbacks in it. The weighbridge on the low-ground road is a stone platform with a beam on two posts, and Ottmar Kuez has stood on it for twenty-two years and reads the rate out to every cart and **will not say the figure aloud**, because it is written in a bridge book with a day against every line and nobody has ever come to look at the book. **Forty carts at the fourth watch, thirty-one the night before, about twenty the night before that**, and they are not Rainmarket carts: nine of the forty are the same make and are coming off the uplands in a hurry that is not a hurry about weather. **Petar Duss has four of the nine**, and he has been on this bridge four times since the twenty-second of the ninth month and been paid the best night of hauling he has ever had. **Twenty-six carts off this bridge since the twenty-second carried a mark, nine of them his or were**, and a man with a lantern at the top of the upland road waves the Crown March paper through and nothing else, and has been told so and does not know by whom, and Duss has been asking him every time he passes. Duss's two wants are both refusals: *I do not care whose mark it is. I care that it goes* — and he would like somebody to write down that he knew the road was opened by somebody on purpose, because **in about three weeks somebody is going to tell a room that the men who brought the barley down did not know where it was from, and they will be right that he did not know and wrong about the not knowing.** A clerk from a good house came into his yard on the twenty-first of the ninth month and told him the rate in nine days, and **a man does not know a rate nine days out unless somebody has written it down for him.** From the fourth bell to the sixth Marek stands on the plank deck and reads the low ground's rate out to every cart, twice, at the ordinary speed, and about nine carts stop and ask him things. He can tell a man where the figure is written and that he does not know who set it; he will not tell a man what a rate is going to do, and he names the one man in Rainmarket who will; and he tells the ninth man that the price is not falling because anybody has decided anything, and that a price is telling you something at four in the morning on a bridge because there is no room. Duss's sentence, from the deck, about a market full of grain it did not grow. Kuez does not write an opinion in his book and says out loud, to nobody, that he has been right about that for twenty-two years and has never had cause to say so before.

### Chapter 102 — Eleven Switchbacks
**The eighth to the tenth of the tenth month, up a road with eleven switchbacks that takes a day, and then the four terraces at dawn.** Hartlow is the fourth and the top. **Of thirty-nine lots sown on the second terrace, nine are sand**, and Neris Kade has been up there since the fourth of the ninth month and has put her own hand in nine trays of it. **Marek has come with a sentence and it is his own, said once in a witness room on an island on the twentieth of the growth month, not for writing down, and not said since** — and the other thing he has come up with is the sentence a woman put on the fourth line of a wall in a shed in Tallow Ward in the ninth month, in her own hand, that a class is not a person and never has been one and cannot be asked what it will bear. **Kade cuts him off in about nine words: *You have brought a good sentence to a bad morning*,** and then spends the whole of the morning showing him what happens to a true sentence carried up a road — a terrace agrees with it, it goes in a book in a room in Bell Stair in nine months under somebody else's handwriting, and in two years a man in a good coat says *they said so themselves, in the valley, at the time.* **Janko Vrabec, fifty-eight, keeper of the Hartlow roll, comes out of the low door and makes Marek say to his face that he is not going to make the dying man the story, and then asks him about the season instead.** The third terrace has stopped reporting: a folded sheet with a figure on it, sent once a year for nineteen years, not sent this year, **and no form in this kingdom in which anybody may be sent to ask why.** The season works are nine lines made by the terraces themselves; the ninth is the fourth-terrace seed store and it is the only one with a key, **and Janko has left the ninth line ruled and empty rather than write his own name in it, because in the eleventh month somebody would have to strike a man's name off a list and there is no box for that.** He asked for the sowing to come early in the second month of MR 412, in writing, with reasons, and it was sealed in the hills in about twenty-two days; the sowing was done in the second month of MR 413; **the rain came six weeks early, it was warm, and then the ordinary cold came, and there is no name in that valley for what has happened to nine trays of ground.** *I asked for it. It arrived.* The chapter ends with Marek on a step in the dark for twenty minutes saying nothing at all, which is the only thing anybody on that terrace could have done with what he was told, and a dying man with a lamp who will sit up in his own yard, because that is the whole of what a dying man can do with a form.

### Chapter 103 — Sixty-One Trays
**The twelfth of the tenth month, and the Elmsworth seed house's lamp is the thirteenth of fourteen lines on a list in Bell Stair and is supplied in the third month and not before.** It went out on the sixth of the ninth month and **Hana Belic, thirty-four, a member in her own right who draws the eleventh tray — one of the eleven that came up as sand — has worked sixty-one trays twice a day since, in the dark, by feel, with a cough in the small of her back.** Mirren Fisk moves the germination bench into the Gate shed. The argument takes about nine minutes in a yard with about forty people in it and everybody in it is right: the bench comes first and a lamp is a list nobody has sent anybody against, and the register is a practice but a cooperative's bench in a ward's room for six weeks is a thing a man in a good coat can point at, and **honesty in a room is cheap and distance is not**, because anything can be said in a shed and the trouble starts about a quarter of a mile away. Marek proposes the fifth line. **A hundred and twenty-two trays a day, which is sixty-one twice, is a person's hours and it is the only hours anybody in this basin has ever said out loud, and it is refused by the yard's own rule and for the yard's own reason: a round that repeats is the third box on the settlement desk's form, and the yard refused the Compact's levy in the seed month for that, and a figure that appears on a board the same way twice turns the board into a roll, and a shed where a person may ask what a piece of paper is becomes a place where a cooperative's hours are kept, which is a thing a man in a good coat can produce at anybody in nine months.** The yard would have fixed the empty fifth line and turned a register into a wage book. What they get instead is a rota on the seed house wall with days on it and **nothing at the head of it** — not the day, not the season, not the Share's name, and not a keeper's name, because there are sixty-one members and which of the sixty-one is on the rota is a question the yard has no standing to ask and the Share has no form to ask it with. Belic finds it herself: *We have built a rota that belongs to a shed.* And then: *Leave it bare. Put the days on.* The bench goes up the lane in eleven pieces in the rain and comes out at the sixth bell; two hours of a four-hour day; no water at the Ropewalk standpipe; one of the two women who carries the yard's water now comes down at the second bell to beat the queue. On the first morning the reader stops in the middle of a second line because the bench frame is in the way of the board and says so out loud, and the register runs forty minutes over, and about nine people laugh. **This is the fifth bare place in a basin in one year and nothing proposes a heading for it.**

### Chapter 104 — Two Hundred and Eleven Sheets
**The fifteenth of the tenth month, eleven steps under a chandler's yard in Bell Stair.** A cellar leased by Vesna Duss, sixty-one, for nineteen years, in which she has never read a document, and which is the only room in this city nobody can produce. Nine crates. **Sedge counts twice: two hundred and eleven sheets of a Trust seasonal advance form**, and the printer's ledger calls the run *eleven hundred and something* — **the phrase is locked and no chapter may fix the figure** — so about a thousand sheets are unaccounted for, which is not a scandal, because a printer sells paper and the season offices of a kingdom use more than eleven hundred forms a year. **Sedge, who has no service length anywhere, says the thing he is required to say: a form is not a contract, and a form is a sheet with printing on it, and the counterweight in the fourth box is in type and has been for nineteen years and is nobody's fault.** Vesna Duss then tells the whole of it before anybody asks: in the flood year a cart came off a road in this lane, the driver asked for a dry room for the night, and in the morning said the load was paper for the Crown March depot at Verrow, that the account had gone on to somebody else, and that he would leave it in the lane. She took it and has never moved it. **And then she excludes herself: I am not going to be thanked for telling you, and I am not going to be the woman in a yard with a cellar of Crown March forms.** Nobody asks her anything. Sedge's find, on the form: the fifth box at the foot is a return, the oldest box on the form and the only one not dated in this century, and it is the box a district may hand a returnable sum over in at a discount — **a box for a thing being handed on, and he has never in his life seen anybody fill one in with anything else.** And the ninth crate's lid has a Tide Reckoning mark on the inside of it: a month, a tide phase, a harbour seal in wax, a translator. **Not a forgery, not a mystery, not a clue: a shipping mark, put on by a man in a port for a crate going to a depot in the hills.** Marek sees it in the fourth minute, does not go back, and tells Sedge and not Vesna Duss. Sedge says no, and gives the reason — a form is stock, a crate is a crate, and a shipping mark is a shipping mark — and goes back down the eleven steps to put a board on, which is the only thing about it that is anybody's business. **Marek writes two hundred and eleven on the back of his own hand in pencil, and it is the first number he has written down this year, and it goes in nobody's record.**

### Chapter 105 — The Man Who Came Down the River
**The nineteenth of the tenth month, and a barge comes up on the morning tide with a Crown March flag on it, and a man of fifty-four comes up the second stair and counts the roofs of Rainmarket.** **Sabin Rell, granary, the depot at Verrow, twenty-nine years.** He has come because the basin feeds three regions, the harvest is good, and the board must certify a quarter's export in six days, and he has to put a number on a piece of paper and be able to say in a room of eleven men that the number is the number. He has read about the Gate shed and has come down a river to see whether the room thinks: four people told him the sentence wrong and one of them asked a question, and he has been in this trade long enough to know the difference between a room that agrees and a room that thinks. **He is right about all of it.** Sefa the boatwoman puts her hand in the oats and tells him the other half: nine days is not a figure anybody can know nine days out, the road was opened on a Crown March paper, and a man with a lantern waved nine carts through. **And Rell does not let the two matters be conflated, and says why: if he does, then in three weeks somebody will tell a room that a Crown March official opened the road to steal a harvest, and it will not be true, and it will be true enough that nobody will hear the rest of it.** He was not asked and says it anyway. Then he takes the clause out. **The projection, defined: a granary's forward estimate of a district's yield, entered in a ledger in the year before the yield, which is a number and not a measurement and is the ordinary practice of a book that has to balance.** The advance is **eleven thousand and some hundreds of pounds and he cannot give the exact figure and will not, because the exact figure is in a ledger in Verrow, it is not his, and a man in a good coat would carry it down a road and ruin about nine people.** **The four terraces are entered at the ten-year mean, and the ten-year mean is not in the book he is carrying and not in any book in Morrowfen, and he is not permitted to produce it, because the moment a depot can produce its own estimate the estimate is an offer instead of a measurement and an offer can be sued.** He says that in about forty words, unasked, on a stair, before he has signed anything, and he is entirely comfortable and he is not hiding anything. **Then the sentence that is the defence: *Go and look for the room that signed the paper, not the room that is keeping the number.*** And he admits he has been in this trade twenty-nine years and has never once known where the number is kept, and has known all along where the paper is.

### Chapter 106 — Four Parts
