**The twenty-first of the tenth month, two rooms on a lease above a chandler's shop.** Marek asks Halvard Sunn to say whether the hand is his on four instruments, and Sunn will not swear, and says the reason in nine sentences, and the reason is the volume's hinge. He builds a mark out of four objects on the table in ninety seconds without touching it — an almanac, a brass seal, a stick of wax, a pen — and says that is the whole of what he can show anybody. **A Tide Reckoning mark is four parts: a month, a tide phase, a harbour seal, a translator. Three of the four are objects and one is a movement, and a man in a good coat is looking nine times in ten at a month and a seal, and the hand is the smallest thing on the paper.** **A refusal to swear is not a denial, and he says that first, because a no at that table costs the man who asked.** Then: **a mark that names a day can be checked against the day by anybody with a calendar, and a mark that names a month can only be checked against a day by somebody with a tide table** — my months are not your months, my tides are not your tides, and there is one tide table in this basin and it is on the table in front of him. He could put a day on any sheet he is handed, and a notary who adds to a paper is a notary who is no longer certifying the paper, and the moment the mark carries a day the mark is corroborating the paper instead of standing beside it. **And the thing he will say, and the thing he has never said to anybody in Morrowfen in nine years: a mark that names a month can go on any day inside that month. That is not a trick. That is what a month is.** The whole worth of a mark is that a person who has never been to Morrowfen can read it and rely on it, **and the price of that is that it can be read by that person and by nobody else, and he has never once been able to have both.** He asks Marek what nine hundred marks are **for**, and it is the question he has never been able to answer. And Marek gives him the answer in one sentence — **a mark decides where a paper can go; the mark does not say the paper is good** — and Sunn asks him to say it again in about six weeks to a man who is going to be very pleased with himself. Then the housekeeping: a mark can be copied and a hand can be copied, so do not come to me and do not go to a court; **write to the harbour, the harbour keeps a list, the list says yes or no or the name is not on the list, in about six weeks for about a pound, and it will not tell you what the hand did on a particular day in a cellar four miles from a river.** Marek says he will do it in about a fortnight when he has somebody else's paper he cannot get into a room, and Sunn does not thank him. Two cups of tea, poured without asking, from a pot that had clearly been sitting on the hot stone all morning, because a man who is alone in a cold room in the tenth month does not make tea for one. **Neither of them mentions that the clause in the satchel Marek did not bring up the stairs carries a mark in its bottom corner.**

### Chapter 107 — The Twenty-Fourth
**The twenty-fourth of the tenth month, in a room over a chandler's shop hired for the day, with one table and four chairs, and four people in the room, and Marek standing because the chairs were for the four.** Sabin Rell, a young man from Verrow with the ledger, Ilse Ferrow — who has heard on the low-ground road that a Crown March paper is being sealed in a room and has a right to be there — and Marek. The clause is one sheet. **The sheet was sealed before anybody wrote a word on it**: the harbour seal struck in wax at the head of it, the Tide Reckoning mark in the bottom corner in four small parts, the Crown March office's own seal on the reverse out of Verrow in a locked box, and the only things not yet on it are a day and a name. **Every instrument in this kingdom that matters is sealed before somebody signs it, and there is a form for the sealing and there is no form for the signing.** The counterseal is Sunn's, hired for a day, and Sunn does not know Rell is in the building. Behind the clause is a schedule, eleven lines, eleven names, four terrace holdings — and the fourth column, which is the thing that will go wrong if the barley does not come, **is the same nine words on all eleven lines: *the seed-keepers of the four terraces after this season*.** **It is a phrase on a printed form, and the *and* before it is printed too, and only the number is filled in by hand, and the same nine words are in the Hartlow clause that Neris Kade confessed to in the ninth month with the number in front of them changed, and Marek reads the nine words four times standing four feet off a table and does not say the sentence he came with.** Rell's answer when asked what a projection is: a column, and he filled in a number, and it is not a fraud and it is not a decision he took. Rell cannot give the number of people in the eleven holdings — **a figure that can be entered can be added up, and the sum of the people in four terraces is the sort of number that a house in a good coat could take to a house in another good coat and turn into a yield** — and it is Volume 02's finding in a fifth mouth. Ferrow asks the true thing of the room before the fourth bell: *Do you know what you have just certified.* He says yes, in one word. She does not approve of him, says so in two words, is not thanked, and says that if anybody asks her in a year she will say that she asked him whether he knew and he said yes and then he signed it, **and that it will not help him and is not intended to.** At the fourth bell Rell dates the sheet twice, once in his hand and once in his own column, and signs. Then he asks, as a courtesy between two men who have been of use to one another, **whether Marek intends to do anything — and Marek says no, and means it, and the chapter says that this is the first time in the volume that he has a piece of information he is going to sit on.** Rell says the paper is clean, the figures balance and the market is in four regions' interest, there is no finding and nothing to appeal, **and he would like the record to say that a man in a good coat stood in that room for an hour, read his schedule four times, and said no.** *Go home. You are not going to do anything and I have given you the chance to say so to my face.*

### Chapter 108 — A Terrace at the Door
**The twenty-eighth of the tenth month, the register forty days old, about eighty in the lane, the germination bench in the shed against the wall, and a woman who has been on the road since the second bell.** She is refused at the counter of the lane and let in at the front, because there are eighty people in the lane and the fourth question is asked at the counter and not at the door. **Zofia Teran, forty-four, second terrace, keeps the terrace's tally in a school exercise book, and she has brought a copy of the fourth column in her own hand because she could not carry the schedule.** *Is that your line, is that the person behind it, is it your words.* **Nobody.** Her holding is on it; her name is against her holding and not against the paper; the paper is between the depot at Verrow and a house she has never been to. **And the fourth column is a class of people. She is in it this year. She does not expect to be in it in nine years, and the people who will be are the ones she has been raising. There are some she could name and she is not going to name them and she has not asked any of them, and she would like it written down that she decided not to and not that she forgot.** Neris Kade came up the road with her, has been in the lane an hour and forty minutes saying nothing, is named at the door as a second person, and gets a hand on nothing. **THE LINE DOES NOT GO IN, and the reason is not new and is not Marek's:** a person may enter a line about their own paper and nobody may enter a line about anybody else's, and the answer at the counter is nobody, correctly — **and the fourth column is a class of people, and a class of people cannot be traced to a door, so there is nobody to name the counterweight to, so the fourth line stays empty, so the line does not go in at all.** Kade, at the wall by the door, four lines, not warm: *You could have made a ruling. Somebody was standing in this room who had been waiting all month to make one.* / *So somebody could. And there is no form on which any of us is anybody's record.* The reader reads the pieces of it out anyway and reads the empty fourth line twice at the ordinary speed and does not explain what he is doing. **Teran: your rule is right and it is the rule that is stopping me and I have been four days trying to work out how to be angry and I cannot manage it** — and what she has got, and cannot do anything with, is that the room says the fourth line out as empty, to anybody who asks, and that a man in a good coat could carry that sentence into Verrow in a fortnight. She goes up the road alone in the dark and nobody walks her. **Sanne Droll says the one thing that has not been said in that shed** — the reader has said a thing about a paper to the person the paper is about and not to the room — **and asks that the yard notice it, and nobody writes it down**, and the reader then enters the date at the foot of the wall list, which is the only thing he has ever entered on that board that was not a line somebody was asking for. The fifth line is empty and the sixth line is empty and always will be, and the rota on a wall in Rainmarket has days on it and nothing at the head of it, and the reader reads the fourth line out twice at the ordinary speed to an empty room, because the room does that.

### Chapter 109 — A Brother, a Slate, and a Kitchen
**The second of the second month, MR 414, and the reckoning turned on the first and nothing in this basin marked it except a boy of sixteen paid for carrying boards in the rain and a girl of nine with a slate.** Marek walks out to his brother's kitchen on the north lane with something he has been building for nine days, and it falls apart in four minutes. Tomas is at the table taking a joint apart and putting it back together slightly differently so his hands are not idle. **Bohum Ostrek, who is on the span and is not in this kitchen, lost two weeks of the middle of his hand at a counter-desk in the rains month under a clause nobody has ever questioned.** Tamsin is at the other end with his coat and a needle and asks whether he has eaten. **Marek asks his brother not to go up the upland road, and Tomas refuses, and refuses for the right reason:** men in rooms have decided which of his losses was worth writing down, he has found out about four of them about a year after each, and the one he found out about soonest was the one Marek carried up a hill in a coat and asked nothing for. *You are eleven years late.* And what he actually wants: **I want to be told when I am being used. Not a month afterwards. Not by a clerk in Bell Stair with a record in his hand. In the room. Before.** Marek gives four sentences and every one of them is about the volume of work and the man being owed and there being no need, and stops, because it is a lie about a reason, and the true one is that he is frightened of saying the true thing in the wrong week and that saying it would be the end of it. **Lio asks what a Crown March is and is not satisfied, and says the sentence that is the best child in the manuscript after Wenna Drel's slate:** *you have said the word a man nine times in this kitchen and you have not said a person once* — and *you did not answer my question on the tail-board either.* Then Tomas says he is going up the road **as a bridgewright and not as a brother**: line two is a drain on the second terrace that has been holding a wall since the flood year and is going to go in the third month, line four is a road whose culvert has its mouth four feet too low, the bill goes on the season works, and the works is nine lines and the ninth one is empty. *I have been on this road thirty-four years and I do not need a plan for a drain.* **He will stand next to his brother when Marek says the sentence and will not look at him while he says it.** In the yard at the eighth bell Marek works out in about nine seconds that his brother is not asking him to be a different man, and that there is no form in this kingdom in which a person may be told anything first, and that the register is the only place he has found where a man says a thing out loud in a room where it is written down, and that nobody has ever told him it is a duty to anybody else as well.

### Chapter 110 — A Cargo Going Down
**The fifth of the second month, MR 414, fourth bell, and the barge goes down loaded, which is the thing nobody in Rainmarket has seen before the year turned.** The rate has stood for four days and the town has been eating since before the harvest month. Marek is on the river stair because he stood on a weighbridge on the fourth of the tenth month and it turned out that a man could do that. **Sefa: a price that stops falling is a price somebody is holding** — and the answer is always a person who is not in the room. **Petar Duss is on the stair at the third bell with a rope round his wrist, loading for the four o'clock up-road, and says out loud in front of about nine people that the mark cost four shillings and was not worth it.** The boat has about nine hundred sacks of barley and a coil of paper on the deck in a box that is not lashed well, and the captain has never had a certificate on his deck in eleven years and takes the box without looking at the paper and gives the boy a shilling for holding the rope, **and that is how a crew stays a crew.** The mark is four small parts on the outside of the roll where the string crosses it, and **nobody on that stair could read it if he had said what it was, and he said nothing.** **Margit Sova, forty-one, cooks on the low ground, a basket and a list and no money, asks the volume's question in plain speech and does not know it is one: *Who is it that says a thing is all right*** — and gets a plain answer: a person with a seal, standing in a room, at a desk, on a day, saying a thing is as it is on a page; always a person, never a district, never a class, never a number. And then the second question: **And who says the person with the seal is the right person. Nobody. A signature does not say whether a thing is true. A signature says where a thing can go.** He tells her it is not his, that a notary said it in two rooms above a chandler's shop, and uses the man's name nowhere, and she notices. *You have told me a man you know and not his name, which is a thing a man does when he is about to be somewhere in the morning and would rather be known as a reader of numbers.* The barge goes down past the second stair with the paper in a box going to Saltglass, where there is a list, and it is not coming back. **Marek has not written to the harbour, because a letter costs a pound and takes six weeks and he has a morning of road.** He has two numbers on the back of his left hand in pencil — a count of two hundred and eleven sheets, and four days of a rate that has stood — and they are the only two figures in this basin that belong to him and neither is about anybody's future. At the fourth bell the register opens with a bench in the shed on the days the rota says, the fifth line empty, the sixth line empty for ever, a column with nothing at the head of it, and a man with no office reads the fourth line out twice at the ordinary speed. Then he takes his coat off the nail and goes up the road.

### Chapter 111 — A Tide Table and a Lease
**The seventh of the second month, MR 414, two rooms above a chandler's shop.** Marek comes down eleven switchbacks to the one tide table in the basin and Sunn lays the almanac open with two fingers going down a month to three days where the water stands the same way, and neither of them can say which of the three is the seventh in Morrowfen's calendar. Sunn's pattern book holds four years of marks in wax with the instruction's date beside each, ordered by his own months, and about four in any month carry no day because the instruction carried none. **The discovery, from the inside and not as a lecture: a mark is made out of an instruction, the instruction is a sheet in a house nine days up the coast that is kept until the tide and then burned, and what is produced about nine hundred times a year leaves no record of who produced it.** Marek writes to the harbour for a pound — *yes*, *no*, or *the name is not on the list*, in about six weeks — and Sunn corrects one word. A boy in the lane says a Crown March clerk called Lenge comes to rooms over the shop on the ninth with a locked box and eleven sets of a paper.

### Chapter 112 — The Head of the Column
**Rooms over the chandler's shop, first bell to third bell, eleven sets of the Crown March standard form.** Petrus Lenge, thirty-three, six years at Verrow and four on the projection column, fills the third column with the same figure eleven times because one district means eleven parties see one number. **What a projection is, from the man who has to type it: a granary's forward estimate entered in the year before the yield, because a book has to balance in the year it is balancing — a wrong number is arithmetic and can be corrected in ink with two initials, and a blank says the office cannot do its job and only the man who left it can fill it.** The third terrace has not returned this year and Lenge turns back a page, reads last season's figure off in a hand four years older, and writes it into the eleventh space in about a minute and a half. The advance is eleven thousand and some hundreds on each set, the share on the district schedule at Verrow and not his to give. He will serve the eleven sets in person up the road on the eleventh, and Marek goes with him because he would be useful and not because he was asked.

### Chapter 113 — Nine Lines
**The eleventh of the second month, a loft over the seed room at Hartlow, nine lines cut in a board with a knife.** Cutting, a drain, the shared threshing floor, a road, a water, two store rooms, a seed store, and the fourth-terrace store with the key. Janko cuts his own name out of the ninth line in nine seconds rather than write it and have it struck in the winter, and there is no form on which anybody may be applied for and he will have no second key made. The third terrace sent nobody and is not put on the board because a line is a job done by whoever comes. **Tomas comes in with pegs, chalks his bill on the board in the margin against the drain and the road with a figure under each, and it is a bill and not a favour — no date, no office, nobody thanked.** At the culvert he shows the inlet four feet below the invert and names the week line two goes in the third month. In the dark he asks which column and whose number, and Marek gives a technically accurate answer about where the head is set instead of the answer, and Tomas hears the evasion: **you have known that since the sixth.** The cost lands the same night and is not raised again for the rest of the season.

### Chapter 114 — On the Back of an Envelope
**The sixth of the third month, the gate of the second terrace's holding, no desk, a notice and an envelope on a flat stone.** The wall has gone in with a terrace's worth of seed and Lenge serves the set-off: the advance not recovered out of the delivery remains outstanding and is recovered by set-off against the next account, so the next projection is smaller and the next advance is the seed. **A set-off takes seed eleven months before the seed wants to be in the ground, and there is no form in this kingdom for taking money back.** The party is the number, a holding cannot be present anywhere, and the holder's name goes on the line because a set-off has to have somebody on it — a field charged, a woman in a lane a witness and not a party. Lenge dates an envelope, signs it, and serves it on the person present; Zofia puts the notice on the seed house nail where forty people read it in a day. On the way down Marek says the whole of it is a charge on a number and Lenge asks what goes in the box at the foot, and Marek has no answer and does not pretend to one.

### Chapter 115 — A School Exercise Book
**The ninth of the third month, Zofia Teran's yard at the fourth bell, a school exercise book read flat for forty minutes to nine people.** It has a door, a day, what was said, and who said it, and it is the best record of the season anybody has and it is not a record: no name at head or foot, hers and she is a member of nothing, the man at the door not on the page by decision, a book in an apron that cannot be checked, a woman's account of what she was told and what she was told is not what was said. She reads doors in order, names nine people with one thing said at each door, and refuses *which nine*. **The garden-door entry says a man she has known nineteen years told her the road was opened on purpose and asked for it to be written down, and the man with the bar in the yard owns it and says leave it, and nobody is thanked.** She says she will not be the woman who came down the hill, and Marek does not ask her to be — Janko's refusal in Chapter 102 stands and stays refused.

### Chapter 116 — A Column With Nothing at the Head of It
**The fourteenth of the third month, the Gate shed lane, a clerk from the Chancellor's office with a case he is told not to set on the trestle.** Emmerich Tace wants a schedule of the persons in four terraces who will bear the settlement weight, by name, in a fortnight — Chancellor Ysara Venn behind him as a want and never as a body: she wants to know whether there is a room of people behind the paper to send a person to. The wall list has forty lines and a column with nothing at the head of it that nineteen hands declined to name and the yard voted in four minutes not to fill and not to rule out, and Droll will not have a yard's wall confused with a form. **Tace writes four names on his own form — the woman with the cloth, the examiner named at the door, the man with no office, and then Petar Duss, who comes down the lane and says the mark cost four shillings and he knew the road was opened on purpose and wants it written down — and it is a list of the people who asked questions and not a list of a district, and there are four hundred and some people in those terraces and about nine on any paper.** Marek writes Duss's sentence that night in his own hand as a private person with no office, from a conversation on a bridge, not evidence, not producible, and tells nobody. From the step Kern says the yard refused twice to fix its own hole on its own rule, cost the yard its water on bench mornings, lost about nine people nobody can name, and nobody thanks him.

### Chapter 117 — The Press and the Proof
**The ninth of the seed month, a printer's shop floor in Bell Stair with the yard door open, nine sheets up eleven steps and a crate lid carried separately.** The lid's mark and the form's mark sit side by side in the same four parts at the same distance from the edge, and Sedge, who has been in the building long enough that twice in it, says the form's is not a stamp but a head set in a case as furniture of the run. Kelb, who has taken nine hundred duplicates and whose left hand is a nine-year-old's and is not his, says a printed mark and a written mark cannot be told apart by looking, because the written one was copied off the printed one or off another written one and the paper does not remember which. They pull a duplicate on a taking press with a hand on the roller and a hand on the screw, and Sedge certifies it — **and that is the moment a copy stops being a copy: not the sheet but the name at the foot of it.** Marek's sentence of the day: a standing authority that certifies a projection has nothing to certify except a number, the figure under a typed head is a hand with nowhere to say how it got there, and nobody in Morrowfen can read a wrong mark because nobody in Morrowfen can read one. The duplicate goes in a flat case under the shed trestle and is never produced in any room.

### Chapter 118 — The Column at the Second Bell
**The eleventh of the rains month, the projection desk at Verrow at the second bell, a bound ledger Marek is not shown and a slate he is.** Lenge writes the coming season's column first and corrects it when the sheets come in — two figures on one line is not a mistake and nobody strikes one out — and nine districts go down in six minutes out of the slate, which is the ten-year figure keyed by number and not by name because a book keyed by number cannot be read out. The third terrace has not returned for two years, *cause not known, no officer has standing to inquire*, and the figure has been carried forward two years running. **Neris Kade comes in at the third bell with returns under her arm, certifies four sheets, says the third terrace has not sent her a return either and her report is not a return and goes nowhere — smaller and quieter than anything in batch 0001, no first names, no thanks — and goes back out.** Lenge rules the foot with no total because there never has been one. Forty-one lines, four terraces inside one of them, and no form in which a terrace is a line. The column is full and the book will close and none of it can be used.

### Chapter 119 — Whether an Unreadable Mark Is a Witness
**The sixteenth of the rains month, a room built for thirty with nine in it, the Trust's seat empty for the fifth time.** Bel Harrow chairs, does not vote in a money matter, has no clerk, and serves the minutes himself at about four minutes a page. The four terms stand; Vendl declines money for the sitting a third time in the first person and is still in the room. Marek asks the one question: is a mark no officer can read a witness to the paper it is on. Ferrow answers no in one line and says the room is really being asked whether four people are the right four, which no form can declare. **The board cannot answer and writes that it was unable to find upon the instrument, and the Chair adds the volume's institutional sentence in a minute that goes into a locked box in a drawer: a mark that no officer can read is not a witness.** Kern takes term four in his own words at a cost — the yard's rule, twice refused on its own reason, now said in a Bell Stair record where a man in a good coat can produce it at the yard in nine months — and asks that it stay in the box and not go on a nail. Nobody is thanked.

### Chapter 120 — The Two Halves of It
**The fourth of the growth month, the Elmsworth seed house with the lamp on since the third of the third month, sixty-one trays now in the light.** Sabin Rell comes down the river, measures the kept-back seed himself as *present* and not *sufficient*, sits on a sack, and says the two halves before anybody asks: **right that the basin feeds three regions on a season granted in writing for good reasons and nobody stole a harvest; wrong that the ten-year mean is a measurement, because it is a number made out of ten numbers in a table he read once and cannot produce, and the third terrace's carried-forward figure proves it.** Taking the advance back is correct, cheap, quick, and puts eleven thousand and some hundreds of pounds of bad autumn onto four terraces — and the only reason it is not the answer is that the money is already in seed and labour and cannot be un-spent, and no office can un-spend a pound. He writes the seed house's name and Hana Belic's in his own depot book to send a letter with a price and a quantity for a shilling that nobody has to answer, and it is not a favour and not a finding. Marek holds back the sentence about the form and the hand and is not asked for it. **Rell is not censured, does not resign, learns nothing about a mark, and is not softened.** The batch ends on a working fact: the bench book takes the count with nothing at the head of it, and the register opens at the fourth bell with the fifth line empty.

### Chapter 121 — What a Season Is For
**The sixth of the growth month, the Elmsworth seed house at night, the lamp burning since the third of the third month, sixty-one trays.** Marek comes up with a question and asks that he be stopped when he is doing it. **The effect's own ground is the basin and Rainmarket is in the basin, and a germination bench in the low ground is inside its scope the way a road is inside a road's scope — which is why a tray answered, and why nothing in four hundred acres above a stone bridge would answer until somebody said a name that meant a person.** **Three attempts and two of them are nothing:** a tray of sand (nothing), the floor under the sacks of kept-back seed (nothing), and then a third attempt that works on the wrong object. **The working attempt is four words different from the two failures — a name, and then *the season you have got*, not *this season, this lot, here*.** It puts a quarter of an inch of something dark into nine months of dead sand in the eleventh tray, once, and cannot be repeated. Hana Belic's sentence is the chapter: *You have got a hand and you have not got a person.* And Fisk's is the boundary: a seed house knows what a tray is worth and does not know what a household is worth. **The four findings, in order:** the effect is not a season, it is an effect somebody obtained in writing, on a form, in advance, and it is still travelling because the paper says *settlement: out of the harvest*; Marek has been *sighting* where an authorised effect stands and has spent four months believing that seeing was knowing, and **a sighting cannot be entered in a column in this kingdom**; the tray was the wrong target because the effect does not want to be told to arrive where it already is; and he needs a person's name and has not got one. He writes nothing down and would not have been able to. **The effect's own ground is the basin, and Rainmarket is in the basin, and a germination bench in the low ground is inside its scope the way a road is inside a road's scope — which is why a tray answered, and why nothing in four hundred acres above a stone bridge would answer until somebody said a name that meant a person.**

### Chapter 122 — The Woman Who Has to Be Asked
**The seventh of the growth month, a yard on the second terrace, the fourth bell.** He opens with a prepared sentence, hears it come out sounding like a man explaining a rule to a queue, and stops after nine words. **Zofia Teran will not say a name, and gives two reasons, both right: a woman named out loud in a yard with nine people in it stops being one household and starts being the one everybody thinks about — the neighbours' kindness, the depot's price, a cart when there is not a cart; and if she names somebody she has decided who a class lands on, and a class of four hundred and some people is the one part of the paper nobody signed away.** She also refuses the book and says why in a form nobody can check: *it is not a record, there is no name at head or foot, it lives in an apron, and what she was told is not what was said.* **The question that makes her give a name is hers, not his — *who is going to tell her*** — and Marek's answer is that there is nobody, because there is no form on which a person may be told a season has moved. He says the true thing out loud in her yard on her condition, before she will answer, and it comes out badly. She names **Stana Peric**, thirty-one, second terrace, on her mother's line, five children, eldest nine, nine of the thirty-nine lots that came up as sand, **and she has no kept-back store of her own, because a kept-back store has a holder and a holder is a person and the holder is her mother, fifty-eight, who does not count.** She will not say why she chose that one and he does not ask twice.

### Chapter 123 — Nine Lots Come Up
**The ninth of the growth month, third bell, before light, on the second terrace.** He tries four times and fails four times, because he is saying it *to the field*. **What works at about a quarter past the third bell, when the light comes, is that he stops saying the season: he says the name, once, and then says the four words *Stana Peric, and the season you have got*, and a season does not arrive — it takes four days.** Tomas comes up the lane at the fifth bell, does not ask, sits four feet off with a coil of rope between them, and makes him say whether anybody's name is on it; Marek says no, and then twice in one answer tells him whose *words* are in it, and Tomas names that as the fourth time this year and the first one that cost somebody else something instead of him. Tomas also says out loud, in a lane, that the second terrace's wall is not going to be right and he has told nobody and is telling Marek because if it went on a paper it would go up a road in nine months in somebody else's hand with a head at the top of it. **The cost is a season and not a person: the coat on the chair.** Four times in two days he cannot produce his own habits without arriving at them, and the finding is that it is not a hole — it is a season of a man's *hand*, a use rather than a fact. **He adds four words in pencil under his own figures on the back of his left hand: *season, third bell, four days*** — not a document, not for anybody.

### Chapter 124 — The Ground That Does Not Answer
**The twelfth of the growth month, the second and the first terraces, and one corner of the low ground.** Nine rows come up; about forty people stand in a field to look at a crop come up, which is the most remarkable thing that ground has held. Stana Peric's mother does not count, which is a compliment. **The store with a lock is not going to be emptied by the set-off, and nobody can say why, and the woman has decided not to think about it until the seed is in and shut. The eighth line of the season works carries her mother's name, not hers, because a store room with a lock has a holder and a holder is a person.** Four miles away on the first terrace **Mileva Vukic, thirty-eight**, has four ordinary things go wrong: a spring that has come up in that field every year of her life comes up brackish; a forty-year-old pear comes out in leaf a second time; a boundary stone has moved nine inches in ground nobody touched; a heifer does not come back in heat. **The jar on the rail is the loneliest object in the basin, because none of it has happened yet** — a wall falls in a year and a man in a good coat can be shown it, and next year's ground cannot be entered, served, sworn to, or shown anywhere in this kingdom. She walks four miles on the seventeenth and asks Zofia Teran to write the day down; Teran writes four lines with nothing at the head of them and reads them back once, **and says out loud that the answer to *was the first terrace's water bad* is that a woman she has known nineteen years told her so, and there is no form on which she can be served with the question.** Teran tells Marek on the eighteenth, having come out of the door with the book, and says one thing only: *somebody came down off the first terrace on the seventeenth with a thing that has got nothing to do with your ground, and I put it in the book, and in about four weeks somebody is going to say look at the day, and there is no form on which a book of mine can be put next to a thing of yours.* Cutaway: **the chaff stall on the low-ground road has been shut four days running** and Margit Sova, forty-one, writes it on her list with nothing at the head of it and tells nobody, and the rate that has stood since the second month is now a rate about a stall with nothing on it.

### Chapter 125 — The Silence in the Shed
**The fifteenth of the growth month, the Gate shed in Tallow Ward, the fourth bell, and Kade's entry on the wall list.** **Marek is the reader on the rota that day, and it is the only line on that wall in anybody's first person, with her name and her day at the foot.** (No ordinal position is claimed for it on the page: the yard has three referents for a fourth line — the form's own line for anything anybody else has to say, the empty fourth line of a refused class counterweight in Ch 108, and this — and the chapter does not add a fourth.) **The chapter is that the silence after the second reading is a different silence from the silence after the first:** the first is four hundred people hearing a thing for the first time and it goes past them like weather; the second is a room that has heard the same sentence on almost every morning since the register opened and has stopped finding it remote. **Nine or ten of the twenty people in that shed are on the fourth column of a Crown March form.** A woman of about fifty with a rope asks the question nobody has ever heard asked at that counter — *where is she, then* — and Marek's answer is that there is no form on which anybody may be applied for. **The chapter's finding is his own, in about nine seconds, alone, with his hand on the form:** the fifth line is empty and the hours in it would have been hers and the yard has refused to fill it twice on the yard's own rule and the yard is right — **and the whole of what he has done this month is that he has turned a class of four hundred and some people into one woman with five children, and a man with a season can do that and nothing in this kingdom can.** The cost of the chapter is an omission: he does not go up the road, for the first time since the seventh, and the reader is not told yet that it is a failure.

### Chapter 126 — The Season He Gave Back
**The seventeenth of the growth month, the low ground, the fifth bell, a drill row and a ditch.** The second sowing is thin. He works out the four words over four days and knows he must not conclude that the basin is owed this therefore the basin may have it. **He aims at the basin and the basin does not come up, four times, and then the ditch goes out from under him in a way that is not the ground:** the hoe, the gate latch, the weight of a bucket, the way a woman turns a sack off her hip, the sound a scythe makes going well — **all gone, all not his, all shaped like a habit that was never his.** That is the second direction and it attaches to him. **Neris Kade is forty feet away with her satchel and watches all of it** and comes over and does not ask what the matter is. Both halves, neither thanked, nothing built: *you have done the one thing in this basin that there is no form for, and the class was the only honest thing on that paper* — **against** *and there is no office that can send a person up a road to ask a household whether it can spare anything, and you are the only person in this basin who has ever tried to fix anything of this shape at all.* **The whole of his defence is seven words long and he has had nine days to find an eighth and there is not one.** Nothing is to be done tonight. She says she will be at the low ground on the twenty-second. No first names. The chapter also has **the harbour letter: it went on the seventh of the second month, the post goes out on the ninth, and about six weeks put it in somebody's hand — that was the third month, and it is the seventeenth of the growth month, and there is nothing on the shelf.** He works out that a thing not coming back is not a no, that not one thing between tonight and the fourth month turns on the answer, and does not go and look. **The loss in this chapter is a stranger's season, not his own: on the ninth of the growth month what he lost was his own boiler, his own door, his own ledger, and that had been a bill. This is not a bill.**

### Chapter 127 — She Will Not Say Whose It Is
**The eighteenth and nineteenth of the growth month, the seed house again.** **Mirren Fisk sends for Sera Holt and she comes to the Elmsworth seed house in the low ground, which she has never once been in, and gives the reason at the door: she has signed things about sixty-one women for two years without ever having seen the bench, and that is not a thing a physician is entitled to do.** Belic's cough is in the muscle and not the chest; she is written out of the evening trays for eleven days and no more, and the certificate becomes a cost on a member's ground that has no box for a cost either. **Then Marek.** She does not take his wrist. She asks him what he did the day before yesterday and then the day before that, and he cannot produce his own coat on a chair, and she says: *you have not had a hole. That is worse.* A hole is a payment; what he has is a season with a person still in it. **She gives him one of five things and keeps four:** he can see that it is not his own, and she will not say what it is, where it came from, whose hands are in it, how much there is, or whether it comes down. **She has seen something with this shape before and will not say how many times or whether any of them is standing in a room somewhere.** She writes one line and a date on a clinic sheet and makes a small hooked stroke with a short bar under it at the left margin, **a character that is not in any of the four books a physician keeps, invented three times and thrown away twice**, and puts it back in her bag and does not give it to him. **The reason for the refusal is the volume's own rule turned on him: no office in this kingdom is going to say out loud that a season may be given to a person by a man who has not asked that person, and not one of them is a coward.** And she closes on the fifth line: the yard is right, the yard is right for a reason that has nothing to do with him, and **she is not going to be the third voice that fills it.**

### Chapter 128 — Three Days at the Third Terrace
**The second to the sixth of the harvest month.** The low-ground water stood since the growth month because a thin sowing takes its water in a thin way; the flood-year channel at the foot of the third terrace is four feet deep at the bend and rising a hand an hour; **the third terrace's cutting is line one and a line is a job and there is no form on which anybody may be sent to ask a terrace why it has sent nobody.** Nine men go up on their own time with their own food. **Tomas builds a thing across the channel to get a bar to the field, in nine hours, and does not say so.** Four people from the third terrace are on the ground and **Marek does not ask any of them anything, at any hour, on any of the three days**, and it is on the record because it is the only correct thing he does that week. Janko Vrabec comes up with a bar and nobody asks why. **The hold: his hand flat on the bank and the water stops, and it is boring — no heat, no light, no sound — and the whole of what he gets to look at is nine men with scythes. He chooses the length and not the destination, and the water is not gone, it is held the way a door is held.** He does not let go for two and a half days — from about the sixth bell on the second to about the fourth on the fifth — and a man who sleeps is a hand that is not there. Somebody brings bread and a pot up on the second night. Tomas brings a coat at about the second bell on the fourth and Marek does not put it on and Tomas does not ask, and that was the whole of the conversation the two of them had in three days. **Nobody is charged, and the reason is not an arrangement and not a kindness: the cost of a hold can never reach a person who did not borrow, guarantee, witness, or certify, and not one of the nine men with scythes did, and not one of the four people on the third terrace did, and no acre of that ground has ever borrowed anything off anybody — and he is the only party, and he made himself the only party on the ninth.** The cutting is the best-cut ground any of them had ever done. Tomas will not speak of the three days to anybody, and Marek understands halfway through the third night that he is going to have to tell his brother and that there is no form on which to do it and the only place left in this basin to say a thing like that is a lane with nobody in it, and that his brother will be on a road in about nine hours with a case in his hand. **Janko's whole speech is about the ninth line of the works and the key and the fact that neither of them has asked the other, twice noticed.** Released on the fifth; the water comes back at the rate it had been coming and is four feet up the slope by about the ninth bell on the sixth, which is three days and four hours later than it would have been and is the same four feet; he cannot for about four seconds produce his brother's face and then it comes.

### Chapter 129 — A Brother, a Case, and a Refusal
**The sixth and seventh of the harvest month, a bend on the road four miles above a stone bridge, then a shed eleven feet by nine.** Marek carries his brother's thirty-one-year-old tool case up eleven switchbacks and says **"Before,"** and Tomas Vale says "What," and does not interrupt. **Four minutes in a lane, in order, no editorialising and not one *sorry*:** the ninth at the third bell, the ditch on the seventeenth, the physician's line and invented mark, the three nights on a bank. Tomas puts the bar down, which he has not done in as long as Marek can remember, and says *that is what I asked for* — and then makes him hear that the word **right** was not in it. **Three things, and none to be written down.** The second terrace's wall is going in about nine years and a weep in it is a thing that has to stay in a margin on a board in a loft because a paper outlives the men on it. The fourth-terrace store is not open, the key is in a coat, and a man with a key in his hand in a valley with no form in it has no answer and Tomas does not expect one. And line four: eleven stakes, a rope, a culvert four feet below its invert, and in about nine years a bad winter somebody will decide about the road — *if there is a board in a loft then I will be forty-three and I will be standing on the road.* Then: **carry that back down and do not say one word about it.** **In the shed, Marek asks to enter a line about his own paper and has neither a form nor a paper**, and Sanne Droll puts the chalk down: the rules are about paper, and he has not brought paper. *Suppose I write it on a sheet of my own.* Then the four questions, and the fourth — the one nobody has ever heard her ask — is **is there a person behind it who is in this room**, and the answer is no, because the person behind it is four hundred and some people on four terraces and about forty people are in the shed and none of them are on four terraces. **It is the first line the yard has ever refused him, and the yard refused it and not anybody.** Then **Petar Duss**, four months with the paper in his coat, asks for a line about his own paper and is refused too — **and the reason is the mark: nine letters in a corner and a man in two rooms over a shop who will not say who he is.** Droll says plainly that this is a new call she has never had to make, that she does not know she is right and has not checked, and what she has checked is that the mark is the only part of the sheet with a person in it. Duss asks for the refusal itself to be written down and the yard cannot, because a written refusal is a list and a list needs a head and there is a column on that wall with nothing at the head of it. **Teodor Lask, nineteen, reads the refusal out loud twice at the ordinary speed, and the two men it belongs to stand eleven feet apart and say nothing to each other.** Duss says thank you, and it was not for the boy.

### Chapter 130 — The Two Harvests
**The ninth to the eleventh of the harvest month.** **The carts turned round**, and that is the whole of what happened in Rainmarket in a week. **Kuez reads the same rate to a cart going up with straw and a cart coming down with barley**, and the rate does not fall and does not rise, and nine people in a lane do not go and look at the bridge book, and that has never happened in nine years. The second sowing did not come, about a fifth of the first did not, and the third was thin; there is no announcement because no office sends anybody and no form exists on which a person may be told a field was paid for a loan it did not know it was on. Duss is hauling straw up and says nothing about either thing and **the office in the hills will want him in a room in about five weeks.** **The four terraces have a harvest**: brackish ground on the first, three days on a shared floor, the third terrace cut by nine men who do not live on it and enterable nowhere, and the fourth terrace's one sentence about nine warm days. **Lenge comes up on the tenth and asks, before the road, whether he should write the good harvest or the bad one first, and says the answer before Marek has finished: a book with one wrong number in it can be corrected in four seconds with two initials, and a book with a hole in it is a book that says out loud the office cannot do its job.** He serves the returns in a yard and reads it well and reads it twice, and Zofia Teran says *you have written a good year* and he says he has written what the terraces sent, correctly, and that the correctness is his whole trade and also the whole of the difficulty. **Then the finding, at the culvert: one line, four terraces inside it, no form in which a terrace is a line, a district that has come in short and four terraces that have come in well, and nowhere to put the second thing.** *Neither of them is where the paper said.* The paper put a district's number on four terraces; the four terraces did well; the district is short; the paper put a class on a form and the four hundred and some are going into a store with a lock; **and the eleven thousand and some hundreds is already in the ground, in the best of the four terraces, put there in the second month of last year by men doing their jobs.** At the second bell on the eleventh Lenge writes the true figure in beside the ten-year figure with his initials and leaves the ten-year figure on the page, **two figures on one line is not a mistake**, and rules the foot with no total, and puts the slate on top of the bound book. **Neither figure can be used, neither can leave the room, and by the fourth month a district will have a rate on it, and a rate goes on a person with a name, and there are about nine names available, and one of them is a haulier who cannot get a line in and is going to be in a room in the hills in about five weeks.**

### Chapter 131 — The Bar That Does Not Sit Flat
**The twelfth of the harvest month, the Gate yard in Tallow Ward, after the register shuts at the fourth bell.** The bar across the yard's store has never sat flat in nine years because the door has dropped a quarter of an inch on its hinge, so there is a wedge under one end and a padlock through the chain at the other, and the key is on a nail inside an office. **Milan Vray, the yard-man, will not let him begin.** "You cannot go and tell a whole terrace what a counterweight is." Marek explains a mechanism and is stopped inside four minutes. He puts **the four questions at the yard's counter** on the trestle with his thumb — *is that your line, is that the person behind it, is it your words,* and the fourth, *is there a person behind it who is in this room* — and Vray tells him what he already knows: the first three want a piece of paper a man brought in about himself, the fourth wants nine people in a room, and **there is no office in this kingdom that can put nine people in a room**, and the office that most nearly could is a granary board a night and a day up the river. He asks for the yard to be the room and is refused. Vray takes the bar off, lifts it a quarter of an inch, and the padlock comes out of the hasp in one movement: **a counterweight is not written down, it is held, the way that door is held** — somebody keeps it, the way a store room with a lock has a keyholder, and a keyholder is a person, and a person can be asked. Marek offers to hold it himself, and that is refused for the sharper reason: **a keyholder is not a name, it is a person who can be asked, and there would be nobody to ask.** A name on a list turns a store into something and makes a yard-man into an office; the key is on a nail and nowhere else, and that is why the store is safe and the paper is not. And the two look identical from a doorway. **The last of it, hands-first: *a counterweight is not a receipt* — a receipt is a thing you hand a man when he has handed you something, and there is nothing in four terraces that anybody handed anybody**, the nine words were printed before the four terraces had a bad year, the only hand that ever touched it is a man in a room in the hills filling in a number of holdings, correctly. So there is nothing to receipt, and a weight sitting on a floor is picked up by deciding to, out loud, in front of the people it will land on, and **there is no form for deciding and no office that will send anybody to watch you do it, and if you do it anyway then you did it, and nobody can prove it, and you will not be able to prove it either.** He goes up the road with a sentence that is not his own and is about a nail.

### Chapter 132 — Nine People and a Jar
**The fourteenth of the harvest month, a threshing floor at the top of the first terrace's upper lot.** The jar comes back out on to the rail nine days after Mileva Vukic carried it inside. She is thirty-eight, not a member of anything, and there is no cooperative on the first terrace and no roll with her name on it. The floor is not the shared floor (that is line three, on the second terrace); they thresh where the barley is stacked, and **nine people stop for two seconds when a stranger with a bag comes in and then go back to it.** He eats bread and cheese at the wall for nine minutes first, because she will not say the other thing until he has eaten. Then he says the true part in four minutes, flat and in the wrong order, with no sentence in it he wrote down, including that he pointed a season at a household on the second terrace at the third bell before light in the growth month and did not ask. **She says no, and says it in the plainest words she has**, and will not let him leave thinking she is going to say yes in a week. *What I am going to do is leave the jar on the rail… it is not acceptance, and it is not a favour to you.* And the part he did not come for: **the paper does not land on me, and if you go down that road and say *the paper did that*, then in nine years a man in a good coat will come to this floor with a figure, and I will have said it first, and I will not have earned it, and my girl will have heard me say it.** So he is not to say it. Not on the second terrace, not to the examiner, not in Bell Stair, not to a woman with a book. The nine words alone are worth something; the nine words plus a cause are worth nothing. He makes her show him the spring — he puts his hand in it, twice, and it is not the water of a drain or a river — and when she asks what he will write down, he says nothing, because a sheet with nothing at the head of it hands a man in a good coat four causes to choose between and he will choose the cheapest to act on. **She asks the only question she asks all afternoon: how long does it last, because she would like to know how long she has to be the woman with the jar.** *About eleven weeks… the woman who has been in a room about it says that is about how long anything of this shape survives being told once.* *Then I will know by the season when it has stopped, and I will not have to ask you, and you will not have to come up here and find out.* He then stands at the rail for an hour and a half and carries nine pairs of sheaves to the headland because a man of about fifty says *you can take that end if you want it*, and nobody asks him a question. **No hand in it at all.**

### Chapter 133 — I Was Not in the Room
**The sixteenth of the harvest month, the second terrace, a shoulder above the new drain.** Nine rows in a shock four feet high, carried down on shoulders to the headland because a fresh drain has made the shoulder uncarryable by cart. Zofia Teran puts the child's coat on the rail — the only accommodation she has made for anybody in three visits — and stops him four lines in. **She will not write the nine words down**, and not because a paper outlives the men on it: the day those words are in a book in this valley, somebody in nine months says *the second terrace said the nine words* in a room, and then nobody on this terrace can ever be told anything again. So the nine words have to live in his mouth, and a mouth is a poor place and does not last about four days. Then the refusal, on a working day with six men on her shoulder. **The ninth of the growth month, the third bell, a lamp he did not light, nine rows on a shoulder above a drain, the ground a woman of thirty-one's and held in her mother's name, and he asked neither of them, and one of them does not know it happened at all** — and nine people on the terrace worked that out in four days without him explaining a word, because nine rows came up on ground that has never grown nine rows at once and the drain was cut in the third month and a woman with a book walked round every one of them. *And I did not come down your road. That was yours to do and you have not done it.* He says he is sorry; she will not let him, because she would have to spend the morning deciding whether to believe him and she has a shoulder to get off. **The ground you want is not mine** — it is on a board in a loft four miles up the road in the name of a woman of fifty-eight, and she is the mother of the woman whose ground he put it in, and she does not count in this valley, and Teran has been to her door. So the person to ask is her, and asking her is asking the woman, and asking the woman is asking me in front of nine people. And she was the woman somebody comes down the hill to since the ninth month of last year and nine households on this terrace have been named in a yard by somebody with a book, and not one by her. **And then the plainest sentence in the batch, in two parts, flat, with a man carrying a sheaf past them: *I was not in the room. You were in a ditch.*** *No. We are not going to be the people who sign for the next one. Not this season and not the next.* They cut the shock off the shoulder that afternoon and the barley does not know what was said in a yard. Stana Peric is on the headland all day with a child on her hip and says nothing about the yard and has never said anything about the yard. **Nobody says the word *failure*, nobody thanks him, and the sentence he came up with is shorter than it was when he came in the gate and the one he takes down will be shorter still.**

### Chapter 134 — The Line He Could Not Fill
**The eighteenth of the harvest month, Hartlow, the fourth-terrace seed store and the loft over it.** The bar lies flat, the door has not dropped, and there is a padlock as well because a bar is a thing that can be lifted and a padlock is not; **the keyway is bright at the edge and black in the middle, which is what eleven years of a key every morning looks like.** The key is on a hook inside a seed room and the seed room hook has a coat on it, and about nine people in the valley know where it is and not one of them has ever been asked. He puts the point of a pencil on the ninth line of the works board and holds it there for as long as it takes to count nine and does not write, and nobody sees it. **Ruta Vrabec reads the board from the bottom up the way she reads it every year, eight lines, and stops at the ninth and says its name, which is a seed store, and says nothing else.** The ninth line is the only line on that board that has ever had a person's name cut into it, because a store with a lock has a holder and a holder is a person and a person can be asked; the space is the right shape and there is a knife-cut round where a name was and there is no name in it. **And the ninth line is not empty in function: the kept-back seed is coming off the first on the sixth or seventh, off the second on the ninth, and Hartlow's own last, four days of carting, and it wants a dry floor, a roof and a lock and it does not want a bar** — a bar is for a thing you are hiding, and that store is holding next year's seed, which is the only thing in this valley anybody in the hills has ever been sure about. Then the ground she hands back: **he went up this road on the seventh of the growth month and looked at that ground and came back and said nothing, and on the second of the harvest month he stood on a bank for two and a half days with his hand on a channel and four people on that ground watched the water not move, and he has said nothing about either.** *So go down and do it now if you like. There is nothing to stop you.* He does. **He goes down the ladder and out to the lane fork, and the lower fork goes to the third terrace, and he stands at an unfinished stone wall about chest high for as long as it takes to count nine, and he turns round and goes back up.** There was nobody watching and no office that would have known, and he could have said nine words to four people and come back up before the light went. **And then he stands in the loft and works out that he has a season in him and four people below him whose ground it partly came out of, and that he could name a name and it would go, and that he is not going to, and that this is the first time in four months that the thing he was carefully given has been no use to him at all.** Janko comes out of the seed room at the sixth bell, is civil, asks whether he came up the road or over the shoulder, and says one ordinary thing about seed and one thing about the door: *I have not opened it since the second month, and it is not shut, and the key has not moved, and I will tell you the same thing about that on another day and you may come back for it.*

### Chapter 135 — A Dying Man Is Not a Party
**The nineteenth of the harvest month, the seed room at Hartlow, a lamp with a very small wick in it.** Janko Vrabec is turning the wick up a quarter turn and putting it back a quarter turn, which is the whole of what a man does with a lamp he is not going to leave burning at full and not going to let go out. There is no work in that room for a man in a coat and he does it anyway: **he fills cones from Hartlow's own stock for an hour with Ruta Vrabec at the other end of the bench, and he is slow because he counts, and she says *You will be here till noon at that, and the count is not in the cone*, and he stops counting and is still slow, and he carries the tray back and stands it down in the gap he made.** He asks, standing up, and gets **"No" in about a second and a half**, with the rest of it in his mouth and the man at the table not looking up and not filling the silence. Then, in nine words, the third refusal and a different one: **"A dying man is not a party to anything."** He will give no second speech. The only thing in the conversation that is his business is a list: if his name goes in the ninth line in the second month, then in the eleventh month a name has to be struck off it, and **there is no box in this kingdom for striking a man's name off a list, and no room, and no office that has ever had to do it**, so the line would be emptied in a loft by whoever was in the loft, with a knife or a wet cloth, and badly. **He gets up, opens the seed room door, stands in it with his hand on the coat on the hook, does not take the key off the hook, and shuts it again.** And then the ninth line is a hole with a scrape through it, which is worse than an empty line, and the empty line at least was honest — and you cannot tell anybody anything through it, because a dead man cannot be asked whether he will bear it and cannot say no and cannot be told afterwards. *Three days of a road, and you have built a room with a person in it, and you have put a grave in it.* He says tell them, and it is true and he would like it told, because he has been sitting on that yard for two months waiting for somebody to need an answer about that line and could not say so without sounding like a man refusing work. **Marek has a second question and does not ask it** — *is there anybody in this valley who could be asked instead of you* — because it was for him and not for Janko, and a man refused four times in seven days does not get to put his own refusal in somebody else's mouth, and neither of them says a word about the not asking. Then the roll, off its nail, on the table: **six lines — four women over fifty, one a child, and a sixth line carrying a season examiner's name who is not a member and has never been up the road.** **Two people on that paper are going to bear what Marek's paper bears, and the count is the only number in this valley anybody has ever been able to put on a counterweight, and it does not help.** One of the two is on the board and is fifty-eight and Marek is not going to ask her. **The other is nineteen, is a person, and is not a line on that roll because he is a man and there is no form for him either** — which he told a man in a good coat in the tenth month of last year, who wrote nothing down, and he has been glad of that for months. *I can count and I cannot ask. Those are two different trades and I only have one of them and I have had it for thirty-one years.* The roll goes back on its nail, the cones go on being filled, and at the eighth bell he goes and looks at the bar the way he has most mornings for eleven years, to see whether the door had dropped any further. It had not.

### Chapter 139 — Five Ruled Lines
**The twentieth of the harvest month, about the third bell, the copy room on the hill in Bell Stair.** The windows face east across nine feet of yard and the light does not last past the fifth, and Sedge has the lamp out early and his own shadow goes across the paper every time he moves it. Marek comes down eleven switchbacks in the wet and puts a piece of paper on the bench: *I want a form for a rate on a member's ground.* Sedge refuses to touch it for four minutes, because *what is it about* is not *what is it for*, and the man who cuts a form has to know the second one. Then he rules four lines and explains each: the thing in the person's own words; who holds the other end and since when; **the return in kind, in kind, and a figure is not a return in kind and a figure is not going in this line**; and anything anybody else has to say, which is very often nothing and is not a failure of the form. **He will not rule the fifth for another four minutes, and this is the chapter.** A form is not a hole — the yard's own sixth line is empty for ever and is one of the best pieces of paper in the kingdom. **What is at the head of a line is a kind, and a kind is chosen by the man who cuts the form and not by the person stood in front of it.** A rate is a charge on a ground; a ground is four miles off and under nine people's feet and cannot be told anything, cannot be asked, cannot refuse, and cannot be in a chair with a hand on it. So if he heads the fifth line, the head is a kind, and if the kind is *ground* then he has filed a charge against a class — **and he has done it with a pencil and a straight-edge in a copy room with a lamp at his elbow, and nobody will ever be able to say who did it, because there will be no name on it.** The way he is not going to do it is by having no word there at all, and he has never once left a line bare that somebody could put a head on afterwards, and he has not been sure about this one for a day and a half, **and that is how he knows it is the right one and also how he knows it is the wrong one and he cannot separate the two and neither can Marek.** Then his line, third use, and it costs him: **a form has to be cut by a man who does not know whether it is right.** It comes out flat, and he stops. *Three times. Once in a yard in Tallow Ward to about a hundred and forty people and it was the best thing I have ever said in my life. Once in a printer's shop under a chandler's yard with my hand flat on a margin, and it was nearly as good. And once this afternoon to a man in a wet coat, and it has come out like a rule out of a book.* A man who has said a thing three times has stopped being able to say it fresh, and a man who has stopped being able to say it fresh is a man who ought not to be cutting anything at all, **and he is cutting one, and he would like it said out loud in the room by somebody, because if nobody says it then it is a thing he has noticed on his own and he has never been able to trust a thing he has noticed on his own.** He rules the fifth line, evenly, in four seconds — the same straight-edge from the same edge of the sheet, same space above and below — and the evenness is the technique: **if the fourth is tighter or the fifth is looser, he has told the room which line he thought was the important one, and the only man qualified to say which is important is the person who fills it in, and he is not in this room and has not read it yet.** It is not the yard's form and does not go near it, the yard's form has six lines and he is not cutting a seventh, it is not a copy of the yard's form, and the difference between them is that the yard's was cut for a person about themselves and this one is cut for a charge, and he does not know which is worse to cut a form for. **"I have been in this building long enough that twice in it, and I do not know whether that is right."** Marek puts two fingers on the bare fifth line and understands that the absence is the only part of the five lines cut by a man who understood the thing, and that in about two months he will have to say the same thing a second time to a woman who told him it would only be told once.

### Chapter 136 — The Box That Is Stock
**The third and fourth of the ninth month, the Crown March granary depot at Verrow.** The tide went up on the twenty-second of the harvest month and he was on the water a night and a day; **he does not go up to see Sabin Rell, who is in the building, because a man with no office who climbs to the officer who signed the paper is asking for something and there is no form on which the asking can be entered.** **Damir Pribis, forty-nine, eleven years opening the basket at Verrow**, describes himself as the man who opens the basket, is not a third-grade clerk and never claimed to be, and says *sit down, you are the first man to come up that road in about two years who was not selling me something, and I have got about nine minutes before the next cart.* Sixty-one returns came back this season, sixty went into a press last week. **He reads them for two things: a figure that is not a figure, and a date that is not a date.** Box one is the party, box two the advance, box three the delivery or a rule at the foot, and the fifth at the foot is a return, the oldest box on the form, with a hundred and about forty entries in eleven years and never anything else. **"And the fourth is the counterweight… and I have never once had a question about it. And the reason is not that the fourth box is careful. The reason is that the fourth box has never once asked me anything."** A clerk reads a form by the heads of its boxes; the head of the fourth box is a kind; a kind is not a hole; **you do not go looking for a hole, you go looking for a figure that is wrong, and the fourth box does not have a figure that can be wrong.** And he gives the reader the half that makes a good story: **a name is in the first box sixty-one times, and it is the first box with the person taken out of it that the fourth box prints** — and he took four seconds to believe it and would like it entered that he took four seconds. **But nobody ever moves a name from one to the other**, because a name in the party box would say that person is the counterweight, and a granary certifies paper and a number of holdings, and the moment a party box has to mean *this person is the counterweight* then somebody has to accept that a person is a counterweight, and no office in this kingdom has a box for that. **A box with a class in it gets a person sixty-one times, four inches away, in a different box, doing a different job, and there is no way on that sheet to tell the two apart.** Asked whether a translator's name is ever checked: *we are not the harbour and we cannot read one and have never claimed to* — and Marek does not tell him that this is the answer to a letter on a shelf since the seventh of the second month, and works out for the second time that a thing not coming back is not a no, and does not go and look. One other thing takes nine seconds: **the coming season's projection column has two figures on one line, the ten-year and the true one with Lenge's initials against it.** Neither can be produced, neither can be used, the ten-year is on a table in a room in that building and is not permitted out of it, and Marek wanted a figure since the ninth of the second month and got the only one anybody in this basin has ever been given and can do nothing with it. Pribis does not remark on it and Marek does not ask. He carries the bundle of sixty-one out of the yard and the fourth box asks him nothing on any of them, and the tide was going down.

### Chapter 137 — The Fourth Line From the End
**The ninth of the ninth month, the Elmsworth seed house in the low ground.** The floor is swept twice a day by a girl of about thirteen who is not a member and is paid by the day, and she sweeps the chaff and the sand and then sweeps four inches of it back under the bench, and nobody in the building has ever mentioned it. **Sixty-one trays in two ranks under oiled paper, the lamp in since the third month, the count onto a bench book with nothing at the head of it, and the days onto an oiled board with nothing at the head of it** — put on by **Petrik, a member's son of about eleven, with a chalk**, whose hand gets better and then worse across the week, and whom nobody has ever told what to do about the top of the board and nobody is going to. **The caps are on a nail by the door, sixty-one of them, each with a name in the band in Fisk's hand, written in the seed month because the house is cold in the dark and because a cap with a name in the band is the only thing in this basin that looks exactly like an answer and is not one.** The cap of the fourth line from the end of the roll is on the bench and the girl filling cones at that bench is wearing it, and its twin has been on the nail since the bad autumn, and nobody has ever moved it and nobody has ever asked. **Fisk states the arrangement before Marek has got nine feet off the door, unprompted and without preamble: there is one thing in this building that nobody is going to ask her, it is not a rule and not a favour, and she is telling him so that nobody in the room has to be careful and nobody gets praised for it later.** The one thing is whether the balance on the fourth line from the end is hers — not Marek's, not a fund's, not a clerk's, not a man from the hills, not Fisk in nine months, not the mother asking from a bed, not the boy with the chalk. **And not because she is sixteen: because a balance belongs to a ground, the ground is the woman's, the woman is a person, the girl is a person, and there is no place in this kingdom where those two get added into one thing that can be asked — the one place they get added is on the Share's own rule, and the rule is not asked anything, and that is the whole of why it is dangerous.** Marek, who is asked nothing and told nothing, works out that the only real question is a line about a rate on a member's ground. Then it is played, twice, without a word of commentary: **Petrik asks *Whose tray is the twenty-third for the last day*, and Fisk answers at the ordinary volume without turning round or looking at the bench — *The fourth line from the end. It is the same as it was yesterday. Put the slate down and bring me the twine* — and Petrik puts the slate down, and the girl does not stop and does not look up, and if you had asked any four people in that building what had just happened, three would have told you about the twine.** And the sand: Fisk tells the girl with the broom, in front of the man in the wet coat, not to sweep it at the end of the day, because it comes back and it does not matter and she will not have anybody stood about counting it in the last hour — *I am saying it out loud because a man in a wet coat has been looking at the floor for a quarter of an hour and I would rather he heard it from me than worked it out and asked.* **Wenna Drel's one ordinary thing, at the sixth bell, without stopping and without looking up: *That is what the twenty-third wants* — twenty-two are the same as yesterday and the twenty-third wants turning at the fourth bell, not the third, and the board has said the third for the rest of them since the second month. Petrik turns it at the fourth. It is right.** That is the whole of what she says in that building that day.

### Chapter 138 — Eleven Lines on a Piece of Sack
**The eleventh of the ninth month, two rooms over a chandler's yard in Rainmarket.** The piece of sack has been in a drawer under a folded apron since the third day of the harvest month last year and she has never taken it out of the front room in front of anybody, and she told a man in this room a year ago that she would not. **She takes it out without preamble, cuts it off a bale two hands wide and a foot and a half long, eleven lines in a hand that is not a clerk's, nothing at the top, nothing at the foot, no column of names, no total, each line a household's rate.** *Do not touch it. There is no order on that and there is no reason for it.* **The rule, stated by the woman who did not write it and cannot unwrite it, nineteen years old and correct:** a lot of ours that does not come up becomes eleven days of labour divided across the members by trays, and if a member's own tray is the one that failed then it is her tray and her days; then a third of another member's kept-back, Ansgar Drel having carried his down on his own back; then the balance as a rate on the member's ground, and **there is no form in this kingdom in which a rate on a member's ground goes, and it goes nowhere.** Eleven lines, the eleven whose trays came up as sand, eight other members owing days on a sheet of days and not on that, and she has never been able to make that sound right to anybody and it is right. **And the finding, with Marek's own sentence against her: a rate is a charge on a ground, and the ground is a piece of the low ground with a roll number on it, and a number about a ground cannot be told anything or asked anything or be in a room — so a man puts a name in, because a charge has to have somebody on it or it is a note, and the name is the name of the woman who holds the ground and she has not agreed to anything, and in about nine months the office will say the holder was notified.** It is on a nail in a seed house four miles and eleven switchbacks up that road and she has read it more than once and cannot improve it. **And the batch's new sentence in a second mouth: *a counterweight is not a receipt*** — a receipt is a thing you hand a man when he has handed you something, and nothing in this basin gave anything to anybody, and so there is no paper to hand a woman saying *here is what you gave and here is what it cost*, and the only thing anybody can do is write a figure on a piece of sack and carry it home in an apron and know that in about two years a woman will be standing where you are standing with a number she cannot pay in money and does not have in seed, and she will be told it is a rule, and she will say yes, because there is nothing else to say — *and that is how it was decided and by whom and on what day is nobody's business to say.*
