**Neris Kade comes in with a satchel and four sheets, puts the satchel on the table, and asks — in the volume's one permitted co-presence — whether she may put her four counter questions to the sack.** She does not pick it up. *May I ask, is that your line.* And she stops. *I cannot. There is nothing at the head of it to ask the question of. That is not a fault in the question. That is the whole of what the question is, and I have been asking it for two years at about nine counters and I have never once had a thing in front of me that I could not put it to, and I have just met one.* She does not ask who wrote it or when, she does not take the sack, she says she is going up the street to certify four sheets at the ordinary speed and good morning, and **nobody thanks her and Marek does not thank her, and there is no note of it anywhere.** Then Marek puts the form on the table — five ruled lines, four heads, nothing at the fifth — and Fisk's thumb goes on the fifth line and stays there. *That is not a hole.* She is glad, and she would like it noticed that she has had a hole in a form in her head for two years and has never been able to say so out loud without it sounding like a complaint, **and that a man she does not know cut it on purpose and left it bare and put a straight-edge on it and has never met her and does not know what it is for, and she is not going to tell him.** She refuses the form on four grounds and the fourth is the real one: eleven days, eleven houses, a kitchen, and a person who has been in that kitchen. *If I do it off a form then I am an office, and the moment I am an office she is being notified, and notified is a different word from told, and the difference is the only thing I have that is worth anything.* She takes it off the table and puts it in front of him and tells him she is going to do a thing in nine days that she has not told him she is going to do, so that he can be in the room and stop her if she is wrong — *I am not going to be wrong, and if I am wrong you are the wrong man for it and I would like you to say so at the time and not afterwards.* He says he will not stop her. *I know. That is why I am telling you.* And then the eleven weeks: a woman with a jar on a rail told him on the fourteenth of the harvest month that the telling lasts about eleven weeks, about a month of that is gone, he has been up that road once since and has not been back, and **he has not the least idea what a man says the second time, and in about nine days he is going to watch somebody else try it.**

### Chapter 140 — Two Women and a Form
**The twentieth of the ninth month, the seed house at about the fifth bell.** Sedge comes down off the causeway the same morning with his coat on, because he has come four miles to watch a thing he cut be used, and says before anybody thinks of it that he is not to be thanked. **Two filled sheets on the bench, four lines in each and the fifth empty, and two women at different hours on purpose, and neither of them knew the other was coming and neither said a word about it and both stayed.** **Ilma Tresch, forty-one, did the eleven days on ground that had already failed and was written out of four of them and then of the rest of them once**, and the certificate went to the clinic board as a cost and the district's answer to a cost is a rate. Her balance is the days she was written out of, and it is all there is in it, and her ground is a lot in the low ground with a roll number on it. She looks at the form for a minute and says *Whose name goes against it*, and when told that she does — because a rate is charged to the ground and the ground is held by a person — says *Then that is right, and I am not going to argue with it, and I have not got anybody to argue with it with, so write it.* Then the second line in her own words, read back at the ordinary speed. **And then the first failure of scale, in her own mouth: there is going to be a rate on this low ground in the fourth month, and the district is short, and there is going to be one of these on her lot — and the two sizes do not meet anywhere, so there will be two charges on one piece of dirt and nobody alive will ever be shown both of them at once, including her, including Fisk, including whoever sent Marek up here. *That is not an objection. I am refusing nothing. I am telling you the size of it.*** And she wants the whole of it minuted that she said the rule was right before she said the other thing. **Hana Belic, thirty-four, drew the eleventh tray and had worked it twice a day in the dark since the sixth of the ninth month, and was written out of four tray-days in the tenth month and not of the other seven, and then written out of the evening trays for eleven days by a physician who came back in the growth month to a cough in the small of her back.** Her balance is the eleven days, she did none of them, and nobody in the room treated it as anything but a certificate. She asks the only question that matters: **who did the work that was not done.** And then the second failure of scale: the trays are the Share's, sixty-one of them and hers is the eleventh, the rule divides a failed lot across the members by trays, eleven trays came up as sand out of a shed shut up wet in the flood year, **the days those eleven would have been are the days she was written out of, and those days went into a book at the clinic as a cost, and the clinic's costs go on the same account as everything else, and the district's answer to a cost is a rate, and the rate is on her ground** — and **there is no line on that paper for the Share, and none for the rule, and none for the certificate, and none for eleven trays.** *So there is one woman on here and one figure, and the woman did the trays and the figure is hers, and she did not do the eleven days and the eleven days are not hers either, and they are nobody's, and nobody's is what a Share is when a thing has to be put on a piece of paper.* She will say yes before she is asked, and wants it noticed that saying yes to a form is not the same as saying the form is right. **Sedge refuses to sign a finding, in three moves, and will not cut one:** a finding is a form, and the head of it would be a kind — *a rate on a member's ground does not scale* — with a space in it where a person is supposed to be; **a man who cuts a form is the man who ought to be told it is not right, and being told is not the same as signing, and signing is what a man does to a form to make it into a document, and he has already made this one into a document twice this morning and it has cost two women a morning each**; and third — and this is the batch's cost to the volume's fourth practice — **he will not put his own line at the bottom of a piece of paper to be read out twice at the ordinary speed, because a line read out twice is not a thing a man said in a room any more, it is a thing the room owns, and in about four years a man will produce it and say that the yard thought so.** *Take your two sheets. Do not let anybody head them.* And: *I have been in this building long enough that twice in it, and I did not come down here to be thanked, and if somebody in that lane thanks me on the day the days go on that board I will tell them it is a chair they are thanking and not a man.* Petrik puts the days on the oiled board and there is nothing at the head of it and nobody told him to put anything there. **The two filled sheets go face down under the slate, the fifth line empty on both, and in the fourth month a rate is going on that ground anyway — correctly, out of a rule nineteen years old, by a woman who has walked to every kitchen in this district and has told two of them the size of it and has been thanked by neither.**

### Chapter 141 — What the Yard Cannot Enter
**The twenty-second of the ninth month, MR 414, the Gate yard in Tallow Ward at the sixth bell.** A barrow, a hundred-pound sack, a folded trestle. Marek puts **two things** on the trestle — that the advance is eleven thousand and some hundreds and an office a night and a day up the river has a box for taking it back and it can be done in nine days, and that leaving it and spending it on seed in the four terraces is not correct, has no office, cannot be produced by anybody, and is the only one of the two that does not turn a season that has already failed once into a famine. **He is looking for a room with no office to put both of them in and there is not one and he is not going to make one.** Sanne Droll (thirty-nine, rope-maker, the yard's second person, asked the four questions like anybody else) asks the four questions and finds that the first three want a paper and there is no paper, and the fourth wants a person in the yard and there is not one, **so she cannot enter it and cannot refuse it, and she has never once been able to do either with a thing like that.** The refusal is the easy one; **entering is the other one, and a line on a board is a thing a person can be shown, and if there is a line on that board in the morning this yard has said the money is a thing a person brought in with him about himself, and it is not — it is a district's figure in a book in the hills, and she would have entered a district.** Milan Vray (yard-man, nineteen years, no age given) refuses twice: a yard can say no, and **a man in a good coat can do the same thing and put the yard's no in his own mouth and be in a room in nine months, and there is nothing anybody has ever been able to do about that, and Vray has stopped minding it and has not stopped being careful.** He will not be thanked and the yard has not been thanked since the eighteenth of the ninth month and is not going to be. Aldis Kern, from the step, and not further: there has been a set-off on a nail in a seed house up the road since the sixth of the third month, and about nine people in the ward have asked him whether it will be served, and he has said no eleven times, and he has never once worked out whether the eleven refusals did anything. **He asks for that to be noticed in a yard by somebody who is not him, because in four years he will not be able to say he did not.** Marek writes both things on a wet slate, reads the first back and finds it wrong, wipes it, writes the second, and does not wipe it — because there is nobody in this kingdom he can hand a slate to. He wipes it with a rag off a nail. **Slate does not come off entirely. The mark is grey and illegible by the seventh bell and there is no office in this kingdom in which a man may be sent to ask what was written on it.**

### Chapter 142 — A Room With No Office
**The twenty-fourth of the ninth month, MR 414, the Elmsworth seed house counting floor, morning.** Eleven sets of Crown March paper laid out in a row on the bench, nine of them never opened, on a nail in that room since the sixth of the third month. **Petrus Lenge has not come up, and that is the first fact about the room: he came in the second month and on the tenth of the harvest month and said on the way down that the second day would be the day he stopped coming up cheap, and he stopped.** Zofia Teran has come down eleven switchbacks and is sitting on a sack with her hands in her lap. Mileva Vukic is standing at the end of the bench with a rag. Marek says both things once and does not argue about either. Vukic: *You have not told us what it is **for***, and reminds him that the paper did not land on her ground and that a hand put on her ground again by a man who is very careful is not a thing to be asked for. Teran: **the four terraces are not in this room, two of them are**, and the room's shape is that they are all people who are already talking. Fisk refuses the premise and finds the fact instead: **nine of the eleven sets came back unopened, not refused — a coat came to four doors and at the doors it was taken off him and sent back sealed — and there is no office in which a person may be asked why their own door sent a thing back, and of the four women in those yards not one would tell him, and not one of them was hiding anything.** Then **Orren Vaas, forty-one, a factor in Rainmarket, a new minor name**, comes in with a slate for seed, rules eleven lines with nothing at the head of them on purpose, and says the thing out loud that is the chapter's mechanism: the advance will come back as a set-off against the coming season, and the set-off comes off the seed money, and that is why he is in the room. He also says he has been buying a copy of the season schedule off a man in a good coat since the third month for a house in Saltglass, and has never been told what it is. **The room's finding, and the volume's turn: the party behind the advance is not a holding but a district, and the district is one line and the four terraces are inside it, and a terrace is not a line, so there is nothing to recover against. A district that has come in short does not pay — it answers with a rate, and a rate goes on a person with a name, and by the fourth month of MR 415 there will be about nine names and none of them is anybody in the room. So there are not two ways of taking it back. There is one, and it is neither of the two Marek has been carrying up and down a road about nine times.** Vukic: *Say the other one and then do not say anything for a while, because a man has said the word famine in this room once.* Then the disagreement in which both are right: **four households (eleven holdings) buy their own seed at the depot's price out of ground and not out of a pledge, and the Share's people sow and are paid in seed at the same price, and it is a return in kind and not a gift and not a favour — because Fisk will not hand the Share's own seed to four hundred acres when the Share's sixty-one are the ones with eleven lines coming in the fourth month.** *And nobody is to call it a pool, and I will not say the word in this room.* Vaas goes down the road with a figure nobody gave him and a piece of paper four households gave up without being asked. The count goes to the seventh bell; nothing at the head of the bench book.

### Chapter 143 — Eleven Times, Separately
**The twenty-sixth to the twenty-ninth of the ninth month, MR 414, a seed merchant off the low-ground road, then the road up.** A seed merchant of thirty-one years with a good scale and a bad thumb on the beam has **eleven sacks on a floor and eleven separate purchases in eleven days**, and the price on the slate is the depot's price out of Rell's letter for a shilling. The instrument was sealed in eleven names and there are eleven holdings; **Marek had been saying four households for a month because a man who has been in a valley twice since the harvest month counts terraces and not doors.** The merchant asks the one question — *is that money of mine* — and Fisk answers **No**, and gives him the words to write: *bought and paid for, out of ground and not out of a pledge, and no line against the buyer's name in this book or any other.* **The same answer is then said eleven times over three days by eleven people about their own money, and not one of them about anybody else's, and the merchant writes all eleven and then rubs five out at the end of the third day because he has a house and a family and a business that has to be worth something in a year's time. He hangs the six on a nail in his shop with nothing at the head of them.** The eighth of the eleven is **Hana Belic**, who says out loud, in a shop, that she has been written out of eleven days by a physician, that those days went into a clinic book as a cost, that the balance of her own is a rate on her ground in the fourth month, and that the seed she is buying is not the seed for that. The eleventh is bought in cash at the fifth bell by **Ruta Vrabec, fifty-eight, who is the only person on the roll of the top terrace whose name is on a line anywhere**, for her son of nineteen, who stayed at the top of the road and does not know. **The labour goes up on the first of the tenth month — nine members, found not arranged, because two said they could and then five more said they could.** Hana Belic does not go and says no twice and says the second no cost her a tray. The autumn work is nine days of ditches, drains, a shoulder above a new cut, and mending a wall before the rain; **the sowing goes in on the ninth and the tenth of the second month of MR 415**. **The third terrace is in neither list, and Stana Peric says it and will not say the next part for him: a cart is not an office, a sack cannot be served on anybody, and so a cart and a sack are the only way four unreached people get seed — and they will not be told, and if anybody asks in nine years how the third terrace came through the winter the answer will be *they had seed*, and it will be true, and it will not be the whole of it.** *You have just found a way round the only rule in this basin nobody was allowed to get round, and it was there the whole time, and I would like you to sit with that for about a day before you are pleased with yourself.* Duss takes the carts up on the first and the third, charges his own rate, and is not thanked; **at the top of the road on the third he says again that he would like somebody to write down that he knew the road was opened on purpose, and Marek stands in a gateway with a slate in his hand and does not write it down.**

### Chapter 144 — The Last Night of the Ninth Month
**The thirtieth of the ninth month, MR 414; the year turns in the night and the first frost comes in with it.** The seed house counts on the last night of every month whether or not anything has happened, and nothing had. **The four terraces had been bought seed** — eleven households, three days, a depot's price in coin, no line against any of them, six of the merchant's eleven sentences on a nail with nothing at the head of them and five rubbed out. The stores are shut; the kept-back came up on the sixth, seventh and ninth and Hartlow's last over four days of carting. **The low ground's second sowing did not come and did not come in the ninth month and nobody sent anybody, and the carts turned round on the ninth of the harvest month, and about a fifth of the first did not come either, and the rate has not moved since the fifth of the second month of MR 414 and Kuez still reads it out to every cart and will not say the figure.** Fisk does the Share's arithmetic in the yard, out loud, twice: **eighty-one days against a measure of seed at a depot's price, and the days do not come back and the seed does, and she wants the two numbers said out loud one after the other without a word between them.** Somebody at the back says the Share has done well out of it. *The Share has done well out of it, and the Share is the sixty-one of us, and we are the ones with eleven lines coming in the fourth month, and every pound that went up that road came out of this yard and went to four households that are not ours.* **The thing said once and not in the yard:** Vukic, at the end of a bench, with a rag in her hand — the first terrace is first on a list of four and it is not because of anything she has done, the counterweight found this ground before the other three, the seed was bought on the twenty-sixth at a price a man in a good coat set, **and none of those four facts is a reason, and she wants all four said out loud in a row so that nobody in a room in nine years can pick one up on its own and make it into a story about her.** She will not be grateful, and the reason is that **she would have bought the seed herself and carried it up on her back and the only thing the room did was be a room.** And then the thing Ch 139 set up: *I have got about eleven weeks' worth of that in my head, and about four of them are gone, and I would like to know whether a man says a thing like this twice or whether he says it once and then lets a woman find out on her own.* **Six places in this basin now have a column and nothing at the head of it** — the wall list, the oiled board, the bench book, the piece of sack, the fifth line of Sedge's form, and the merchant's six on a nail, the only one of the six a man put up of his own free will and can take down in four seconds and will not. Nobody has named any of the six. The year turns in the night, nobody is told, Petrik breaks the frost off the trough with a flat stone, and Marek walks up the low-ground road at the first bell to be on the first cart up the switchbacks, having told nobody and been asked by nobody, on no form.

### Chapter 145 — Five and Two
**The ninth of the second month, MR 415, the Elmsworth seed house, second bell, under the lamp.** Sabin Rell, fifty-four, twenty-nine years in the depots, comes down the river **under regional oversight** and reads a printed censure out in a working room while the count goes on. **The grounds, in the paper's own order: he certified the underwriting clause on the twenty-fourth of the tenth month of MR 413 upon a projection entered in advance; the projection was a figure carried forward out of a mean of ten years; the mean is kept in a table in a room he has never been in; he could not produce it and could not swear to it; the season disagreed; and the counterweight named in the instrument of the second month of MR 412 fell in the district in which the holdings lie.** Censured, **not removed from his post**, under regional oversight, **and not thereafter to certify an underwriting clause upon a projection** — and that last clause is the only thing in the sheet that will cost this basin anything. **Five sat, two dissented, and no chapter says how the two went**, because the papers are in a drawer in Crown March and nobody in Morrowfen will ever be shown them. Rell refuses to describe the room, and the reason is a reason about papers: **a description of that room is a thing that can be produced, and a count of how five men voted is not a fact about a censure but a fact about five men, and a man who has written the count will be asked which two and will not know.** Marek takes out a slate to write *a censure was recorded, on a vote of five, with two dissenting* and puts it back, and Rell sees him do it and says the true reason and not the good one. **Rell is right about the arithmetic and wrong about the practice and says which half is which before anybody asks.** He says he said the two halves out loud in a better order for twenty-nine years and nobody has ever put them next to each other and asked him. He says he could not produce the mean and could not swear to it and told a man with no office on a stair in the tenth month before he signed anything and nobody wrote it down, and that he would like that noticed and is not going to be thanked. He refuses to describe the room because he was asked for the last of the three clauses and was told no, twice. **Fisk does not stop the count, and asks him plainly what he wants, and he says: did the four terraces sow.** Nobody thanks him. Belic asks him, without turning round, whether the count on the tray ought to be the same as on the second of the month, and he says it ought to be, and says he got two figures wrong off that bench in the growth month, and that the tray is hers and he had not asked for it. He goes up on the evening boat.

### Chapter 146 — The Head of the Column
**The fourth of the third month, MR 415, the Gate shed lane, seventh bell; about seventy people in the lane.** **Emmerich Tace, forty-four, comes back a year late on a fortnight** and says he was not told to come, and has been three times to that office and twice told it could not ask a person to be told something, and once told it could, by a clerk of about thirty on the way out of a room who would not put it in writing. His printed schedule has a head in type — *persons in the upland holdings who will bear the settlement weight* — and a hand-filled number the office expects, and **Droll will not have it on the trestle, so he holds it against his own chest.** He rules nine lines beside the printed column and **stops, and does not rule a head**, and says out loud that he knows what he is doing: a thing with nine names and nothing at the head of it is not a schedule, it is a list somebody wrote, and it is worth nothing in a room in the hills, and **a list somebody wrote is worth nothing in a room in the hills** — and he does it anyway. **The four names on his own file are a woman with a cloth, an examiner, a man with no office, and a haulier; he wrote all four out of an office's file, has had them in a drawer for a year, and not one of them knew.** He asks one of the yard's four questions and stops — *is that your line* — because there are nine names and about four hundred and some people and the number of them is not on any form in the kingdom, and Droll reads the other three out at the counter so that a person who is not a clerk hears them, and Tace writes them in the margin of his own case with a date and no name. **The finding, and the reason a list of names is unobtainable and the reason is not courage: a list is a list of people who can be sent to, the only way a name gets on one is that somebody else writes it down, the yard opposite produces a list of nobody out of a four-question rule rather than out of courage, and a counter is a room, and a room is a thing that can be produced at a household in nine months, and the moment it is produced it is a list of people who were asked and not a list of people who agreed.** Droll then gives the chapter its sting: **the yard refused Duss's line, on the fourth question and on the third, because the mark is the only part of that sheet with a person in it and a person cannot swear to a mark; the yard has never entered a name of its own accord; so the Chancellor is being sent nine names of which the only one anybody in this basin has been asked anything about is the one the yard would not have entered, and the eight the yard would have entered are the ones somebody in the hills wrote down on their own.** Venn's answer arrives in five lines: no room stands behind the paper, no person is to be sent, the schedule is returned unheaded, **and the request is closed. Not answered. Closed.** *She wrote that in nine days. It took me a year to get it.* **Marek's flaw arrives on schedule: he knew all four people were on it, and he did not walk four miles, and the reason is that he wanted to be able to prove it first.**

### Chapter 147 — A Slip on a Wire
**The eighteenth of the third month, MR 415; the posting desk in Bell Stair in the morning, the second-floor hearing room in the evening.** A four-inch slip printed on the back of a form run, pinned on a wire put up in the tenth month of MR 413: an extract of a granary board minute, registry of district instruments, **carried five and two, the papers in a drawer, no usable date, no name.** The duty clerk is sixty, twenty-two years in the copy room, and does not read them, and says the man who pins a thing up does not get to be the man who explains it. The rule: **a mark which no officer of a district can read is not a witness to the paper it is on, and a paper whose counterweight, security or assurance is carried by such a mark alone shall not stand alone for any purpose touching the future of a district**, and a copy or certified duplicate of any such paper is of no effect for any such purpose unless the mark is read and certified by an officer of that registry. **Harrow's board sits for the third time** — four at the table, Trust's seat empty for the sixth time, *in the ordinary course* still in quotation marks in a minute in a drawer; Kade not a member with a sheet whose only function is to hold four figures in the order they are called; Ferrow on a lapsed licence; Marek let in by a door-keeper who did not write him down; the four terms confirmed in about eleven minutes. **The disagreement in which both are right: the rule does something and cannot do nothing — it is a Chair's sentence from the rains month now in type with five and two behind it — and it is in type, and a minute in a drawer has to be found by a person and a registry entry can be produced by anybody who can read, and a registry holds entries and not findings, and in about four years a man will take six lines to a woman on a holding and she will not be able to answer him because there is no box in that sentence for her.** Kern declines term four in about nine words and does not say why. Then Marek's nine lines: the six lines of type now say the advance cannot be recovered, **which means the seed bought in the ninth month is not a decision anybody took but a consequence of a rule printed three months afterwards by five men who have never heard of a yard or a register or a seed shop or a man with a barrow and a bad thumb on a beam.** And the part he says once: **if anybody in nine years puts the two side by side it will look like a plan, and there was no plan, and he is the only person alive who can say that and cannot prove one word of it, because the room it happened in had no office and wrote no minutes and does not exist, and four of the nine people who could establish it will not — two are right not to, one is dead, and one is not a clerk and not a member and not in this basin — so in nine years somebody will say a man in a good coat arranged it and there is no office in which anybody may be produced to say he did not.** Harrow writes it, in his own hand, at about eleven minutes, and puts it in the drawer with the other four, and says the sentence is worth nothing and he would rather be one of five in a drawer than be the man who was not in it. **The fifth minute goes into a locked box on the back of a notice about a water office's list of fourteen lines**, and says in the fifth paragraph that no mark in the finding is a witness, and in the seventh that the finding of the board is that a room with no office cannot be declared the wrong room, **and nobody in that building will ever read either sentence to anybody in Morrowfen.**

### Chapter 148 — The Levy Tin
**The second of the fourth month, MR 415, the Gate shed and its lane, second to seventh bell.** A tin on the second trestle from the first bell, a hundred and forty a season, in a notice in Sedge's hand, and a person gives it or does not, and Droll says the amount out loud once to each person because she is asked the four questions like anybody else. **The tin goes into the office at the fourth bell and is not counted out in the yard, deliberately, and about nine people in the ward know it and all of them found out by accident.** The reason is in the yard's own unwritten method: **a figure that appears on a board the same way twice turns the board into a roll, and a roll is a thing a man in a good coat can produce at anybody in nine months; a total on the tin would be a figure beside a name, and this yard has no form for a name beside a figure, and a tin with a column on it is a receipt, and a receipt is a thing a person is handed and can be produced with.** A man comes down the lane to ask what the ward took and Kern tells him there is no total and that he may add it up himself over a year and enjoy it. About seventy in the lane, twenty in a shed that holds twenty-two, Lask reading a second line twice. The wall list has about forty lines and a column at the end of the fourth with nothing at the head of it since the second week; **the fifth line is the hours and is empty, the yard has refused it twice on its own rule about a round that repeats, the sixth is the days and is empty and always will be.** The flat board with four ruled sheets and the certified duplicate are both under the trestle and have not been out. **The water still does not come down the Ropewalk standpipe on a queue morning, and the cost of the queue is on one woman's back and her name is not in this chapter because she has not asked for it to be anywhere.** **Ondra Kelb** asks the standing question — how can a column be in a room for a year with nothing at the head of it and not be a mistake — and is told the column stays bare, and is told that **nobody knows and that not knowing is a different thing from a man's *such and such*, and it is the only head this column has ever had and he is not going to be able to write it down.** He is not thanked for asking and Droll is not thanked for answering and neither is a policy; it is a yard. **About nine people stopped coming in the first year of the register and about nine more stopped in the fourth month of MR 415, and there is no way in this kingdom of telling the two lots apart, and the second lot is not smaller than the first and the yard is not bigger.** A man from the water office counted heads at four bells for four days on his own initiative and told nobody and has not been back. **Kern: the yard has cost this ward its water and about nine people and nobody knows which nine; two men in good coats have asked him in a year whether the room is a success and he has said no twice, the second time in a worse temper; and a register is not a seed, and he has heard the word said in a lane twice and both times by men who had not been in the shed.** The district's rate comes on the same month, on about nine names, every one a person, none a household or a terrace, none asked anything at a counter — **and it is a different size of the same shape from the eleven lines in a drawer in Rainmarket and cannot be put beside it on any form in this kingdom.** Fisk went to eleven kitchens in the fourth month and was told no by four and told that figure to nobody outside the eleven kitchens. The register is a practice and not a remedy and no office in this kingdom has been told it is either.

### Chapter 149 — The Key in a Dead Man's Coat
**The ninth of the fourth month, MR 415; the fourth terrace in daylight, the low-ground road at the second bell, the Gate yard at nightfall, the seed house at the fourth bell. There is no question mark in the chapter.** **Janko Vrabec died in the frost at the end of the ninth month of MR 414 and nobody in that valley said a sentence about it.** Ruta Vrabec gives it in about nine words and then says she will not tell Marek what anybody said to her husband in the growth month two years ago, because she did not hear it from her husband and he never said it to her. **The key was on the hook in his coat for eleven years and on the hook when he died, and it was in the pocket of that coat on the ninth of the second month when Tomek, nineteen, took the seed out of the store himself, and she told him to stop and he stopped and said *the seed has to come out today* and she said *there is nobody to say you may* and he said *there is nobody to say I may not*.** Asked the same nine words by about nine people for a year, and no different answer once. **The key is in the pocket of the coat and the coat is on the hook in a seed room that has not been locked since the flood year, and that is how the ninth line came to have a key on a hook and no name beside it, and the two are the same thing and nobody has ever said so in a room.** In the loft the ninth line is still empty with the knife-cut round the space where the name went, and Ruta says it is not going to be filled, not by her, not by him, not in the fourth month and not in the ninth. **On the third terrace there is a shed at the end of a lane with eleven sacks of seed in it put there by a cart that told nobody, and four people live on that ground and all four knew by the second day, and nobody in this kingdom can be produced to say that anybody was asked.** Coming down: **Ottmar Kuez reads the district's rate, not the market's, to a cart coming down at the second bell, and the bridge book has a column of nine names against it and a day against every line, and nobody has ever come to look at the book.** Duss has had his name on the roll since the second of the month for nine days and there is nobody at the far end of the river who can tell him whose name it is. **Marek gives him the sheet folded in the left breast of his coat — his own hand, the bridge, a date, no name, not evidence, not producible, not in the register, nobody has ever seen it — and Duss reads it in about nine seconds and says: *That is worth nothing. I know. You do not know it.* And the true reason: a piece of paper in a lane is not anything, and a man in a good coat will come to that counter in nine years and four people will say no, **and he would like it written that he asked for it and got it and that he is not going to be thanked for it and is not going to be sorry for Marek either, because the two are the same size.** And Marek's flaw, owned in four sentences: *I put your name on a list in a drawer in the hills and I found out in the third month of this year and I did not come and tell you.* — *I know you did not* — *I know it because I did not hear it, and I have been on this road for thirty-one years and I have never once heard anything from an office a day and a night up the river.* **The column of nine names in a bridge book is the only place in this kingdom where a haulier, a mark and a figure are in a line with each other, and the man who put it there did his job and is not a villain and does not know.** Ovid Merrow's shed is open on the low-ground road with a lamp in it and he says in the door that it is not better than the Gate shed and has not become better, that the Gate shed has water on a queue morning and his has a roof, and that a man could have both and would not, and nobody thanked him and nobody had asked. Petrik puts the days on the oiled board with nothing at the head of it. The twenty-third tray is turned at the fourth bell, a girl with a broom is at the far end of sixty-one trays and is not spoken to by anybody, and the bench book has the number with the string across the top and nothing at the head of it. **The bar on the fourth-terrace store is not there to hide anything; it is holding next year's seed, which is the only thing in that valley anybody in the hills has ever been sure about.**

### Chapter 150 — A Paper in a Coil
**The eleventh of the fifth month, MR 415; the low-ground reach at the second bell, the posting desk at the fourth, Tallow Ward at the sixth, the road at the first bell the next morning.** A barge with a full bed of barley and **a coil of paper by the fore hatch, tied with a piece of string, going to Saltglass and not coming back.** **Orren Vaas, forty-one, put it there and tells Marek about it on the tailboards before being asked, because he has been about two days working out that he is not going to be asked and has been wrong the other way round for a year.** The paper is a copy of a schedule of four holdings, bought in the third month of last year from a man in a good coat who did not say what it was and did not charge much, and **bought a second time in the second month of this year by a house in Saltglass that is not a house he has ever been inside, as a security on a year that has been paid twice**, and the clause in the second sale says the paper stands or falls with the mark on it, because every schedule of four upland holdings in this region has the same clause and it is the fourth clause of a standard form. **He cannot name the month in the mark and has never met anybody who can, and has concluded the month is not the point of it, and is not going to find out before the barge is at the mouth.** He has looked for a form on which to be told *stop that* and there is not one, and he has asked two clerks in the hills and one said *registry* and one said no and the one who said no was right and he is paid to be. And the sentence he has been turning over since a man in two rooms above a chandler's shop said it to him in a room with a tide table on the table, out loud, in the growth month of last year, and he did not take a note of it: **a signature says the paper is good; a mark says where a paper can go.** And Marek works out four things in about nine seconds and can do one thing with none of them — he cannot stop the barge, cannot report the mark, cannot find out who gave the schedule to the man in a good coat (the only name he had was a haulier, and the haulier had not given it to him and would not), **and cannot put it in a shed in a drawer, because he did that once already with a yard's own method and it cost a yard a thing it made in the rain, and there is no form in which a man may take a sentence back out of a room, and he said exactly that out loud nine days ago and had it written down in a finding and was not thanked.** At the posting desk he pays a pound and a little over and posts a second letter, the same nine lines, the same question about the translator's name. The first went on the seventh of the second month of MR 414 and the answer was due in the third month of that year and has not come. **The man at the counter says he is going down to a place that did not answer the first, and takes the money, and says he would like it noted that he noticed, because he is the man who will be asked in a year whether he noticed.** In Tallow Ward the shed is shut and through the doorway: about forty lines on the wall list, a column at the end of the fourth with nothing at the head of it since the second week of the seed month of MR 414, the fifth line empty, the sixth empty for ever, the flat board and the certified duplicate under the trestle, the notice in Sedge's hand, and the register a practice and not a remedy with no office told either. **And then the last of it: there is no second time.** The eleven weeks ran out somewhere in the winter and **nobody in this basin can put a day to it**, and there is no form on which a person may be told that eleven weeks have run out, and he was not there, and nobody is going to find out. He has a sentence ready that has been ready for about a month and has not been used, and he works out at about the fourth turn of the switchbacks that there is no second time, and then goes on up, because the sowing was in on the ninth and the tenth, the district's rate came on on the second of the month, there is a man on the low-ground road with a rate and a straw cart, and there is a woman of thirty-eight who will be the woman with the jar for another two months, and nobody is going to say one word about any of it to her in a gateway before the next harvest unless a man with nothing in his bag goes up the road and stands in it. **A barge went down the river with a full bed of barley and a coil of paper by the fore hatch, and a mark on the paper in a month nobody on the bank could name.**

# VOLUME 04

### Chapter 151 — Twenty-Two in Four Months
**The fourth of the sixth month, MR 415, the tower's public witness counter, causeway side, from the second bell.** A rail that is not for leaning on, a brass plate saying what the counter is for, and a second plate under it saying what the counter is not for, put up in the flood year by a clerk who is not named and who was right. **Perpetua Ohl, fifty-one, thirty years at that counter**, enters other people's words every working day and keeps a book of them and will not give Marek the number because a figure made on the page in front of you is a figure somebody can be shown afterwards. **A challenge is free, anybody may bring one, there is no fee for one, and there is no form on which one may be refused, and she has never refused one.** The count she will give and no other: **twenty-two since the first of the fourth month, and two in the whole of MR 414.** A woman at the third window with a sheet in a cloth who has been there since the first bell and is not going to leave; the sheet names a district water account, a season, a rate that has not come, and in the third of five boxes a man as witness, in a fair small hand. She has been told four things in a month by four men who were not the same man. **Ohl's nine, in the same four months, are the same case in different coats.** Marek came for a hand and there is no hand: **every one of the nine signed it themselves.** The bundle under the counter — untied in three places, not with a knife, retied wrong once and then properly because a bundle that has been opened is a thing a man in a good coat can produce in four years. **About three hundred sheets in thirty years, a mixture, because a counter is a place where people bring the thing they have and not the thing that is tidy; nine in ten the same.** Four ruled lines, no boxes. The name. Then a line, in a person's own hand, describing a thing that is not a paper — a ditch, a roof, a stall, a boundary, a bin, a boat. Then **a third line in type on every one of them: *I will speak to any paper touching this line while I live.*** Then a fourth line with a month and no day, and there has never been a day on one in the time of the counter and she has entered about nine hundred. **There is no line on the form that names the paper.** The room behind the third window is eight feet by eleven with a rail round three sides and a wire in the window and a woman stood in it for two days and a night in the ninth month of MR 414 because there is no form on which anybody may be given a bed. And the chapter's last sentence from a woman of fifty-one: **nobody has ever asked me whether the people in that book were free when they signed** — and she has not been praised for not noticing and has not been told she should have.

### Chapter 152 — The Ditch She Signed For
**The ninth of the sixth month, MR 415, the boundary ditch between two seed yards on the Counters' Walk, third bell, in the rain.** Cut in the growth month of MR 411 by four men and a boy, silted up by the rains of MR 414, a line of wet grass with a hedge on both sides; the wall at the head of it taken down and built again in the same season and mended once since on the near side with a course that does not run. **Odile Marn, thirty-one, a spade with a worn socket and label tape nine inches from the top**, two children asleep over a seed shop. *You are the second one.* Told on the sixth by a man in a good coat, very polite, who said her name, said *the district water account*, said *it is only a formality*, and asked whether she would like to hear the terms, and she said no. **She said no to hearing the terms and not to the thing, because you cannot say no to a thing already said about you in a room you are not in, and she has known that since she was seventeen and has never found a form that improves it.** **Neris Kade is nine paces behind and made up the distance deliberately and will not explain it.** Marek reads her nine sentences from the bank in the order: what she signed, what it said, what has been done to her name, what a man in a good coat is going to say in four months — and **she stops him, because the true answer is that it is not about the ditch. It is about a person who signed a thing about a ditch, and the only question anybody can ask her about the paper is a question about her name.** He tells her the options: a challenge is free, anybody may bring one, no form on which one may be refused — **and then the true cost: there is no form in this kingdom in which a person may be entered as saying they do not consent, and in about four months a man in a good coat will say she consented, and the paper is the paper she cannot read and the box is a box that does not say what it is.** She says the worst of it is not that. **The worst of it is that she would have signed the ditch paper again and would have been free, and everybody would have been able to say so, and there was nobody in that ditch on the sixth of this month to ask her.** Kade does not say the true thing and Marek notices her not saying it and does not use it. Then the copy: she has had it since the sixth of the month and has been carrying it, and there is nowhere in this kingdom to put a thing you do not want. *I want it asked* — and not written anywhere. She goes at the course that does not run, and nobody says one word to her about the two and a half years since a man from a water office came along a hedge with a printed sheet.

### Chapter 153 — Two Names at the Head of It
**The thirteenth of the seventh month, MR 415, a first-floor room in Bell Stair and then the copy room.** Eleven feet by nine, a table planed soft, a rail round three sides, a wire in the window, a bell on the keeper's side that is not rung for time; the keeper comes in at the second bell, writes four lines, and goes out without saying which of them is a day. A records office has put a field examiner and a man who reads papers out loud in one file and neither of them asked. **Kade opens with the thing she did not say in the ditch, in about nine seconds: you read her a paper, you told her what the room would do, you told her what a man in a good coat is going to say, in that order, and at no point did you ask her what she wanted to do about it — and that is the shape of the thing that has gone wrong in this basin for three years, and neither of us has ever once been the person it happened to.** He says it is fair; she says it is correct and he is going to do something worse. **The first honest disagreement about who should bear a risk, and it is about each other, and neither is right.** He does not want her name on anything because in four years a man in a good coat can produce the sheet at a shed and nine people will look at him and then at her. That is the other half and it is correct. *Put a second name on it* — **and then the cost has been described and he has not asked her to pay it and she has been waiting nine seconds for him to notice.** She will have her name on it at the top of a column and not in it; she wants his at the top of the other; the sheet ruled so neither can write in the other's column; the rule written on the sheet in one line; cut in Bell Stair by a man who will not say whether it is right. **A column with nobody's name at the top is a column a man in a good coat can put any name into, and about nine careful people have each made a sheet with nothing at the head of it, and there are six places in this basin in that state and the count is closed.** Sedge cuts it: pencil ruled, both columns gone over in ink, two names at the head in his own sloping hand, the rule under them. **He says nothing whatever about whether it is right and asks to be noticed not having said it.** They begin on it the same afternoon. Four lines and nine lines about four people, one of them a man of twenty-two whose name is on neither column because he is not in the room — *that one is for later, and it is not later.* She tells him he has not written a sentence about the woman with the spade, and he says that is the wall list's job and it is bare on purpose, and that he has to go back to that ditch and ask her and not have read her anything first, and has never once done that in the correct order.

### Chapter 154 — Five Lines
**The second of the eighth month, MR 415, a print shop on Ropewalk in Bell Stair, from the third bell, door up.** Ink, oak, and the heat of a case worked since the first bell. **Iveta Roslak, forty-three, has had the shop nineteen years.** She does not stop working when a man with a wet sleeve arrives; she puts the stick down, wipes her hands, and says he is not here about the prices, and then asks him to say the form. He puts a finger on the third line. **A printer's tray of one of everything she has ever set, in date order — not a secret and not an archive: you cannot set type you have never seen a proof of, and a quarter of an inch in a forme is a quarter of an inch on every sheet for the rest of the run, so every shop keeps one, and it is the most useless thing in the building and the reason a shop can be trusted.** The ninth item in the tray, nineteen years old this month, set in the third month of that year with a stick and a galley and no compositor, **because she was twenty-four and had had the shop about a year. She has set that forme eleven times in nineteen years and nobody has ever asked her why.** **It has five lines.** The first is the name, the second the line, the third the same twelve words in type, the fourth a month and not a day — and **the fifth says *the paper*.** Same paper, which is worse; same type, which is not, because some of it was re-cut by a different house and has a different shoulder on the letter *l*, and that is the kind of thing that is in a tray and not in a discussion. **She cut it.** It was a half-sheet: four hundred copies going out that month to counters and offices and kitchens, and four inches of type in a forme that had been in the shop longer than she had. **She consulted nobody, and gives the true reason: a line is a line, you are not removing a right and you are not removing a person, and you cannot consult anybody about taking four inches out of a sheet.** And then the finding, in her mouth, and it is the volume's: **cutting the fifth line did not make the form cheaper to read, it made it cheaper to print. Before it, the form had one job, which was to say that a person would speak to one thing, and the paper was on it, and a person at a counter could look and say *that is not the paper*, and the man would go away. There is no paper now.** She thought there was a line; she did not know it was a person. She is not going to tell him she did not know, and there is no version in which a woman of forty-three at a forme in the third month of a year nineteen years gone knew that. **If you have come up this hill to find somebody to be angry with then I am the worst candidate in this city and I have been for nineteen years and I have not made one thing worse by being angry at myself.** **Orren Vaas, forty-one, a factor in Rainmarket, NOT Oren Pike, nineteen years in the trade**, wants nine hundred of the four-line one, does not say why, will pay trade price; she tells him the price of resetting a forme she does not keep type for and he does not argue, **which means he knew in the lane**, and she refuses to ask a paying customer what he wants nine hundred of a paper for. He leaves one sentence, said once: a woman asked him in the second month whether he could find somebody to print a form that had a line on it; he said he did not know anybody who kept type; he did not ask which form, because a person who does not say which form she wants has usually worked out that saying it makes it a conversation. Two coins for a docket nobody asked for, and out into the rain. The press pulls eleven times.

### Chapter 155 — The Price Is Not Money
**The sixth of the eighth month, MR 415, the journey out on the fifth; the records office at Verrow, a night and a day up the river, from the third bell.** A night and a day up the river is not a journey; it is a room over an inn, a breakfast that costs about nine times as much, flat light, a stair — **and the reason people call it a journey is that nothing that happens on the trip is available afterwards, because the boat puts you down at a stair and goes.** **Damir Pribis, forty-nine, eleven years opening the basket** — on the page in Volume 03 and used again, **and not Josip Malnar** — tells Marek before he has his coat off that he is not on anything: no standing, no office, no form, and if he had come with a form Pribis would have had to write his name in the day book. The basket is every entry into every regional record of instrument for eleven years, on a landing because a record has to be carried down a stair by two men in a hurry if there is a fire. Case one is this month, case two the third; he does not remember either and will not pretend. On the eleventh of the third month, at the fortieth entry down: **the record loan.** Not a district entry. Sealed here on the eleventh of the third month of MR 415 by a man from the board above, on a standing authority, correctly, to stop a district account being struck out at the end of the season. **A record loan changes what an office will accept as evidence; it does not change what is true and it does not change what a person saw, and no office that can seal one likes paying for things in people.** **Its price, in the last of five boxes, is not a figure and not a number of months: *fourteen attestations*.** Pribis entered it and says the rest before he is asked, in the order he has said it to himself about nine times in three months: he did not ask what fourteen attestations are, **and there is no form in this kingdom on which a filing clerk may strike an entry out of a regional record**, and he looked, and did not look hard, and looked hard enough to be able to say that sentence out loud in a stair in the third month and did not. **There is no office that will read you a sheet of names and give you a figure**, and the reason, given once: a figure about a number of people is a figure about a district; a district is a line in a book; a book that has to balance in the year it is balancing gets one number first and the true one in beside it with two initials, and the first one stays on the page. So fourteen is a district figure, there is no return, and **the reason there is no return is that a return is a thing you make about a person, and there is not a person on that entry, there is a class of people's willingness.** Down the river on the after board with a sheet of nothing in his coat: **nobody forged anything.** Every one of them signed a form, in type, at a counter, in person, about a ditch or a roof or a stall or a boundary, and was free when they did it, and was not asked about any of the other thirteen things, and the thirteenth thing did not exist yet. And a man in a good coat has said to about nine of them, one at a time, in four months, in the ordinary voice of a man doing an ordinary job, *it is only a formality*, and has not asked one question, and there is no box for it, and the box that did exist was cut out of a forme in an afternoon to save a quarter of a sheet of paper on four hundred copies. **He had been in rooms for three years looking for a hand. The hand was a compositor's, nineteen years old, and it was not in anybody's.** And a sheet in a flat case in Bell Stair has two names at the head of it and about eleven lines about four people, and one of the four is a man of twenty-two whose name is on neither column because he was not in the room.

### Chapter 156 — A Name on a Copy
**The nineteenth of the eighth month, MR 415, the second watch, the roof of a dye house, a candle in a saucer, a bundle the size of a small cat.** A woman in a doorway on a street he had never been down looked at him for two seconds and then did not, and that was the negotiation. **Nim Rook, twenty-two** — nineteen on a stair in the flood year with her knees up and her boots wet through, and the price she set then was not money. *You have not asked me once what this is about, and there is a candle on a roof and I have been waiting a good ten minutes for you to ask me, and you are not going to now, are you, because you have worked it out.* **She had the copy four days and did not send for him on the first day, and the reason is that she wanted to see whether he would come and ask.** He works it out and says so: she signed something years ago about a line, it has been produced twice, she has found a name on a page in a book in this city, and she cannot get anybody to look at the page because looking at it is a thing a person has to be able to say they did. **He says Ilen Rook into the dark, not to her — the second time in three years that anybody has said it out loud in a room.** The certified copy was taken on a press and is true: **a printer will not set a copy that does not match the page, because the first thing a proof shows a printer is that the type is wrong.** It is a ward roll — district, number, head-count, street, a line for a repair, a withdrawal, a death, and a far-right column filled in on about nine lines and with no head. The fourth column has a head. The far-right column does not. **On the fourteenth line down, in a fair small hand, *Ilen Rook*.** The true part he is not going to like and she cannot argue with: **that is a true copy of a page, so the page had that name on it, so somebody wrote it in a column with nothing at the head of it, in a room he has never been in — and the same shape is on a wall list in a shed in Tallow Ward — bare at the head of it since the second week of the seed month of MR 414, which is a year and a half back from this night — where nineteen people declined to name a column in a fortnight and about four hundred yards of a lane voted in four minutes not to fill it and not to rule it out.** It is not a coincidence he can rule out and it is not one he can rule in. She has looked in three books in this city and one a day up the river and in nine or ten people, for three years, on her own, and has written nothing, because there is no place to put it **and because a woman kept a list in a hand that has got worse over twenty-six years in a box under a counter and showed it to nobody and was right.** He asks about being told something after the moment had gone and gets an answer about the second of the month instead of the sixth. **She has been told once, on the second, by a man in a good coat who said *it is only a formality* and asked whether she would like to hear the terms and was told no.** She has not told him which, or when, or about what, and he has not asked. **There is no name for this street, she will not give him one, he is not to ask twice, and if he does she goes down the ladder and he will not find the roof again — which is a direction, not a threat.** She tells him why there is no name and it is not a mystery and not a door: **because of what is on the fourteenth line.** He has looked at all nine names and not at them for long, and does not ask the question he should have asked four days ago, and she lets him not ask it, and they sit on a roof in the eighth month with about nine names in a column with nothing at the head of it. **He has read all nine twice: two of them he can follow back to a counter and a form and a day, and seven he cannot, and the one on the fourteenth line is the worst of the seven, because she has a sister on the same roof and no day on the page anywhere and nothing at the head of the column — and in three years he has learned that a name in a column and a person who signed for it are two different things, and he has never once found the second of them on a page that was not true.** And one of the nine a woman of twenty-two who was told once and was not asked and told nobody, for four days, on purpose, **and was right to.** **CORRECTED at the next-0004 review: this summary previously said every one of the nine was a person who signed something else, in type, at a counter, and that sentence is gone from the chapter. It merged Ch 151's nine counter-signers with Ch 156's nine names in a column and made Ilen Rook a person who walked into a counter, which pre-spends Ch 199. These are two different nines and they may never be merged; see `state/continuity.md` and `state/open-threads.md`.**

### Chapter 157 — Told Twice
**The second of the ninth month, MR 415, the causeway side, the first to the fifth bell.** About nine at the first bell, about forty at the fourth, and a rail that is not for leaning on. **Perpetua Ohl puts a boy of about eleven on the end of it at the second bell with four coppers an hour and a slate**, and tells him what to do, which is to count at four bells for four days — **and tells him not to put anything at the top of the slate, and he is never asked to.** The method is the chapter and it is somebody else's method: **a man from the water office counted heads in a yard in the third month of MR 415 for four days on his own initiative, got a number, told nobody, and has not been back — and three months later a woman who had heard about it asked whether it was a thing people did, and somebody said *somebody ought*, and a counter keeper did it, with a boy, and told a man with no office, which is not the same as telling somebody, and the second half of that is the only part of it that was a decision.** Five numbers: about nine, about forty, about thirty, about forty-four, about twenty-two. The number that mattered was that one woman had been in the queue on the second and had been told she was a witness, and had come back on the fourth and had been told she was a witness to something else. He turns a sheet on the ledge over and puts his hand flat on it. **The only Ledger Sight panel in the batch, one line, and it costs a tunnel vision and an ordinary hour: *The same name is the witness and the party.*** Then no light, a headache, and he cannot afterwards remember eating at the first bell, or the room he slept in, or whether he put the latch down. **It is not a lie.** It is a true sentence about a proper sheet, correctly filled in, signed in person at a counter, and there is no way on earth to prove it. So he looks at nine sheets with his eyes. Nine of about forty. On seven, the person behind the paper and the witness are two names; on two, they are the same name — **and the same name on two of the nine is not a coincidence either, because a man who is behind a paper and is also its witness is not a man chosen twice; he has been used once and his name read off the first sheet onto the second, and there is no form on which anybody may be told the first one happened.** He finds it by the hand and not by the rule, because a witness and a party cannot share a name and that is the second of four questions at a counter in a shed in Tallow Ward, **and it is not the second of four questions at a counter on a causeway, and nobody has ever made it be.** **Margit Sova, forty-one, twenty-six years in the trade**, chaff stall shut four days running in the harvest month of MR 414 and not opened since. Told four times since the sixth of the month; the same nine words four times — *I signed about a bin and not about water*. Two of the four are on this ledge, two are not, **and the first man was from the water office on the second and the second was not from the water office on the fourth, on the same morning, at the third window and the first, and neither looked at the other, and both said five words in the same voice.** She knew at the third and did not go and find out why, **and the reason is not fear: if she finds out he has been to four people then she is a person who has been to four people, and that is a *line*, and she has a box under a counter with twenty-six years in it and was not going to be a line on the fourth day of the ninth month in front of a boy with a slate.** *I am not a line. Put the number.* Ohl says the sentence she said nine years ago to a man looking for his mother — **there is no form on which a person may be told that they have been spent** — and that she cannot enter it and cannot refuse it and will not look at the two sheets on that ledge twice.

### Chapter 158 — A Fortnight on a Named Ground
**The twelfth of the ninth month, MR 415, the seed house off the low-ground road, first to seventh bell.** The district's season works on a trestle: nine lines cut in a board, made by the district, belonging to no office, **line four the Counters' Walk ditch, the ninth a well and the ninth empty in the same way the ninth line four miles and eleven switchbacks up a road is empty, which is a shape and not a subject.** He has come down the road and has not gone up to the ditch, and the second thing he does right is not to say so. She is not going to be read anything: **he has had a sheet in his coat since the sixth of the month and has not read her a word of it and has looked at a board.** Then the reason nobody has ever asked her, and it is not an insult: **it is a queue. There is a man at a window with a list and her name comes up and he says the next thing, and the next thing is never a question, because a question is not on the list.** He asks her what she needs, and she says: not fuel, because two of the four men in good coats had fuel on a cart and one had money and one had a form, and she has two children and a stove and a season behind her. **A fortnight on this ground, not the yard above it and not across the hedge, this month, so the second sowing is not the sowing of a woman who has spent nine weeks being told what she is a witness to. And the ditch cut, second, and she has known that for four years.** He tells her the mechanism before asking whether she wants it: **there is no form, and he is not going to invent one, and he is certainly not going to use the paper in her hand, because that paper is the reason she is standing in this room instead of sitting in it.** What there is, is a board with nine lines and line four the ditch, and a season works in force on it, properly made and properly witnessed, until the last night of the month. **He can point that at one holding instead of the whole line, for a fortnight, and it will work, because it is in force and he is not making anything up and is not inventing a power.** And the cost comes out of the ground it is put on: not this season, this season is now, **next season on this holding and on no other, because the effect is pointed and he will point it**, and he does not know what it will be, and he has never once known until about nine months afterwards, and there is no form on which he could tell her today, and he is telling her so that in nine months she will not be told it by a stranger. So the whole of it is a fortnight on this ground and the ditch is not in it. **She does not answer for six or seven seconds, and he finds out he has done it in the wrong order again and that it has only come out right because she made him say the cost and then waited.** Her condition before she says yes: there is a man at the head of this road who has worked the ground above hers for thirty years and who signed a paper about a wall in the seventh month of MR 411 because she asked him to, in a fair small hand, and does not know what he is in and neither does she, and she has never asked him because she did not want to be a woman who has been told something in a season and goes and asks her neighbours about it in a wet week. **Which one of those two things is the cost. I do not know. I have told you three times that I do not know.** She says she will go and stand at the end of his lane and decide, and can come back tomorrow and tell him and he will wait — and then, that she can also decide not to have the fortnight, and has, for a good ten minutes, **and that it is the first decision she has made in a month that was not made for her at a window. Yes. Put it on the ground.** He puts his hand flat on line four and slides it four inches along the board to the sixth holding on the second rank, which is not the line. The ground comes up: nine days past the ninth month, the second sowing is thin everywhere on the low ground and is not thin there, the drill went through at the fourth bell and did not have to be lifted, no weed in it, and a man of fifty-six who has worked that ground for thirty years looks at it for nine seconds, sits down on a seed bin, says *it is early*, and says nothing else about it for a week. **The ditch is a ditch.** The line said the ditch and the ditch is a line of wet grass that has been there since the rains of the year before last, and the wall is not mended. She will not thank him and will not be ungrateful; it is a fortnight and it is real and she did not have one. The cost comes out of this ground in the year after, and she will go and look at the man's wall and he will not be told what she decided. **A fortnight is not a ditch.** The count goes on, Petrik says a number and does not write it, Hana Belic writes it on the bench book with the string across the top of the page and nothing at the head of it, the oiled board has the days on it and no keeper's name, and **there is nothing whatever in this basin that can cut a ditch.**

### Chapter 159 — A Stall That Is Not a Stall
**The twenty-first of the ninth month, MR 415, the low-ground road at the second bell.** Fourteen feet of stone edge and a canopy frame with no canvas on it, there since before the flood year, a lockable box under the only counter that still has a top on. **Margit Sova with a broom, because a man with a barrow on a market road will sweep a pitch whether or not he is holding it, and it is the cheapest thing a person can do about a thing they have lost.** *You have a lease. I have had a lease and it is finished and I cannot renew it and cannot sell it and cannot give it away, and four men in good coats have told me all three, and none of them is the reason.* The reason: **a lease is witnessed. A person cannot be a witness twice, because the first time says the word and the second time there is nothing left to say it with, and everybody in this kingdom knows that, and no form in this city or any other will let a person be asked a second time.** So the lease came to the end of a season in which she could trade and she cannot hold the ground — an arithmetic, not a hardship, and it does not come out differently in the ninth month or the first of next year. **And the chapter's finding, in her words: I have not been thrown out. I have not been sold. I have not been cheated. I have been spent.** Somebody used up the only thing she had that was anybody else's, four years old, about a bin, signed in a courtyard in the second month of MR 412 for a man from the water office who wanted to know who put the bins out on the Counters' Walk. She refuses to have what she says written down — **not because of the law; because a list in a drawer is what she has been living inside for twenty-six years and she will not make one more** — and names the mechanism before he admits it: he goes into a room with paper in his coat and comes out with four lines in his own hand and a date and no name, and then goes looking for a place to put them, and there is not one, and he keeps them anyway. He admits it out loud, in a market, and why: **he wanted to be able to prove it; he learned in the third month of last year that a man's name was on a sheet in a drawer in the hills and did not walk four miles; the man found out four months later at a weighbridge from a bridge clerk; the paper was worth nothing, because a piece of paper in a book of his own is not a room. She has had it for twenty-six years and he has had it for one, and neither of them has a room.** Then she gives him the thing she has instead, unasked: a box under the counter with a hasp, a padlock and a key on a nail inside, twenty-six years old, never lost and never moved — **about nine thin sheets gone soft along the middle crease, and on the top sheet a column with nothing at the head of it.** **It is not a list of names.** It is what a pitch owes, what it was given, in what order, in one book since she was fifteen, **no name in the whole of it, no head at the top of any page, no total anywhere, never added up and could not be, because a total is a figure she would be putting on a box under a counter in her own hand about about nine hundred people who have bought chaff off her in twenty-six years.** **This is the second of six** — six places in this basin with a column and nothing at the head of it, and she knows the number because the woman who keeps the wall list in Tallow Ward told her in the fourth month when she went to ask whether a column with nothing at the head of it could be produced at her, which it can, and which is why she has never asked for one. She has been at the shed, was told no, the woman was right, she has not been back and is not going: four witnesses who cannot be found, one dead, two in a ward she has never been to, and a man who counts at a rail and is not a person you can call. **What she will say to a man in a good coat in four years, and may be written down because she has nowhere to put it and neither has he: it is a column about what a pitch owes, in a hand that got worse over twenty-six years, and there is no head at the top of it because the only person who could head it is me and I have declined, for the same reason the woman in Tallow Ward declined — a head is the one thing on a sheet a stranger can be shown without having to know anything else about it.** He says he will find out who else is on it, not by asking the men (no form on which a man in a good coat may be asked what he has said to about nine people, and if there were one he would not use it, because the answer would be a document and a document can be produced at her in four years) **but because two of them have been told twice, and the only reason he knows that is that one of them told him at a rail after he stood there for an hour and asked her nothing at all.** And the sentence the movement closes on: **a man can be free when he signs a thing about a ditch and not free when somebody tells him what he is a witness to, and there is no form on either.** Not thanked; and not one word in the chapter about a column at the head of anything, because she would not have it and he did not ask her twice.

### Chapter 160 — The Counter-Signature
**The thirtieth of the ninth month, MR 415, from the sixth bell, and the year turns in the night.** The fourth of the four rooms behind the Threshold: eight feet by eleven, not a hearing room, not a witness room, the room people are put in when they have to stand and wait; a table planed soft, a rail round three sides, a window with a wire across it, a bell on the keeper's side that is not rung for time. The counter shut at the sixth bell, the causeway empty, the lamp on the table because a table lamp is a lamp that can be looked at. **Nim Rook has the certified copy in a flat case, and a sheet of plain paper with a line ruled on it and a box about two inches by one and a half at the foot of it, with nothing at the head of it — and she had nine days to have a head cut on it and did not, and that was her one decision and she made it in four seconds.** What it is for: **on the fourth of the next month she is going to stand in a room and say that she is a witness to a thing she was never told the name of and does not consent to, in about nine sentences, and then hand in a piece of paper — because in four hearings in this city a person who makes a speech is a person with a grievance and a person who hands in a piece of paper is a person with a document.** And the document is the copy, because it is true, and true is the whole of it, and **true of a page is not good in a room**: a copy says what the page says, not what it means and not that it is a person, and to put it before a room somebody has to stand behind the comparison, and that act has a name and the name goes in a box, and **a counter-signature is the one thing he is actually qualified to do and no office in this kingdom will do it for him and no office will stop him either, and both have been true since the flood year.** He tells her the cost before she is asked and in the wrong order and knows it: a chain that is false-witnessed walks to the living clerk who certified in good faith, which is the rule that put a clerk in a ward in the flood year;
