**what comes off a certifying clerk he does not know, and he has watched it land once, on a man of twenty, and it came off him in nine days and his left hand is a nine-year-old's and it is not his.** *Then tell me what you think.* **I think it is my word.** **Neris Kade comes in at half past the sixth bell, unasked, in a coat over her work clothes, and says in one sentence — you have not told her the moment — and he turns round, and she says it is the fourth time, and that he knows it a few seconds before she says it, and then gives two sentences about a man who has spent three years being careful and is about to be quick, and does not know which she would rather see, and then says: *Go on.*** He tells her the whole of it, including that he has read a copy and not a page, and that a person who certifies a thing whose subject they do not know has not witnessed anything. She listens standing up without interrupting once, and says **ask me** — *I want a great many things and none of them is that* — and then yes, and the true reason, and it is not that she thinks he will be all right: **she thinks he is not going to be all right, and that the paper will be in a room on the fourth of the next month and in a chest after that and then in a file, and there is no telling how long a file is, and she has been in files. And I am saying yes because on the fourth I am going to stand up and say that I am a witness I never agreed to be, and if I say it with a document it is a claim, and if I say it without one it is a woman having a bad morning, and I have been a woman having a bad morning in this city for three years and it has never once been a finding.** He shakes the ink, breaks the skin with the nib, picks the good pen out of two — *a thing that is going to be in a file about him for about nine years if anybody ever keeps one* — and writes his name in the box, and then comes to the date and **can only write *the ninth month*.** *You can only give a month. That is the form.* And he has known that since a woman of fifty-one untied a bundle under a counter on the sixth of the sixth month, and has had about four months to do anything about it, and there is no other date in this kingdom a person can put in that box. He squares the sheet and turns it round: **read it out loud, twice, at the ordinary speed.** She does. **It is the same date and the same room and the same lamp and the certification is good** — a copy with a clerk's counter-signature against it is good in a room, and the chain runs copy → roll → page → column with nothing at the head of it → a name in a fair small hand → **and there is nobody at the end of it at all.** About four seconds. **And the room did not take him.** No ink, no light, no pain: he cannot remember whether he ate at the first bell, he had, the room is the colour it was, his coat is on a nail he put it on himself. **What it took was not a year and not a season and not a scar. It was that the room did not take him** — the only thing he had ever had, that a person in a room would hear a name and a date and a number out of his mouth and it would go into the file, on which he had built a book of four things, including a sheet about a haulier's road that was folded into a coat because a name and a date was a thing that could be spoken. Kade comes a foot off the rail and stops, the way a person stops at a counter: **the certification took, and it is the first of its kind in about nine years, and he did that and nobody can say it was not him; and he has made himself a person who cannot prove that he did it, and in four years a man in a good coat will ask who stood behind the comparison and there will be a name in a box with a month on it and no day, and there is no form in this kingdom in which *I did that and here is my word* is a thing a man can say in a room and be believed.** And she is not going to say one word about the ninth of last year and about four miles and about not walking, because he knows, and because saying it would be cheap, and because he did it anyway and she would have done the same and they are not going to discuss that in a room. Nim puts the copy back in the case and hands it to him to carry. Then the price, four days old, taken off a stair in the flood year: **the moment you find out anything at all about a name of mine you tell me. You have had three years and you have told me nothing — not the bundle, not the four lines, not a type face with a different shoulder on the letter *l*, which I heard about from a factor in a print shop nine days ago, not a hand in a landing a night and a day up this river, which I found out because I went and asked a man in a good coat on purpose.** *You found out about nine things about me in four months and you told me none of them on purpose, and that is the part I cannot get round, because I have known you three years and I have watched you do it to other people and I thought you did it because you were frightened, and tonight I have found out that you do it because you would rather be able to prove it later. That is not the flood year. The flood year you were too slow. You are not too slow any more and that is worse.* *Yes.* *Good — because now there are two of us who know it, and one of us is going to be in a room on the fourth of the next month saying a thing out loud, and the other one is going to be standing at the back of it, and I am not going to look round, and if anybody asks me who stood behind the comparison I am going to look at the man in the good coat and wait for him to find the paper in his own hand.* The year turns in the night, no bell, no office. The lamp burns about two hours. The sheet stays on the table and is found face down on a pile at the first bell and nobody will ever be asked about it. **He sat on a bench and worked out that he had spent the one thing he had, and that it had not been taken from him, and that it had been given away by him, in about nine seconds, with a good pen, in the ninth month, with no day on it.**

### Chapter 161 — What It Would Take
**The second of the first month, MR 416, the journey out on the first; the records office at Verrow, a night and a day up the river, from the third bell, and a boat down the next day.** **Damir Pribis, forty-nine, eleven years opening the basket** — reused, **and not Josip Malnar** — has to move a chair to get at a drawer above his reach. The standing instruction in it, in a hand that was in the building before he started, says that a room in which instrument is witnessed is recognised by the region where a **responsible party** is named, and a responsible party may be a person, an office, a share, a ward, a guild, a market or a company, **and where the responsible party is a person the form requires an employer.** A minute on the wording is in the same drawer and does not say why, and Pribis has read it and will not say what he thinks. **A room with no employer cannot be recognised, so a paper witnessed in it is good in it and nowhere else**, and the moment it leaves the door somebody must write where it was witnessed, in a clerk's hand, **and there is no form in this kingdom on which a clerk writes the address of a room that is not in a book — and there is no book of rooms, and he has looked.** The one form for a witnessing **is** the attestation, and it names a line and not a paper, and a room is not a ditch or a roof, and he has held a pen over a piece of paper three or four times in eleven years and put it down again. **A form invented in a room no office owns is a form a man in a good coat can produce at anybody in about four years with nothing on it to say where it came from.** And the one part Pribis cannot get off a shelf, said at the window on the way out, the only thing he has said in eleven years to a man who came with nothing in his hands: **you will need somebody standing in that room whose word a person in another room will take, and that is not an office and it is not on a form.** On the after board he works out that the only person in the basin who ever had that was him, and that he spent it on the thirtieth of the ninth month in about nine seconds with a good pen — and then works out the second thing: **if a room like that ever has a price put on it, the price will be entered in the last of five boxes as a class, in words, and a filing clerk will read it out and write it down and not ask, and the second time it is done it will be done faster, and a woman of forty-nine and a man with no office would both be unable to say no.**

### Chapter 162 — Nine Sentences
**The fourth of the first month, MR 416, the second of the four rooms behind the Threshold, ten feet by eight, from the first bell.** A challenge is free and cannot be refused, and **Perpetua Ohl entered it at the first bell and brought it up herself and stood at the rail for the whole of it and did not go in.** **Nim Rook, twenty-two**, with the copy in the flat case, does not look round and has said she will not. The hearing officer is **Deputy Vera Tolek, forty-four**, who tells her there is no fee, no clock, and that she is going to count the sentences aloud afterwards and **the count is not going to be used against her.** Nim says **nine sentences** and the Deputy counts them and says *nine is not a fault, it means you were not performing.* The copy is good: **a true copy of a page, and a true copy of a page that was never true is still a true copy** — said out loud in a room for the first time in about nine years. **The certification is read: Marek Vale, a clerk, the ninth month**, and he is at the back by the door on the side with no rail, and the Deputy reads the name in the box out loud, and puts his statement in the finding, and then says the cold line: **I have your name on a paper. I do not have you.** Recorded as *name on paper, person not before the court.* **The room then takes her argument apart, correctly and without cruelty: there are two nines and they are not the same nine.** The first is about nine names in the far-right column of a ward roll page; the second is nine people told they are witnesses, which is not a set of names but a thing four men in good coats have said at four windows. **A room cannot make a finding on a conjunction nobody can test, and if it does, in four years the finding is a true copy of a mistake.** And Nim must hear that the Deputy was right, and Nim says the Deputy is right and it is the worst thing anybody has ever done to her. **The Deputy does not ask whether she was free, and dictates that it was not asked** — there is no such finding in nineteen years of her own hand — *because a court that asks a question it cannot answer is not a court, it is a queue.* The finding goes in with the day on it, in a building that keeps it. **The cost, said before she is asked: her name is in a book for as long as the building stands, and a finding about a form that cannot name a person is a finding about nothing, and she would have paid for it, and the price was her name, and she is not going to be told she was brave.** And on the causeway afterwards, **Perpetua Ohl gives him four names and the reason she has never said it: the only way anybody in this basin finds out whether a person will stand in a room is to produce their attestation and find out — so the people of record are the people who have been produced, and there are four of them, and every one of the four is the reason they were produced.** She has had them since the first of the fourth month, will not write them down, and will not give them before the seventh.

### Chapter 163 — A Room With a Rail Round Three Sides of It
**The seventh of the first month at the counter, then the ninth, from the first bell.** Ohl reads the four names and shuts the book: **Odile Marn, thirty-one, a line of wet grass between two seed yards, the growth month of MR 411 · Jelka Sondor, twenty-six, a bin behind a chandler's, the growth month of MR 412 · Zvonko Maras, fifty-eight, a roof over a coal bay, the second month of MR 410 · Drazen Topic, thirty-four, a boundary between two market pitches, the sixth month of MR 413.** And the reason, given once: every one of the four is a person who was produced, and every one of the four is a person because something went wrong, and not one of them is standing there for anything to do with being willing. Nim takes him in on the ninth by one of about four ways from the towpath, **and does not name the street, and he does not ask twice, and she makes him hear himself say it.** **The district is not named and is described as ends: the end by the dye house, the end by the wall where the rain comes off, the end where the steps go down and the water comes up.** No roll, no rate sheet, no plan; two offices asked in four years and one answered. **Zlata Hruska, thirty-four**, mends for a living in the top room over the dye house, reached by a ladder up the outside; **the rail is a bed frame and one of the four brackets is a shoe-tongue of iron made in a lane**, and she put the rail up in the ninth month of MR 414 after a man stood in the room for two days and a night with nowhere to sit, and she has never once stood on this side of it. **She has asked one question at that door for nine years — *were you asked* — and tells them the truth afterwards and then asks whether they would like a cup of something, and has never written one of them down, and has been asked no such question by anyone in nine years.** Her three conditions, and they are the chapter: **no employer, no surety, and nothing on the door** — and the third is the reason the first is possible, *there is no office in this city that can be shown a door in this city, that is not modesty, that is geography.* Marek says it aloud before he has decided to, *then it is worth nothing*, and she makes him finish it: good in the room and nowhere else, an opinion, and **she cannot be named by an office either.** She refuses to be a keeper, **a keeper is a person with a name at the top of a thing**, and gives him the one job he can have: **read.** *Reading it out loud in this room is not a witnessing and is not evidence and does not have to be, because in this room nothing is being proved; believed means heard and does not mean entered.* Nim comes down the ladder and says **a hand in chalk**, and Marek does not write any of it down, and gives the reason, and says he learned it from a woman with a broom in the ninth month of last year.

### Chapter 164 — The Basin Is Short
**The sixteenth of the first month, MR 416, the Rainmarket low ground, first to seventh bell.** The flood bank is a foot up and has about a mile and a bit to go, the ground still takes a wheel and will not much longer. **Eleven gangs and a board on a trestle with eleven witness names on it, which the district put there because a works board takes eleven, and the number of people whose attestation was of record in this basin and had been produced is not eleven — there are about nine.** **Seren Calder, forty, a settlement marshal**, comes down the face of the bank with a coat that has been in a hedge, takes a glove off and puts her bare hand flat on the clay, and says **the clay is good** first. The bank cannot be raised because witnessing is a person of record standing in a place on a day and the people of record are nine and the nine have been produced. **Her argument, in her order and unsoftened: she is going to treat a refusal in a public emergency as endangerment** — if a person of record is asked to stand in a place on a day and says no, and the consequence of the no is that a bank does not go up, that person is endangering the district, and she has the instrument and has used it twice in nineteen years and **both times she was right about the water**. **Kade's answer is the true technical one and is accepted and does not help:** nine signatures in a queue in Calder's own voice, none of them consenting, and no way afterwards to know which, and in about four years a man in a good coat produces it and the finding is that the name behind the paper is the name in the witness box. **Marek puts the record loan on the bank** — the third month of MR 415, a standing authority, correctly, a price of *fourteen attestations*, and the only reason there is no rate on this low ground this season — **and says the figure on it before she asks: he has no paper, no standing, no office, and no room will take his word.** Calder declines to act on it and says so, **and says out loud on the bank, in front of three witnesses, that she knew there was a loan sealed at Verrow in the third month and did not go and read the price box and has not got a good reason after a week.** Then: what would you have instead. His answer is a room, a question, an answer written down. **Hers is the volume's argument in a marshal's mouth: that is a form — and the box gets filled in**, not by a villain but by the person at the window with the list at the eighth bell, nine or ten deep, in a good voice, and the entry will be true and false and nothing in the world will show which. Kade writes in her own column and not in his; **his column has had nothing in it since the last night of the ninth month.** Calder will be on the bank on the eighteenth, nineteenth and twentieth with a list of eleven names, and will write a short true line about him if he is not findable.

### Chapter 165 — What a Marshal Can Do With a Form
**The seventeenth, then the eighteenth, MR 416, the room over the dye house, from the second bell.** **The boy's message on the seventeenth is what brings her: somebody had said that a settlement marshal for the reach was asking after a room with no name on the door. CORRECTED at the batch-0002 review — the message was planted in this chapter and never referred to again, and it now pays.** Calder comes up the ladder, stands on the other side of the rail, and asks three questions and writes nothing down, because a marshal's questions are not evidence. **None of the four people in the room is in a book an office could open** — a carter's boy of nineteen, a dyer of about fifty who is in a book in a different city, a man of about forty who came in the ninth month of MR 415 because he had nowhere to be stood, and Zlata. Then the door, and it is real: **a settlement in a public emergency may accept a witnessing held in a room the marshal has been in** — and behind it the wall, because **a surety is a person and a person is named and the naming is done by an office out of a book, and there is no book and no form on which an office may make one, and she has asked for a roll for this ground twice in four days and been told both times that there is no district, and both men were correct.** Therefore **any settlement she seals on anything witnessed in this room is void on the face of it, and void has to be sealed again, and there are about four days left in which the ground will take a wheel.** And the sentence the volume turns on: **this room is not dangerous, this room is useless, and useless is worse than dangerous, because a dangerous room gets closed and a useless room keeps four people standing at the top of a ladder being useful to each other.** Nobody is blamed and nobody is thanked for anything. She asks Marek why he is not a surety; he says he is a spent witness; she asks him to say the last part again; he refuses; she writes it anyway **on the back of a printed notice about a standpipe, with a pencil stub, and puts the day in** — *spent witness, no surety, standing refused* — and gives it to him, and tells him what it is: **every person in this basin can put a month in a box, and she can put a day on a piece of paper, and in about four years a man in a good coat will produce that paper and it will say a true thing and nothing can take it off the book, and he asked for it.** She did not do him a favour. Then the offer, said once: **if anybody in this basin finds a way to seal a settlement in four days on sureties an office can name, she will seal it on the standing authority and will not read the price box and will not read it whatever is in it** — and she has said the opposite in four rooms in four days and meant it every time and would take it back now. **And she restates the three days she named on the bank — the reach at the sixth bell on the nineteenth and on the twentieth, with a list of eleven names — and gives the reason she is giving it, which is that he will not have to spend a day working out whether he can be found. CORRECTED at the batch-0002 review: those two days were a promise the batch never kept. This is the first time in the batch that findability is something a person arranges for himself, and Movement III owns the nineteenth and the twentieth.** At the foot of the ladder she asks for the verb for what she has done, for her file at the fourth bell, because if it is his word then she has taken his word and spent it. Zlata does not answer and says so. He cannot find which way the towpath is, because a man put down a ladder gets no direction, and there are about four ways out of that lane and every one looks the same at the foot of a wall.

### Chapter 166 — Two People Who Need One
**The twenty-third of the first month, MR 416, the low-ground road, second to fourth bell.** There is nowhere to put a piece of paper, and a person standing up in the open on a market road is a person in a queue, so **Marek and Kade carry a deal table out of a room behind a chandler's and set it down outside a shut pitch with a canopy frame and no canvas.** **Odile Marn** needs a witness for the Counters' Walk works board, of which line four is the ditch: the ditch has not been cut since the rains of the year before last, she asked for it in the ninth month of last year and got a fortnight on the ground behind her wall and no ditch, and **she is spent**, produced in the sixth month of last year, and found out in a rail queue because somebody counted the queue and wrote a number on a slate. **Margit Sova** needs a witness for the *holding of a pitch*, which is a different thing from a lease, and there is no form for the holding of a pitch, and a woman who cannot be a witness cannot hold a pitch and has no way of saying so that anybody has to do anything about. **Neither can be the other's witness, and they each refuse the other for the same reason in different words: they know exactly what being spent is, and they will not do it to each other.** **Kade offers — she has not been produced, and there is a sheet in a flat case in Bell Stair with her name and his at the head of two columns and a rule that neither may write in the other's — and both of them refuse her**, Margit because she would be spending her on a market road in front of four people, Odile because Marek's offer is not a body in a room but a form with a box in it. And the turn: **what they each need is not a witness but a form that says no** — one sheet, her name, one word, *not* — and there is no such form in this kingdom and neither the woman of fifty-one at the counter nor the clerk up the river was lying to him. Margit then puts the box on the table unasked and **reads nine lines of twenty-six years of what a pitch owes out loud in the open, having never read it to anybody**, and it is not a list of names and there is no head at the top of any page and there is no total in it, and it is not evidence, and she does it because a table is the first thing anybody has put in front of her since the sixth of the month last year that was not a form. **Nobody asks her what the column is for and nobody offers to head it.** They carry the table back. **The ditch is not cut and the pitch is not held and two women who had never spoken know each other's names by the fourth bell, which is four hours, and neither of them said a word about it.**

### Chapter 167 — The Wall
**The twenty-seventh of the first month, MR 416, a lane in the district, about two hours.** Nine feet of brick, and a day is chalked against the top of it, and the wall goes down. **Margit Sova writes it**, with a lump of chalk off a kerb, and the reason is given once and will be said again in the same words if she is asked twice: **there is a woman on that wall who cannot write** — Borka, about sixty-one, nine years in the lane, in it before there was a lane — and a child of about nine writes her name in chalk while she watches and does not say it was wrong. Nobody gave her money and there was no money. **Zlata Hruska says what it is before anybody asks: a rota of days, a day against every name — and do not put it together with the rota of hours at the shed in Tallow Ward, because a man in a good coat could take a line off that one in a moment and there is no line on this wall anybody can take off, the whole of it is chalk and the whole of it can be rubbed out with one sleeve.** And: **this is not a column with nothing at the top of it, and the reason it is not is that a day is written against every single line, so anybody can walk along the wall and say *that is the twenty-ninth* and be right or be wrong about it in front of the other nine.** **Zvonko Maras says no, twice**, and gives the reason: he has had that roof since he was twenty-two, he does not know what he is standing behind or what it cost, and **if his name is not on the wall there is one less thing to argue with** — and his no is on the wall in the only way a no ever gets on a wall, which is by not being on it. **Drazen Topic puts his own name on it**, because a man who counts ought to be counted. **Jelka Sondor went to the coast on the second of the month and has not come back and nobody knew she was on it at all.** **Odile Marn asks to be on it and says why out loud: being on this wall is the opposite of being a witness, and she would rather be wrong on a wall than right in a queue.** Ten names, about six days, three of them Nim Rook's and three the women in the lane settled between themselves. **And the hand: by the tenth name the letters have gone from a tradeswoman's hand to a different thing, and it is not that they are less careful, it is what a hand does over twenty-six years.** Margit says it out loud in the lane instead of finding out in a room: **that is the end of what my hand does, the next one is not going to be mine, and there is no form on which anybody may be told that they cannot hold a piece of chalk.** Nobody wrote anything down. **The wall is not a remedy, it does not un-spend anybody, and about nine people in this basin are spent, and four of them cannot be found and one of the four is dead, and none of that is on a wall.**

### Chapter 168 — A Finding in a Room That Does Not Exist
**The second of the second month, MR 416, the room over the dye house, fourth bell to the seventh.** Four unmatched chairs from four kitchens in a rough square, the lamp in the middle, the fifth person on the bed. **There is no form anywhere in this kingdom on which two or more people may sit in a room and make a finding** — no office, no chair, no minute, no seal, no stipend, no member list, and no name for the lane, no roll, no book anybody there has been inside, and therefore no place the finding could be sent and nobody it could be produced at. **Nim Rook says that last part out loud before anything else and is told not to be sorry for it.** Then Zlata asks the room to ask her whether she has been produced. **She has, twice — a sheet in the sixth month of MR 414 about a bin behind a dyeworks, and a sheet in the second month of last year about the same bin in front of nine or ten people — and neither asked her anything and nobody told her.** She says *Thank you* in a completely flat voice, trims the wick, and says she is not going to be grateful, and says she wants it written down that she said so, because a person who has been grateful in that lane gets a form afterwards. **The asking is Neris Kade's — Zlata asks the room to ask her, and Kade asks it, and it is Kade who supplies the two sheets, the sixth month of MR 414 and the second month of last year, both about a bin, and it is Kade who says *give me the rest of it in one piece, because you have been in four rooms since the fourth of last month*.** Four findings go on the inside of a door-shutter with a carpenter's pencil: **any person may ask whether their name is on the wall and the answer will be given; the answer cannot be made to hold and the room knows that and is going on giving it anyway; the person who keeps the room has been produced twice and was not told, and she is the first person in this basin to be told the whole of it in one room in one go, and two others in this basin know they have been produced and neither of them has been asked about it in this room; and this room has no power to do anything whatever with any of the three, and is not going to pretend otherwise, and is not a court and will not be called one.** Then **Lovro Prazak, forty-nine, who carries fish up from the low ground and sleeps in a doorway at the end of that lane, comes up the ladder with wet hands** — his name is on the twenty-ninth in pencil under the chalk, put there by somebody who has gone, and he never gave it to anybody in that lane. Zlata rubs it off herself with a wet sleeve, **which is the only thing that has ever actually been done in that room.** And then the other half of it, which is the batch's: **a name is a name against a thing; his is worth about a foot of brick; and the where anybody in that lane can give for themselves without being invented is *a doorway at the top of this lane, and no other address, and no name on any map* — and that is why the lane has no name, and it is not because anybody has been cruel about it, there is nothing to be cruel about, there is no where.** Zlata states the whole of what the room is: the answer is given, the answer is worth nothing outside the room, **and a man in a good coat came up that ladder in the fourth month of last year and she let him stand on the other side of the rail while he told her what a room was for.** The fourth finding is written on the **outside** of the same door as well, deliberately, because the door is going to be painted in about two months and no form anywhere can be used to ask anybody to paint over a finding or not to. **There is no copy, no book and no third copy anywhere in the kingdom, and the only copy is a second coat of pencil on the other side of a board about four feet by two.** Nim comes down the ladder and says the Deputy was right about the two nines and that it is the worst thing anybody has ever done to her and she is going to be angry about it for a year.

### Chapter 169 — The Fourth Line
**The ninth of the second month, MR 416, Tallow Ward, the shed at the Gate, from the second bell.** The shed is shut and there is a notice on it that says what the room is and does not say a Crown March office, put up in a week when the yard voted four minutes on something and would not say afterwards what. **Ruzena Kvas, sixty-one, who has kept the wall list for as long as there has been a wall to read**, lets him in and does not ask who is at the door. **The list is about forty lines on the far wall and the hand has got worse going down and at the end of the fourth line there is a column with nothing at the head of it, bare since the second week of the seed month of MR 414 — and he looks at it for a moment, says nothing, would not, and is not going to ask.** She reads the whole list from the top at the ordinary speed in the voice of a woman reading a price list, **and the fourth line comes out in the same voice as the other thirty-nine**, between a line about a boiler in the second month of last year and a line about a handcart in the third month of the year before that, and then she reads all forty again and reads the fourth line a second time and stops for the length of a breath, **and the silence after the fourth line is about the length of a line.** *Neris Kade. A roof over a counting floor in Bell Stair. The growth month of MR 412. Eleven weeks.* **Kade is in the shed and is not asked, and this is the chapter.** Not by Ruzena, who could have said *is that you* in four words; not by a yard-man at the fifth bell; not by a boy with a pail who stopped at the wall because everybody stops at that wall. **Marek's hand goes into his coat and comes out — the fifth time in two months — and Kade sees him do it and does not use it.** She says she is not going to be asked what the line is, and gives the reason at length: **if he asks, it becomes a conversation between two people about a paper, and there is a moment in which she would answer truthfully, and in about four years a man in a good coat could ask her about the paper on the wall and she would have to say *we spoke about it once on the ninth of the second month*, and there would be nine words on a sheet in a file in Bell Stair that she had never seen, and it would be the only document in this business she had made herself. And: she has not been asked that about anything in four years, she noticed it in the fourth year, she decided to let it keep happening, **and it has cost her a reading of her own name a week for four years.** And then the other half, out loud, in the shed: **you did not know what the fourth line was, and you have been reading this yard's papers out loud for years, and nobody in this yard has ever asked you what the wall is, and if anybody asks me today I am going to walk out and stand at the tank.** Nobody asked her anything for the rest of the morning. Then **Drazen Topic at the market rail**: Marek tells him he is the second of nine he has found who can be told, that five of the nine can be found and four cannot and one of the four is dead, **that there is no form on which anybody may be entered as saying they were free and that a woman of fifty-one at a counter has been entering other people's words for thirty years and has never once been asked whether the person was free**, and that the finding of the fourth of last month says a room cannot enter a finding that a person did or did not consent to anything and that there is none to enter. **And the other half: nothing is going to happen to him because nobody is going to be able to do anything to him either.** Topic counts nine hundred and about forty, puts the number up, and says he is not going to start thanking the man at the third window on the strength of nine words on a market road. **The yard did not thank anybody, the register did not gain a line, the rule was not extended, nobody put a head on anything, and the reading will be done again on the sixteenth, the twenty-third and the thirtieth.** **CORRECTED at the batch-0002 review: the chapter previously gave Marek *a year and a half* at the shed twice, sitting two lines under the anchored date of the bare column, and gave Perpetua Ohl nine years of saying a sentence that is Zlata Hruska's and thirty years at her counter. The span is out of the prose and Ohl's line now says what is true of her. Neither a derived span nor a transferred figure may be left standing in this batch's prose.**

### Chapter 170 — The Coercion Is Not in the Signature
**The sixteenth of the second month, MR 416, the room over the dye house, second lamp to the seventh bell.** The room's standing business is one word, *instead*: **what do we do instead.** The deal table is carried up the ladder on the fourteenth, half sideways, by two men and a boy, and is not in any book. **Four go on it in the order they were signed: Zvonko Maras's roof over a coal bay (second month of MR 410) · Odile Marn's boundary ditch (growth month of MR 411) · Jelka Sondor's bin behind a chandler's (growth month of MR 412), brought down from a coast in a carter's coat by a woman at the coast who did not say a word and did not want a receipt · Drazen Topic's boundary between two market pitches (sixth month of MR 413), a copy of a page taken out of a drawer at Verrow on the eleventh by a man with no office who was neither refused nor served, because a person of record may have a copy of a page and there is a form for that one. These are Ohl's four from Ch 163 and nobody else's, and Odile Marn says they have to be the four or it is not a finding. CORRECTED at the batch-0002 review: this summary previously put Margit Sova in Topic's place, which broke the locked four, broke Sova's refusal in Ch 166, and read as a draft substitution. The prose is corrected and so is this.** Maras says the first thing and it is the first half: **that is a good piece of paper, a well-made thing, and there is not one mark on any of the four a man could point at and say he forged it** — Zlata: nobody ever wrote the second half down, and the second half is that it does not matter. They go at it in four steps. None of them names a paper: **a roof, a ditch, a bin, a boundary.** Every one carries a month and not a day. **And third: there is nothing at all, anywhere, on any of the four, for whether the person was free.** Then the man who reads papers out loud, in the wrong order: **the fifth line was the paper and it was cut out of the forme nineteen years ago to save a quarter of a sheet on about four hundred copies; the freedom was never on the form at all, not on the four-line printing and not on the five-line printing and not on any printing anybody in this kingdom has ever had; a cut line can be put back and a line that was never in type cannot be put back by anybody, it has to be printed for the first time, and a new form is a thing a man in a good coat can produce at anybody in about four years.** Then Maras says *so put a box in it* — a new form, a box, a hand that fills it in — **and Zlata agrees, and gives the reason she took nine years and the room took a moment: the person who would fill it in. Not a villain. A man at a window, with a list, at the eighth bell, nine or ten deep, in a good voice, saying the same five words since the fourth month. He would fill it in *free* nine times in a morning because the form requires him to ask, and the entry would be true and false and nothing in the world would show which, because a page does not know the difference between a man who asks because he wants to know and a man who asks because there is a line on a sheet in front of him. That is what happened to nine people, and to her twice, about a bin, and nobody ever has to put a hand on the paper.** The finding goes on the inside of the door in a third coat of pencil and Kade reads it out twice at the ordinary speed. **That there is no form in this kingdom on which a person may be entered as saying they were free when they signed anything, and that there has never been one, and that a box for it would be filled in by the person whose not asking has done the harm. That therefore the harm is not in the signatures and never was, and the coercion is a queue with a man at it, and a queue is not on any form, and there is no box for a queue, and there is not going to be one. That this finding changes nothing** — four good sheets stay four good sheets, the ditch is not cut, the pitch is not held, four of the spent cannot be found, one of those is dead, and **of the nine who have been produced this room has told two and cannot tell the rest.** **Zlata says: which means the wall** — and nine days, and a piece of chalk, and a woman of forty-one who has got to the end of her hand, and a question asked out loud by somebody who is not an institution in a room where nothing is proved. Then a boy of nine comes up the ladder with news, from a carter, from a man in a lane, **that a four-line form is being set in a shop off the copper road that is not on Ropewalk, and nine hundred are going out, and nobody in that shop will say who asked.** Nobody in the room can do one thing about it: no form on which anybody may be told to stop a printer, no office whose business it is, **the Trust's seat on the board of five empty seven times**, and the person who would stop it would have to be a person with a name at the top of something and there is not one in this basin. Zlata says, flatly, not a question: **a form with a line on it is a thing a printer sets.** Nobody answers and she does not ask twice. **The lamp goes out at the seventh bell, nine people go down a ladder into a lane with no name, and the last of them comes down with both hands on the rungs because there was nothing in either of them. And on the brick there are ten names in chalk in a hand that has got worse over twenty-six years, the tenth of them is the worst one on the wall, and there is no eleventh, and the queue is still at the window, and the wall is the only thing in the basin that nobody owns.**
